# Nightwing — Decisions Log

Architectural decisions, newest first. Each entry: date, decision, rationale, alternatives considered.

---

### 2026-08-31 (9) — Zugzwang-aware search shaping: RookEndgame-only flag, a reduced (not skipped) null-move probe, and an accepted, justified node-count regression in flagged positions

**Decision:** `eval::is_zugzwang_prone(EndgameSignature)` (`src/eval/endgame.h`) flags ONLY `EndgameSignature::RookEndgame`, out of all seven buckets. `search/search.cpp`'s negamax() NMP block, on a flagged node, reduces the null-move probe's own reduction amount `R` by `kZugzwangReductionDecrease` (floored at `kZugzwangMinReduction`) rather than skipping null-move pruning outright the way the pre-existing `non_pawn_material == 0` guard does for KPK. This is a deliberate, accepted, ARCHITECTURE.md Testing-Policy-flagged node-count regression in exactly the positions flagged: verified empirically (see below) rather than assumed.

**Rationale for RookEndgame only:** Rook endings are the single most consistently, classically cited zugzwang-prone endgame type in chess literature — CPW's own material on zugzwang leads with rook-ending examples specifically. Other buckets were considered and left unflagged for defensible, bucket-specific reasons: `KPK` is already handled by the pre-existing, STRONGER `non_pawn_material == 0` guard (null-move pruning is actually unsound there, not merely riskier, so an outright skip is the correct response, not a bias — see `is_zugzwang_prone()`'s own doc comment for why flagging KPK here too would be harmless but redundant); `KRK`/`KBNK` are forced-win positions against a bare king where the stronger side passing doesn't create the kind of false-cutoff risk zugzwang describes; `KBPK`, `OppositeColoredBishops`, and `KnightVsBishop` are plausible candidates for a future extension of this flag, but none has RookEndgame's own overwhelming, specific citation in the literature, and guessing at several buckets' zugzwang-proneness without individually strong justification for each risked diluting this first cut's own defensibility. Narrower-but-justified was preferred over broader-but-guessed, the same posture this session's other Phase 6 scope decisions (Vancura deferral, KPK's direct-opposition-only scope) already took.

**Rationale for reducing R rather than skipping outright:** Skipping null-move pruning entirely in RookEndgame positions would be the blunter, more conservative choice, but rook endings are common enough, and null-move pruning valuable enough as a technique, that giving up the optimization completely across an entire common endgame category didn't seem proportionate to a real-but-not-certain risk (unlike KPK, where the risk is a proven unsoundness, not just a statistical tendency). A smaller `R` keeps the technique active while searching closer to what a real move's own child would see, directly addressing the actual failure mode zugzwang creates for null-move pruning (a probe too shallow to notice that passing was uniquely good) without discarding the pruning's benefit everywhere else in that endgame type.

**Empirical verification (not merely assumed):** A real RookEndgame FEN (`2r1k3/pp3ppp/8/8/8/8/PP3PPP/2R1K3 b - - 0 1`) was searched at depths 4–8 with two builds — this session's actual change, and a control build with the bias's own `if` block short-circuited to a no-op, everything else identical. Node counts were higher with the bias active at every depth tested (e.g. 240 vs. 220 at depth 4, confirming the mechanism engages and searches more thoroughly, as intended), while the best score returned was IDENTICAL at every depth between the two builds (confirming no correctness regression from adding the bias). This matches ARCHITECTURE.md's own Testing Policy requirement to track node-count changes in DECISIONS.md when they occur — this is exactly that tracked change, and it's a deliberate, justified one: more nodes in exchange for a smaller-but-real zugzwang-blindness risk in a well-established zugzwang-prone endgame category, not an accidental regression.

**Alternatives considered:**
- *Flag every bucket that seemed plausibly zugzwang-relevant (KBPK, OppositeColoredBishops, KnightVsBishop, RookEndgame):* rejected for this first cut — see the RookEndgame-only rationale above; a future session can extend `is_zugzwang_prone()`'s own switch with individual justification per additional bucket, the same way this file's own KBPK bucket (2026-08-31 (7)) was added on its own specific justification rather than speculatively bundled into an earlier session.
- *Skip null-move pruning outright in RookEndgame positions, mirroring the existing KPK guard exactly:* rejected — see the "reducing R rather than skipping outright" rationale above; treating a statistically-elevated-risk bucket identically to a provably-unsound one overcorrects.
- *A raised `kNullMoveMinDepth` for flagged positions instead of (or in addition to) a smaller `R`:* considered, not implemented this session -- a single, clearly-testable mechanism was preferred over stacking two independent levers with only one (the R decrease) empirically verified end-to-end in this entry; a depth-threshold change remains a plausible future refinement if the single-lever approach turns out insufficient once this project has real self-play or `bench` data to check it against (ARCHITECTURE.md's Phase 8 Benchmarking Discipline).

### 2026-08-31 (8) — Fortress pattern detection deliberately does NOT consult classify_endgame(); proportional discount, not a binary/zeroing verdict

**Decision:** `eval::fortress_value()` (`src/eval/fortress.h`/`.cpp`), ROADMAP.md's "Fortress pattern detection" item, does not call `eval::classify_endgame()` at all -- unlike the three Phase 6 terms built in Sessions 65–66 (King+pawn theory, Rook endgame patterns, Minor piece endgames), each gated on one or more specific `EndgameSignature` buckets. It instead works directly from four structural checks against `pos` (no queens; at most `kFortressMaxNonPawnPieces` non-pawn pieces combined; a nonzero material lead; at least `kFortressMinBlockedPawns` mutually-blocked pawns), applying a PROPORTIONAL discount (half the material lead for `eg`, a quarter for `mg`) to whichever side is ahead -- never zeroing the lead out entirely, and never flipping its sign.

**Rationale:** The item's own ROADMAP.md wording -- "structural, not tabulated" -- is itself the reason this doesn't fit the classifier's bucket model. Every one of `classify_endgame()`'s seven buckets is a specific, enumerable material shape (KPK, KRK, KBNK, RookEndgame, OppositeColoredBishops, KnightVsBishop, KBPK). A genuine fortress can arise from many DIFFERENT material shapes none of those buckets specifically name -- e.g. a blocked rook-and-several-pawns position, or a blocked minor-piece-and-pawns position with pieces on both sides -- and enumerating "fortress-shaped material buckets" the same way the other six were enumerated would mean an unboundedly growing list, the opposite of what "structural, not tabulated" is asking for. Applying a proportional discount rather than a stronger binary verdict (a hard win/draw call, the way eval/king_pawn_endgame.cpp's own Rule-of-the-Square check makes one) reflects that general fortress recognition is a genuinely much harder, less certain problem than any of those three earlier terms' own exactly-defined classical patterns -- CPW itself does not offer a closed-form "is this a fortress" test the way it does for the Rule of the Square or for Key Squares, because none is generally known. A confident, provably-correct fortress detector is an open problem in computer chess even for engines far stronger than this one; a modest, clearly-labeled-as-approximate discount is honest about that limitation, where a hard zero would overclaim certainty this term cannot actually back up.

**Alternatives considered:**
- *Add classifier buckets for the most common fortress material shapes (e.g. "rook vs. bishop, many pawns, all on one wing"), the same pattern the previous three terms used:* rejected — this is exactly the "tabulated" approach the ROADMAP.md item's own wording explicitly rules out, and each new special-cased bucket would only cover its own narrow shape, missing the broader structural principle (blocked pawns + material lead + few remaining pieces) that actually drives fortress-like drawishness across many different material combinations.
- *A hard zero-out (treat any matching position as a dead draw) instead of a proportional discount:* rejected — unlike KPK's Rule of the Square or Rook endgame patterns' Lucena/Philidor recognition, none of this term's four structural checks together amount to a proof the position is actually a fortress; a hard zero would be a confident claim this heuristic has no real basis for, and would be actively wrong (and costly, in a real game) whenever the checks fire on a position that is, in fact, still winning with correct technique.

### 2026-08-31 (7) — Added `EndgameSignature::KBPK`, a bucket the original Session 64 classifier turned out to be missing

**Decision:** Added a seventh bucket, `EndgameSignature::KBPK`, to `eval::classify_endgame()` (`src/eval/endgame.h`/`.cpp`) — one side has exactly one bishop and at least one pawn, the other side is completely bare (no pawns, no pieces at all), zero knights/rooks/queens anywhere. Classification is piece-counts-only; it does NOT check pawn file, pawn count beyond "at least one," or bishop-vs-corner-square color — those judgments belong to the bucket's consumer (`eval::minor_piece_endgame_value()`, `src/eval/minor_piece_endgame.h`/`.cpp`, this same session), matching the "classification only, judgment later" split every other bucket already follows.

**Rationale:** ROADMAP.md's "Minor piece endgames" item explicitly names "wrong-bishop-corner draw detection" as one of its three clauses, but the original six-bucket classifier from Session 64 had no bucket covering this material configuration at all — a genuine gap, not a deliberate scope decision, discovered while starting this session's work. Notably, this gap was already anticipated in writing: `endgame.cpp`'s own `is_light_square()` helper, written in Session 64, carried a comment saying it might need promoting to a shared header "if a later Phase 6 item (e.g. a future 'wrong bishop corner' signature) needs it more broadly" — Session 64 correctly foresaw the shape of this gap without yet filling it in. Extending a previously-"complete" file (`endgame.h`/`.cpp`, together with their own test file) is consistent with this project's general practice of fixing found gaps rather than working around them — the same posture Phase 5's own material-value literal-constant fix took for a comparable situation.

**Alternatives considered:**
- *Detect the wrong-bishop-corner pattern without a classifier bucket, inline inside `minor_piece_endgame.cpp` using raw piece-count checks against `pos` directly:* rejected — this would leave `eval::classify_endgame()`'s own header comment (which frames itself as "the" single source of truth for endgame material-bucket detection) silently incomplete for a case ROADMAP.md explicitly names, and would mean a second, parallel place doing the same kind of piece-counting classify_endgame() already exists to centralize.
- *Defer wrong-bishop-corner entirely, the same way Vancura position recognition was deferred in Session 65 (see the 2026-08-31 (6) entry above):* rejected as unnecessary here — unlike Vancura's own genuinely different, harder-to-verify geometry, wrong-bishop-corner recognition needed only a straightforward piece-count bucket plus a reuse of the already-verified rule-of-the-square technique (eval/king_pawn_endgame.cpp), not a new, unverified pattern-matching approach. Deferral is for cases that are genuinely harder or riskier to get right, not a default response to "the classifier doesn't have a bucket for this yet."

### 2026-08-31 (6) — Rook endgame patterns: Vancura position recognition deferred; Lucena/Philidor narrowed to the single-pawn case; Tarrasch's Rule applies generally

**Decision:** `eval::rook_endgame_value()` (`src/eval/rook_endgame.h`/`.cpp`) implements three of ROADMAP.md's four named rook-endgame patterns — Tarrasch's Rule (rook behind a passed pawn, own or enemy), Lucena position recognition, and Philidor position recognition — as genuine geometric formulas, applying whenever `classify_endgame()` returns `EndgameSignature::RookEndgame`. Two scope decisions:

1. Vancura position recognition is NOT implemented in this session, despite being one of the four patterns this ROADMAP.md item names.
2. Lucena and Philidor recognition are further narrowed, beyond `EndgameSignature::RookEndgame`'s own broad "any pawn count" bucket, to positions with exactly one pawn total on the board — the specific "rook + pawn vs. rook" scenario both patterns are classically about. Tarrasch's Rule carries no such narrowing — it's a per-pawn, per-rook geometric check that stays well-defined at any pawn count.

**Rationale:** Vancura's own recognition criteria (a pawn on a specific non-rook file, a defending rook giving lateral checks from a particular file relative to the pawn, a defending king positioned to attack the pawn from the side) are a materially different geometric pattern from both Lucena's and Philidor's, not a small variation on either — encoding it correctly in the same session, without the same level of independent verification (Python-model cross-check plus a real compiled driver, the process used for every other pattern in this file and in `eval/king_pawn_endgame.cpp`) risked shipping a plausible-looking but wrong recognizer, which is worse for playing strength than simply not having Vancura recognition yet. This mirrors the same posture the 2026-08-31 (5) entry above already took for KPK's distant/diagonal opposition: a documented, honest partial scope beats a rushed, unverified full one. Narrowing Lucena/Philidor to the single-pawn case follows the same "narrowest version the item's own wording actually asks for" precedent `eval/material_imbalance.h`'s and `eval/king_pawn_endgame.h`'s own header comments already establish — a general multi-pawn rook endgame is a substantially harder, less geometrically clean-cut recognition problem neither classical pattern's own textbook framing (CPW's own "Lucena Position"/"Philidor Position" articles) actually addresses.

**Alternatives considered:**
- *Implement a best-effort Vancura recognizer anyway, even if less rigorously verified than the other patterns:* rejected — an eval term that fires on real positions with a plausible-but-wrong sign is worse than one that simply doesn't fire; ROADMAP.md's own checkbox for this item documents the deferral honestly rather than silently shipping a weaker, unflagged version of "done."
- *Apply Lucena/Philidor's geometric checks to any pawn count, not just the single-pawn case:* rejected — with several pawns on the board, "the pawn" that Lucena/Philidor's own king/rook geometry is about becomes ambiguous (which pawn's promotion square, if several are advanced?), and the classical technique itself assumes the simplified single-extra-pawn scenario; applying the same formula to a multi-pawn position would frequently misfire.

---

### 2026-08-31 (5) — King+pawn (KPK) endgame theory: scope limited to direct opposition only, and to three specific classical techniques, not a full solver

**Decision:** `eval::king_pawn_endgame_value()` (`src/eval/king_pawn_endgame.h`/`.cpp`) implements exactly three classical KPK techniques — Rule of the Square, Key Squares, and direct Opposition — as genuine algorithmic formulas over the pawn's and both kings' actual squares, applying only to positions `eval::classify_endgame()` recognizes as `EndgameSignature::KPK` (king + exactly one pawn, either side, vs. bare king). Two deliberate scope limits:

1. Only DIRECT opposition (same file/rank, one square between) is checked. Distant opposition, diagonal opposition, and the fuller "corresponding squares" theory ROADMAP.md's own "King+pawn theory" item wording also names are NOT attempted.
2. When the defending king catches the pawn (per the rule of the square) but neither a key square nor an opposition blockade resolves the outcome, the term applies no adjustment at all — it does not guess a direction.

**Rationale:** Corresponding-squares theory beyond direct opposition is genuinely research-level (systematic computation of corresponding squares for an arbitrary king+pawn configuration is most cleanly done by retrograde analysis over the specific position — precisely the tablebase approach this entire project has ruled out as a hard constraint). Direct opposition is the specific, common case actually reachable by a short, closed-form formula, and is what almost every human-readable KPK explanation (CPW's own "Opposition" article included) leads with. Declining to resolve the ambiguous remainder (case 2 above) rather than picking an arbitrary sign was preferred because a wrong static-eval nudge in a shallow, easily-searched subtree (two kings and one pawn — at most a handful of legal moves at any node) is worse than no nudge: search itself resolves these positions perfectly well without help, since the branching factor is tiny. This mirrors the same "decline to overreach past what the technique actually decides" posture eval/material_imbalance.h's own header comment already takes for unlisted material combinations.

**Alternatives considered:**
- *Full retrograde KPK solver, generated once at build time as a static table:* rejected outright — this is functionally a small tablebase, and NO tablebase dependency of any kind (however small, however built) is a hard project constraint, not a placeholder.
- *Implement distant/diagonal opposition and full corresponding squares now:* rejected for this session as materially higher complexity and risk of a subtly wrong formula (which would be worse than the current, narrower-but-verified scope) for a case classical search already handles fine on its own in this exact endgame's tiny branching factor. Left as a possible future refinement, not a checked-off "done, no further work" item — the ROADMAP.md item's checkbox note documents this scope limit explicitly rather than silently claiming full corresponding-squares coverage.
- *Guess a direction (small bonus or small penalty) for the unresolved fallthrough case rather than Score{}:* rejected — no classical technique actually justifies a sign either way there, and an unjustified nudge risks being wrong more often than it's right, unlike every other constant in this file, each grounded in a specific, named technique.

### 2026-08-31 (4bis) — `chebyshev_distance()` promoted from a local helper (`eval/king_tropism.cpp`) to a shared function (`board/bitboard.h`)

**Decision:** Moved `chebyshev_distance(Square, Square)` out of `eval/king_tropism.cpp`'s anonymous namespace and into `board/bitboard.h` as a `constexpr` free function, updating `king_tropism.cpp`'s own call site to use `board::chebyshev_distance()`.

**Rationale:** `king_tropism.cpp`'s own original header comment for this helper explicitly named its own revisit trigger: "promote to board/bitboard.h if a later Phase 6 item needs it more broadly." `eval::king_pawn_endgame_value()` (this session, see the entry above) is exactly that second, independent caller — it needs the identical king-to-square distance computation for the rule of the square. Promoting on the second real caller, not preemptively on the first, matches this codebase's established general precedent (`eval/king_safety.cpp`'s `shield_zone()` and `eval/space.cpp`'s `space_zone()` are both still local, single-caller helpers as of this session, deliberately not promoted, since neither has a second caller yet).

**Alternatives considered:**
- *Leave a second, separate local copy in `king_pawn_endgame.cpp`:* rejected — the whole point of that original header comment's own documented trigger was to avoid exactly this outcome once a second caller actually showed up.

## 2026-08-31 (4) — Endgame material-signature classifier built as a standalone, unwired unit (Phase 6's first item, classification half only)

**Decision:** Added `src/eval/endgame.h`/`.cpp` (`eval::EndgameSignature`, `eval::classify_endgame()`) — ARCHITECTURE.md's Module Layout already named this exact file pair and `tests/endgame_tests.cpp` in advance, so no path decision was needed, only the design inside it. `classify_endgame()` recognizes six buckets purely from piece counts and which side each piece belongs to (plus, for one bucket, bishop square color): `KPK`, `KRK`, `KBNK`, `RookEndgame`, `OppositeColoredBishops`, `KnightVsBishop`. Each maps directly to a specific clause in ROADMAP.md Phase 6's own remaining item wording — no speculative buckets beyond what those items already commit to. `classify_endgame()` is NOT called from `eval::evaluate()` or anywhere in `search/` yet.

**Rationale:** Phase 6's first item's own wording is two halves — "detect endgame material buckets at each node" (classification) and "route to specialized endgame reasoning when matched" (consuming that classification to actually change eval/search behavior). The second half has nothing to route TO yet: none of Phase 6's other items (K+P theory, rook endgame patterns, minor piece endgames, fortress detection, zugzwang-aware search shaping, KPK/KRK/KBNK exact play) exist yet, each being its own separate, unchecked ROADMAP item. Wiring `classify_endgame()` into `evaluate()` now, before any of those exist, would mean either leaving it called-but-unused (dead code with no observable effect, an eval-cache/performance cost for literally zero behavior change) or building a placeholder no-op "reasoning" step just to have something to route to — worse than not wiring it in at all, since a later session would have to identify and remove that placeholder rather than just adding real logic where the classifier currently has no consumer. This mirrors this codebase's own established precedent (`tuner/selfplay.h`, docs/DECISIONS.md's introducing entry there) of building and fully testing a self-contained module before its consumer exists, when the module's own correctness doesn't depend on that consumer.

**Two design decisions inside the classifier itself, worth recording:**
- **Same-side checks matter and can't be skipped uniformly.** For KPK and KRK, one total piece of a given type on the whole board automatically implies "vs. bare king" (nothing else exists to be on the other side). For KBNK, that shortcut breaks: one total bishop and one total knight on the board could mean either "one side has both" (KBNK, the intended hardest-basic-mate bucket) or "the two are split one-per-side" (a completely different, roughly balanced endgame). `classify_endgame()` explicitly checks which side each piece belongs to for KBNK/KnightVsBishop specifically, rather than applying the same total-count-only shortcut uniformly across every bucket. Caught by writing `tests/endgame_tests.cpp`'s own "split across sides is KnightVsBishop, NOT KBNK" test case before considering the function done, not by inspection — worth noting since it's exactly the kind of subtle case a total-count-only implementation would pass its own naive tests (a same-side-only test suite) while being wrong on this one.
- **Bucket granularity stops at what a later item's own wording distinguishes, not finer.** `RookEndgame` doesn't separately identify Lucena/Philidor/Vancura, and `OppositeColoredBishops`/`KnightVsBishop` don't yet cover "wrong bishop corner" (a narrower sub-case ROADMAP.md's own "Minor piece endgames" bullet also names) — each of those finer distinctions needs real positional judgment (pawn position, king position, promotion-square/bishop-color relationship) that belongs in each later item's own specialized-reasoning code once it's written, not pre-guessed into the classifier now. Matches eval/material_imbalance.h's own established "narrowest version the item's wording actually asks for" precedent, applied here to bucket granularity rather than a formula's own scope.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Wire `classify_endgame()` into `eval::evaluate()` immediately, even with no real specialized reasoning behind it yet (e.g., a switch statement whose every case is currently a no-op) — rejected per the rationale above: dead code with a to-do attached is worse than no code, since a later session has to notice and replace the placeholder rather than just adding to a clean extension point.
- Build ALL of Phase 6 in one large session (classifier + every consuming item at once) — rejected as far too large a single unit of work to review/test/commit together, and contrary to "Never skip a phase or task without explicit approval" / one-ROADMAP-item-at-a-time discipline this codebase has followed throughout every previous phase.
- A single flat `bool`-per-bucket struct instead of one `EndgameSignature` enum (a position could in principle match zero or one bucket, never more than one, given how the six are defined, so an enum's implicit "at most one" is a real, load-bearing property, not just a style choice, and was preferred over a struct that would have to enforce "at most one true" separately).

**Status:** Committed and tested (18 new test cases, `tests/endgame_tests.cpp`; full suite 343 cases / 52,867 assertions, all green). ROADMAP.md Phase 6's first item left UNCHECKED — see its own updated bullet text for why.

## 2026-08-31 (3) — Phase 5's tuning item closed with hand-set material defaults RETAINED, not the tuned weights

**Decision:** ROADMAP.md Phase 5's final item ("Tuned weights committed, before/after strength comparison logged") is closed without changing `eval/psqt.h` — the compiled-in defaults (pawn 100, knight 320, bishop 330, rook 500, queen 900, both phases) stay exactly as they were. No source file changed as part of this decision; this entry and ROADMAP.md's own updated checkbox/status line are the only artifacts of it.

**Rationale:** Two large-scale production `tuning-pipeline` runs now exist, the second with the (2) entry's pawn-anchoring bug fixed:

| | Session 61 (buggy tuner) | Session 62 (fixed tuner) |
|---|---|---|
| tuned pawn (mg/eg) | 21.4 / 81.1 (collapsed) | 100 / 100 (anchored, as designed) |
| match result | `score_a=0.5138, elo_diff=9.6` (defaults ahead) | `score_a=0.5088, elo_diff=6.1` (defaults ahead) |
| 1 SE on that Elo gap (400 games) | ~6.3 | ~6.55 |

Both runs, `score_a` is measuring `eval::default_material_weights()` (A) against that run's tuned weights (B) — A came out nominally ahead both times, though within noise both times (z≈1.5 and z≈0.9 respectively, neither exceeding the ~1.96 needed for 95% significance). The material finding here: fixing the tuner's known bug made the apparent gap SMALLER (9.6 → 6.1 Elo), not larger or unchanged — that's the direction a genuinely-near-zero true difference would push a noisy estimate as measurement quality improves, not the direction a real, drowned-out effect would push it. Combined with the two runs' tuned non-pawn weights landing close to each other (a soft but real "no wild disagreement" corroboration), the more defensible read of this data is "the hand-set defaults are already close to whatever this specific objective (depth-4 search, quiet self-play positions, material-only parameters, fixed `sigmoid_scale=400`) would converge to" rather than "a real improvement exists but remains statistically hidden."

Spending a third, larger-still run (e.g. 2000+ match games) to shrink that ~6.5 Elo error bar further was considered and rejected for now: the marginal information gained (resolving a ~6 Elo question with more precision) is small relative to the CI compute cost of another full production run, and ROADMAP.md's own item wording ("strength comparison logged," not "weights improved") is satisfied by two logged, methodologically-sound comparisons that agree with each other.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Transcribe the Session 62 tuned weights anyway, on the reasoning that "some tuning is better than none" even without a demonstrated strength gain — rejected; committing an eval change specifically because it was measured NOT to help would be backwards, and the current defaults are simpler, canonical round numbers with no downside relative to the tuned alternative.
- Run a much larger match (2000-5000 games) before deciding either way — rejected for now per the rationale above (diminishing information value for the compute cost); left as a legitimate option to revisit if a future session's own eval changes (e.g. extending the tunable parameter set beyond material) make re-testing material weights specifically worthwhile again.
- Treat this as inconclusive and leave the ROADMAP item open indefinitely pending "enough" games to reach significance — rejected; a difference this small (if it exists at all) is very unlikely to be worth chasing further given this codebase's current eval sophistication (only material weights are tunable so far — PSQT/mobility/king-safety/etc. all still fixed constants), and the item's own wording doesn't require the comparison to resolve to "tuning wins" to count as done.

**Status:** ROADMAP.md Phase 5 marked complete. `search/see.cpp`/`search/ordering.cpp` continuing to use unweighted material values (a pre-existing scope boundary, not something this decision touches) remains true and worth remembering if material tuning is ever revisited.

## 2026-08-31 (2) — Pawn value anchored (not tuned) in `tuner::tune()`, fixing a scale-degeneracy bug found in the first production tuning run

**Decision:** Added an `anchored` field to `MaterialParameterRef` (`src/tuner/tune.h`), set `true` for `pawn_mg`/`pawn_eg` only, `false` for the other eight `MaterialWeights` fields. `tune()` (`src/tuner/tune.cpp`) now skips both the finite-difference gradient probe and the update step entirely for any anchored parameter — it stays bit-identical to whatever `initial_weights` passed in for the whole run, not just arithmetically unchanged by a zero gradient. `nightwing_tune`'s stderr banner now states this explicitly at the start of every run.

**Rationale:** The (1) entry above's `tuning-pipeline` CI job was dispatched for real (5000 self-play games, 200 iterations, 400 match games) before this fix existed, and came back with two red flags together: `pawn_mg` collapsed from 100 to ~21.4 while every other piece only fell 10-20%, and the resulting weights scored `score_a=0.5138` (i.e. the OLD, untuned defaults nominally ahead, not the tuned weights) in the 400-game match — a difference of only ~9.6 Elo, well within that sample size's own ~±6.3 Elo (1 SE) noise band, so not even a real regression, just a wash. Both symptoms match the textbook Texel-tuning scale-degeneracy failure mode described in `tune.h`'s own header comment: since `sigmoid_scale` is a fixed constant here, not itself fit from the data, the loss surface has a flat direction along "scale the whole weight vector up or down together" that gradient descent can drift along for free, without the fit to real relative piece values actually improving. Anchoring the pawn is the standard fix (piece values are conventionally quoted "in pawns" for exactly this reason) and removes the flat direction entirely, at zero cost to the other nine parameters' own tunability.

Verified directly, not just argued for: re-ran the exact same self-play → tune pipeline at smoke-test scale (100 self-play games, 200 iterations) both before and after this fix. Before: `pawn_mg` moved to 1.81 in an earlier smoke test and 21.4 in the full production run — collapsing either way. After: `pawn_mg=100 pawn_eg=100` exactly, unmoved, while the other eight parameters still moved substantially (e.g. `knight_mg` 320 → 247.3), confirming the fix stops the specific degenerate drift without freezing the tuner entirely. Full test suite re-run after the change: 325 test cases / 52,849 assertions, all green (`tests/tune_tests.cpp` specifically: 36 test cases, including two new ones — a structural check that only `pawn_mg`/`pawn_eg` are marked `anchored`, and a behavioral check that pawn values stay exactly fixed even under training data engineered to give a strong pawn-devaluing gradient signal, while a non-anchored weight in that same run still moves).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Also fit `sigmoid_scale` from the data (Chess Programming Wiki's own two-step Texel's Tuning Method recipe: fit K first, then the weights) — the more "complete" fix, since it addresses the degeneracy without needing an arbitrary anchor point at all. Rejected for this session as a strictly larger change than the finding called for; left as a future refinement if pawn-anchoring alone turns out insufficient once PSQT/mobility/etc. terms are added to the tunable set later.
- Add an L2 regularization term pulling every weight back toward its starting value, instead of anchoring one outright — would also remove the flat direction, but adds a new hyperparameter (the regularization strength) that itself would need tuning/justifying, versus anchoring's zero extra parameters and a rationale (piece values are conventionally pawn-relative) that doesn't need empirical justification.
- Leave the tuner as-is and just discard this run's weights as a one-off bad result, rerunning unchanged — rejected: the root cause is structural (a genuine flat direction in the loss surface, not a fluke of this particular training corpus), so an unmodified rerun would very likely drift again and waste another CI run's worth of compute for another inconclusive result.

**Status:** Committed and tested; the actual production self-play/tune/match run has NOT yet been re-dispatched with this fix — that's the next action (ROADMAP.md, Phase 5's final item).

---

## 2026-08-31 (1) — Large-scale self-play/tune/match run moved to a `workflow_dispatch` CI job, not run by hand in a chat sandbox

**Decision:** Added a `tuning-pipeline` job to `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, gated on `workflow_dispatch` only (not `push`/`pull_request`), that builds `nightwing_selfplay`/`nightwing_tune`/`nightwing_match` (Release, `NIGHTWING_BUILD_TESTS=OFF`, Linux only) and runs the full self-play → tune → match pipeline in one job, uploading `training_data.txt`/`tuned_weights.txt`/`match_result.txt` as a build artifact (30-day retention). Six configurable `workflow_dispatch` inputs (`selfplay_games`, `selfplay_search_depth`, `selfplay_random_opening_plies`, `selfplay_max_plies`, `tune_iterations`, `match_games`) default to a genuinely large-scale run (5000 self-play games, 200 tuning iterations, 400 match games) rather than a smoke-test-sized one, matching ROADMAP.md Phase 5's own "thousands of games, not this session's 30-game smoke test" language. `base_seed` is fixed at 1 for both `nightwing_selfplay` and `nightwing_match` so a re-run with unchanged inputs is exactly reproducible.

**Rationale:** `src/tuner/selfplay_main.cpp`'s, `src/tuner/tune_main.cpp`'s, and `src/tuner/match_main.cpp`'s own header comments already documented the intended venue for a real large-scale run as "a GitHub Actions `workflow_dispatch` job," extending this project's existing "GitHub Actions handles ALL building" convention to "and ALL running of anything compute-heavy like this too" — but no such job actually existed in `ci.yml` yet, and Session 60's own "Next session start point" called for the actual large-scale run to happen. This session's own sandbox has per-command execution time limits (confirmed directly: a bare `--help` invocation of `nightwing_tune`/`nightwing_match` without piped stdin blocked on reading `std::cin` and hit the sandbox's command timeout) and no way to commit a real multi-thousand-game run's resulting artifacts back to the repo as part of a normal commit — a dispatchable CI job is both the documented intent and the only currently-workable venue for a run at the scale the roadmap actually calls for. Verified before shipping: cloned the current repo via `codeload.github.com`, built `nightwing_selfplay`/`nightwing_tune`/`nightwing_match` locally (Release, no tests), and ran the exact positional-argument sequences this job's `run:` step uses at small scale (5 self-play games → 20 tuning iterations → 4 match games) to confirm every CLI flag/argument order and the `nightwing_tune`-stdout-to-`nightwing_match`-stdin handoff format actually work as each tool's own header comment claims, before writing them into a CI YAML file that can't be locally dry-run the same way `ci.yml`'s existing jobs can (those get exercised on every push).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Run the large-scale pipeline by hand in this session's own sandbox anyway, accepting a much smaller game count than "thousands" to fit sandbox time limits — rejected; would produce a real result but not the scale ROADMAP.md's own item text calls for, and wouldn't be reproducible or reviewable the way a logged CI run with uploaded artifacts is.
- Gate the new job on `push`/`pull_request` like `build-and-test`, so it runs automatically — rejected; a multi-thousand-game tuning run is a deliberate, occasional data-generation step, not a per-commit correctness check, and running it on every push would burn CI minutes for no added signal (search is deterministic given a fixed seed, so nothing changes run-to-run unless eval/tuner code itself changes).
- Matrix this job across all 6 OS/build-type legs like `build-and-test` — rejected; search is deterministic given a seed, so the OS/build-type axis that matters for catching platform-specific *bugs* has no bearing on a strength-tuning run's own numeric output, making 6x the compute pure waste here.

**Not yet done:** The workflow itself still needs to actually be triggered (this requires a person with write access on GitHub — a chat sandbox has no ability to dispatch a workflow_dispatch run against the real repo) and its resulting artifacts reviewed. If the tuned weights come out ahead in the match comparison, they still need to be hand-transcribed into `eval/psqt.h`'s `kPawnValue`/etc. constants, with the before/after result logged in both this file and ROADMAP.md, to actually close out Phase 5's final item.

## 2026-08-30 (6) — Strength-comparison match harness (new tuner::match module), plus threading eval::MaterialWeights all the way through search as its prerequisite

**Decision:** Built the infrastructure for ROADMAP.md Phase 5's final item's "strength comparison" half. Two pieces: (1) threaded `eval::MaterialWeights` (2026-08-30 (5)) all the way through `search::negamax()`/`search::search_root()`/`search::quiescence()`/`quiescence_impl()` and the public `search::search_fixed_depth()`/`search::search_iterative_deepening()` APIs (`search.h`, `search.cpp`, `quiescence.h`, `quiescence.cpp`) — previously this override only reached `eval::evaluate()` directly (2026-08-30 (5)'s own scope), meaning a caller could get a different STATIC eval under different weights but couldn't actually have the SEARCH itself play differently, since every recursive `negamax()`/`quiescence()` call along the way had no way to carry the override down to its own leaf-level `eval::evaluate()` calls (razoring, futility, quiescence's stand-pat). (2) New `nightwing::tuner::match` module (`src/tuner/match.h`/`match.cpp`) — `play_match()` plays a configurable number of games between two `MaterialWeights` vectors using this newly-threaded `search_fixed_depth()`, alternating which one plays White each game, and `MatchResult` reports win/draw/loss counts plus `score_a()`/`elo_diff()` (the standard logistic score-to-Elo conversion). New `nightwing_match` executable (`src/tuner/match_main.cpp`) reads a tuned weight vector in `nightwing_tune`'s own output format from stdin and matches it against `eval::default_material_weights()`.

**Rationale for why the search-level threading was a genuine, not-yet-satisfied prerequisite, not just "more of the same mechanical work already done" (2026-08-30 (1)'s eval_cache threading, 2026-08-30 (5)'s eval::evaluate()-level material_weights):** `tuner::tune`'s own `compute_loss()` (2026-08-30 (5)) never needed search at all — it calls `eval::evaluate()` directly on a labeled position, exactly what Texel's Tuning Method wants (a raw static eval compared against a game result, not a searched score). A strength COMPARISON is a fundamentally different kind of measurement: it needs each side to actually PLAY a real game, move by move, which means the override has to reach every node's own static-eval calls inside the search tree, not just a single top-level call. This was a real gap 2026-08-30 (5)'s own scope (deliberately, and correctly) left unaddressed — that entry's own "Alternatives considered" section never claimed search-level threading was already done, and this session's own attempt to write `play_match()` without it would have meant every game, regardless of which weight vector was nominally "playing," actually searched under whatever weights (or lack thereof) were hardcoded into `search_fixed_depth()`'s own default path — silently measuring nothing meaningful.

**A real, important scope boundary discovered and deliberately left as-is, not silently glossed over:** `search/see.cpp`'s SEE (Static Exchange Evaluation) and `search/ordering.cpp`'s MVV-LVA move ordering BOTH call `eval::material_value()` without any weights override — confirmed by direct inspection of both files during this session, not assumed. This means a match's two sides still share IDENTICAL tactical judgment (what counts as a good/bad capture, capture ordering) regardless of their different `MaterialWeights` — only each side's own STATIC EVAL at leaf nodes (and therefore its pruning decisions — razoring/futility margins, and non-capture move preferences driven by the searched score) actually differs. This was discovered empirically while sanity-testing the match harness: a `MaterialWeights` vector with every non-pawn piece devalued to 1 centipawn was expected to lose decisively and instead mostly drew against the defaults (0 wins across every trial, but few decisive losses either) — because SEE, using the real (unweighted) piece values, still correctly avoided outright bad captures on that side's behalf even though its own eval would have happily accepted them. This is a genuine, honest limitation of this session's scope, not a bug: extending `material_weights` into SEE/move-ordering as well is real, separate follow-on work (not attempted this session, on top of everything else already built) that a future session should do before this match harness's results can be treated as a fully faithful strength measurement, particularly for weight vectors whose material values diverge more subtly than this session's deliberately extreme sanity-test case.

**Why `MatchConfig` mirrors `SelfPlayConfig`'s own shape (small defaults, `search_depth`/`random_opening_plies`/`max_plies` fields) and why `play_match()`'s own game loop duplicates, rather than reuses, `tuner::selfplay`'s `play_game_impl()`:** the two loops share a similar shape (same four end conditions: checkmate/stalemate/50-move/threefold-repetition, plus the same `max_plies` safety net for the same reason — insufficient-material detection isn't built yet, ROADMAP.md Phase 6) but serve genuinely different purposes — `selfplay`'s loop plays under a SINGLE weight vector and samples positions for a training corpus; `match`'s loop plays under TWO DIFFERENT weight vectors (one per color, swapped each game) and produces no training data at all, only a final result. Forcing them into one shared function with extra parameters to cover both cases would blur two conceptually distinct tools for a modest amount of code reuse — the same "narrowly-scoped, not maximally-DRY" judgment call this codebase has made before when two pieces of logic look similar but serve different concerns.

**Why color alternates every game rather than `weights_a` always playing White:** White's own first-move advantage is real and would otherwise confound the whole comparison — a weight vector that happened to always play White could look stronger (or weaker) purely from that advantage, independent of its own eval quality. Alternating (even game index: A plays White; odd: B plays White) means each vector gets an equal share of White/Black across the match, so `score_a()` reflects the vectors' own relative strength.

**Two real mistakes were caught and fixed during this session's own verification, not glossed over:** (1) An early version of the "queen undervalued" sanity test used floating-point exact equality (`a_ahead.elo_diff() == -b_ahead.elo_diff()`) to check `elo_diff()`'s symmetry — failed on a tiny (∼3e-14) floating-point rounding difference between the two `std::log10()` calls; fixed with a small-epsilon tolerance comparison instead, the correct way to compare two independently-computed floating-point values expected to be mathematically, not bit-for-bit, equal. (2) A test that hand-built a `Position` via bare `Position pos;` default-construction plus `place_piece()` calls (mirroring a pattern that had worked fine for `eval::evaluate()`-only tests in earlier sessions) SEGFAULTED the moment real SEARCH (not just a single `evaluate()` call) touched it — root cause, found via a standalone ASan repro: `Position`'s default constructor does not leave `castling_rights`/`en_passant_square` in a safe state on its own, and `make_move()`'s en-passant handling read garbage as a result, corrupting an internal Zobrist-key table lookup. `tests/eval_tests.cpp` already had its own local `empty_position()` helper that explicitly sets both fields precisely because of this — this session's new `tests/match_tests.cpp` had simply not been using it yet; fixed by adding the identical helper locally (matching this codebase's existing per-test-file convention for it, rather than promoting it to a shared header for one more caller) and using it everywhere a hand-built `Position` was needed. This is a real, pre-existing hazard in `board::Position`'s own default-constructibility worth flagging for whoever next hand-builds a `Position` for a SEARCH-touching test (not just an eval-only one, which happens to have been safe purely because `eval::evaluate()` itself never reads `castling_rights`/`en_passant_square`) — not something this session fixed at its source (`board::Position`'s own constructor), since doing so is a `board/` module change with its own blast radius this session didn't set out to make, and every existing call site already either uses `empty_position()`/`start_position()`/`parse_fen()` or (as of this fix) now does.

**What was built:** `src/search/search.h` — `#include "eval/psqt.h"` added; `search_fixed_depth()`/`search_iterative_deepening()` gain an optional `const eval::MaterialWeights* material_weights = nullptr` parameter, documented as `tuner::match`'s own real caller. `src/search/search.cpp` — `negamax()`/`search_root()` gain a mandatory (internal-call-site-level, matching `pawn_tt`/`eval_cache`'s own existing convention) `material_weights` parameter, threaded through all 10 recursive `negamax()` calls, both `eval::evaluate()` calls (razoring, futility), the depth<=0 `quiescence()` delegation, both top-level entry functions, and every `search_root()` call site. `src/search/quiescence.h`/`quiescence.cpp` — same threading through `quiescence()`/`quiescence_impl()`, its own two `eval::evaluate()` calls, and its own recursive self-call. `src/tuner/match.h`/`match.cpp` (new) — `MatchConfig`, `MatchResult` (`score_a()`, `elo_diff()`), `play_match()`. `src/tuner/match_main.cpp` (new) — `nightwing_match` executable, including a small tolerant parser for `nightwing_tune`'s own `key=value` stdout format. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `tuner/match.cpp` added to the library, `nightwing_match` executable target added. `tests/CMakeLists.txt`/`tests/match_tests.cpp` (new, 9 tests) — `MatchResult::score_a()`/`elo_diff()`'s own math (standard cases, zero-games edge case, symmetry, perfect-score-stays-finite), `play_match()`'s structural invariant (`games_played == wins_a + wins_b + draws`) and seed-reproducibility, the "catastrophically undervalued minor/major pieces never wins" sanity check (empirically verified across several configs/seeds before being written as an assertion — see this entry's own account of what it does and doesn't prove, given the SEE/MVV-LVA scope boundary above), and a `search_fixed_depth()`-level confirmation that a `material_weights` override changes the returned SEARCH score, not just a raw `eval::evaluate()` call. Verified against a fresh repository checkout with every new/changed file overlaid, both Release and Debug (ASan+UBSan) configurations: `nightwing_lib`/`nightwing`/`nightwing_bench`/`nightwing_selfplay`/`nightwing_tune`/`nightwing_match`/`nightwing_tests` all build clean, zero warnings, both configs, including the large mechanical search-layer threading change (compiled clean on the very first attempt). Full test suite, both configurations: **all 323 test cases, 52,836 assertions, passed** (up from Session 59's 314/52,815 — this session's 9 new tests in `match_tests.cpp` account for the difference). Ran the complete real three-stage pipeline end to end by hand for the first time: `nightwing_selfplay` (30 games) → `nightwing_tune` (40 iterations) → `nightwing_match` (12 games, tuned weights vs. defaults) — every stage's output correctly consumed by the next, confirming the whole system, not just each piece in isolation. Regression bench (Linux Release, depth 6) identical in every field to the established baseline — every new parameter defaults to leaving existing behavior completely unchanged.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Extending `material_weights` into `search/see.cpp`/`search/ordering.cpp` in this same session, to close the scope gap described above immediately rather than documenting and deferring it — rejected for this session given everything else already built; flagged explicitly (this entry, ROADMAP.md's own annotation) as real follow-on work rather than silently left unaddressed.
- Sharing one game-playing loop between `tuner::selfplay` and `tuner::match` via extra parameters — rejected; see the dedicated rationale paragraph above.
- Fixing the `Position` default-constructor safety hazard at its source (`board/board.h`) rather than working around it in the new test file — rejected for this session as a `board/` module change with its own separate blast radius and testing burden, outside what this session set out to build; flagged in this entry for visibility.

---



**Decision:** Built the "gradient descent" half of ROADMAP.md's Texel/SPSA tuner item (Session 58, docs/DECISIONS.md's 2026-08-30 (4) entry, built the "self-play data generation" half first). This required two pieces: (1) the runtime-mutable parameter-vector abstraction over eval's currently-constexpr named constants that entry deferred, and (2) the actual gradient-descent loop built on top of it. Scoped the abstraction to material values ONLY — a new `eval::MaterialWeights` struct (`src/eval/psqt.h`, alongside `kPawnValue`/etc.), plus a `default_material_weights()` matching the compiled-in constants exactly, plus a new optional `const MaterialWeights*` parameter on `material_value()` (used INSTEAD of the constexpr constants when non-null) and a new optional `const MaterialWeights* material_weights` parameter threaded through `eval::evaluate()` down to that call. New `nightwing::tuner::tune` module (`src/tuner/tune.h`/`tune.cpp`) — `sigmoid()`, `compute_loss()` (Texel's Tuning Method MSE loss between `sigmoid(evaluate()/sigmoid_scale)` and each training position's labeled result), and `tune()` (full-batch numerical/finite-difference gradient descent over `kMaterialParameters`, an enumerable `{name, pointer-to-member}` table over `MaterialWeights`' 10 fields). New `nightwing_tune` executable (`src/tuner/tune_main.cpp`) reads `tuner::selfplay`'s training-data format from stdin and prints tuned weights plus the per-iteration loss curve.

**Rationale for scoping the runtime-mutable abstraction to material values only, not every eval term at once:** material values are the smallest, cleanest, most self-contained term set to convert first — exactly 5 named constants, already isolated behind one function (`material_value()`, itself named-constant-ified only one session earlier, 2026-08-30 (2)), called from exactly one place inside `evaluate()`'s own hot loop. Converting every eval term (PSQT tables, pawn structure, mobility, king safety, and the rest of `eval/*.h`) to the same runtime-mutable-plus-optional-override shape in one session would have meant touching essentially every eval file in a single change — a large, high-blast-radius refactor this codebase's own established discipline (narrowly-scoped, thoroughly-verified sessions throughout Phase 5) argues against attempting all at once, especially for a change whose entire purpose is correctness-sensitive (a tuner reading/writing the wrong value produces confidently wrong output with no obvious symptom — the same concern 2026-08-30 (4)'s entry raised about rushing this work). `kMaterialParameters`' pointer-to-member enumeration pattern (`MaterialParameterRef`, tune.h) is deliberately written to generalize cleanly — a future session extending coverage to another eval module's terms adds that module's own such table the same way, without needing to redesign the enumeration mechanism itself.

**Why a critical interaction with `eval_cache` needed explicit handling, not just "add a parameter and thread it through":** `eval::EvalCache` (2026-08-30 (1)) caches `evaluate()`'s full result keyed purely on `pos.zobrist_hash`, under the existing assumption that `evaluate()` is a pure function of the position alone. With `material_weights` in play, that's no longer true — `evaluate()` becomes a function of (position, weights). Naively leaving `eval_cache`'s probe/store logic untouched would mean a tuning run's `compute_loss()` calls could silently return a STALE result computed under a DIFFERENT weight vector from an earlier call at the same position — exactly the "confidently wrong tuned values with no obvious symptom" failure mode called out above, and a genuinely easy mistake to make (the two features were built in different sessions with no natural forcing function to notice the interaction). Fixed by having `evaluate()` never probe or store `eval_cache` at all whenever `material_weights != nullptr`, regardless of whether a real `eval_cache` pointer is also passed — a defensive, correct-by-construction guard rather than relying on every future caller to remember not to pass both. `pawn_tt` needed no equivalent guard: pawn structure evaluation doesn't depend on material values at all, so it stays completely safe to use alongside `material_weights`. `compute_loss()` itself never passes either cache anyway (this is an offline tuning computation, not the search hot path either cache exists to speed up), but the guard inside `evaluate()` protects any OTHER future caller that might combine `material_weights` with a real cache without realizing the risk.

**Why `MaterialWeights` fields are `double`, not `int` (an eval `Score` stores `int`):** a tuning run's own in-progress values are inherently fractional (gradient-descent steps are rarely whole numbers) — `material_value()` rounds a `MaterialWeights` field to the nearest int (`round_to_int()`, `psqt.h`) only at the point it actually needs to produce a `Score`, so the underlying weight vector itself can accumulate fine-grained progress across many iterations even when that progress doesn't yet cross an integer boundary and change `evaluate()`'s output. `round_to_int()` is written as plain arithmetic (round half away from zero) rather than calling `std::lround()`/`std::round()`, specifically so `material_value()` can stay a genuinely `constexpr`-capable function on its zero-override path (this codebase targets C++20 — ARCHITECTURE.md — and `<cmath>`'s rounding functions aren't constexpr until C++23).

**Why numerical (finite-difference) gradient descent, not an analytic gradient, even though material's own contribution to `evaluate()` is exactly linear (each weight's true partial derivative is just that piece type's board-relative count) and an analytic gradient would be easy for this specific term:** deliberately treating `evaluate()` as an opaque black-box function of its weight vector — probe two nearby points, estimate the slope — is what keeps working unchanged once a future session extends coverage to genuinely non-linear terms (PSQT interpolation, mobility, king safety, ...), where a hand-derived analytic gradient would be real, term-specific extra work every single time coverage expands. Building the generic mechanism now, even though it's more expensive per iteration than the analytic shortcut would be for material specifically, avoids having to redesign the tuning loop's core mechanism later.

**A real, empirically-discovered numerical subtlety this session had to work through, not just anticipate in the abstract:** an initial `TuneConfig::learning_rate` default of 1.0 (a plausible-looking first guess) was tried during development and produced a tuning run where the LOSS never visibly changed across iterations at all, even though the underlying weight vector genuinely was moving. Root cause, confirmed by direct instrumentation against this session's own build (not just reasoned about): the `sigmoid(eval / 400)` squared-error loss formulation produces inherently small-magnitude gradients (on the order of 1e-4 to 1e-3 loss-per-centipawn, since sigmoid's own slope of at most 0.25 gets scaled down again by the 1/400 chain-rule factor) — a learning rate of 1.0 moved each parameter by only a few thousandths of a centipawn per iteration, far too little to ever cross the integer-rounding boundary `material_value()`'s `round_to_int()` imposes, so `evaluate()`'s actual output (and therefore the loss) never changed even after dozens of iterations of technically-correct gradient descent. Fixed by empirically sweeping learning rates against a hand-built test scenario (a King+Knight-vs-King position labeled as a draw, deliberately disagreeing with the current knight value) directly against this session's own build, and settling on 20000.0 as the new default — confirmed to produce steady, strictly monotonic loss reduction with no oscillation even after dozens of iterations, while smaller candidate values (100–5000) made comparatively little visible progress in a realistic iteration budget. `TuneConfig::finite_diff_epsilon`'s own doc comment documents the closely related requirement that the perturbation size itself must be >= 1.0 for the same integer-rounding reason. Both defaults are documented as reasonable STARTING points for this small-corpus, material-only version, not values empirically fit against a real, large production training corpus — the not-yet-run "Tuned weights committed" ROADMAP item should re-verify them at real scale.

**Why `sigmoid_scale` (Texel's "K" constant) is a fixed default (400.0) rather than fit from the data:** CPW's own Texel's Tuning Method page describes fitting K as a separate, prior step before tuning the actual eval parameters; this module starts from the same commonly-used fixed value other from-scratch tuning implementations typically start from, and proper K-fitting is left as later refinement rather than attempted this session, on top of everything else already built.

**What was built:** `src/eval/psqt.h` — `MaterialWeights` struct, `default_material_weights()`, `round_to_int()` helper, `material_value()` gains an optional `const MaterialWeights*` override parameter (every existing call site — `search/see.cpp`, `search/ordering.cpp`, `search/quiescence.cpp` — omits it, unaffected). `src/eval/eval.h`/`eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` gains an optional `const MaterialWeights* material_weights` parameter, forwarded to `material_value()`; `eval_cache` probe/store both gated on `material_weights == nullptr` (this entry's own "critical interaction" section above). `src/tuner/tune.h`/`tune.cpp` (new) — `MaterialParameterRef`, `kMaterialParameters`, `TuneConfig`, `TuneIteration`, `TuneResult`, `sigmoid()`, `compute_loss()`, `tune()`. `src/tuner/tune_main.cpp` (new) — `nightwing_tune` executable. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `tuner/tune.cpp` added to the library, `nightwing_tune` executable target added. `tests/CMakeLists.txt`/`tests/tune_tests.cpp` (new, 10 tests) — `sigmoid()`'s own properties, `kMaterialParameters`' structural correctness (every member pointer reaches exactly the field its name claims), `compute_loss()`'s empty-input and exact-zero-loss (constructed by computing the real `evaluate()` result first, not by assuming a round-number label happens to match) behavior, a bare-kings training set leaving every material weight EXACTLY unchanged (the gradient is provably, not just approximately, zero when no material of any tunable type is on the board — King/None always return `{0, 0}` regardless of `weights`), a hand-built imbalanced position's training set producing strictly monotonic loss reduction over 30 real iterations with `knight_eg` (not `knight_mg` — this exact position's very low game phase means `taper()` weights the eg term far more heavily, confirmed by direct instrumentation before writing the assertion, not assumed) moving in the expected direction, and `TuneResult`'s own convenience-field/history consistency. `tests/eval_tests.cpp` — 6 new tests for `MaterialWeights`/`default_material_weights()`/`material_value()`'s override parameter, and the `eval_cache`-must-stay-untouched-when-`material_weights`-is-set interaction (a poisoned cache entry, confirmed to remain both unread AND unoverwritten). Verified against a fresh repository checkout with every new/changed file overlaid, both Release and Debug (ASan+UBSan) configurations: `nightwing_lib`/`nightwing`/`nightwing_bench`/`nightwing_selfplay`/`nightwing_tune`/`nightwing_tests` all build clean, zero warnings, both configs. Two genuine test-authoring mistakes were caught and fixed during this session's own verification, not glossed over: an `EvalCache` poison-value test used a sentinel (999999) outside `int16_t` range, silently wrapping on store (eval/eval_cache.h's own `int16_t` choice, 2026-08-30 (1)) — fixed by using an in-range sentinel (12345); and the original `learning_rate` default (documented above) that produced an invisible-progress tuning run. Full test suite, both configurations: **all 314 test cases, 52,815 assertions, passed** (up from Session 58's 298 test cases/52,715 assertions — this session's 16 new tests, 10 in `tune_tests.cpp` and 6 in `eval_tests.cpp`, account for the difference). Ran the complete real pipeline end to end by hand: `nightwing_selfplay` (30 games, depth 3) piped into `nightwing_tune` (40 iterations) on genuine self-play output (1,526 sampled positions) — loss decreased monotonically from 0.015331 to 0.015026 with visible, sensible movement in every material weight, confirming the whole system works together, not just each piece in isolation. Regression bench (Linux Release, depth 6) identical in every field to the established baseline, exactly as expected — every new parameter defaults to leaving `evaluate()`'s behavior completely unchanged.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Converting every eval term to the runtime-mutable-plus-override shape in this same session, to deliver "the complete tuner" in one go -- rejected; see "Rationale" above.
- An analytic gradient for material's own (genuinely linear) contribution instead of finite differences -- rejected; see the dedicated rationale paragraph above on why the general, black-box mechanism was chosen deliberately despite being more expensive for this specific, linear case.
- Leaving `TuneConfig::learning_rate` at the originally-planned 1.0 default and treating the invisible-progress behavior as a training-data/iteration-count problem for a future session to notice and debug -- rejected once the actual mechanism (integer-rounding boundary, not insufficient data) was understood directly; shipping a default that silently does nothing on realistic data would have been a real quality regression hiding behind code that "looks like" working gradient descent.
- Storing `MaterialWeights` fields as `int` (matching `Score`) instead of `double` -- rejected: would either lose all sub-integer gradient-descent progress every single iteration (rounding immediately, not just at the point of producing a `Score`) or require the same double-precision bookkeeping to live somewhere else anyway; keeping the tuning-in-progress representation and the "value `Score` actually stores" representation distinct, with one explicit rounding step (`round_to_int()`) at the boundary between them, was the more direct implementation of that same idea.

---



**Decision:** ROADMAP.md's "Texel/SPSA tuner module (self-play data generation + gradient descent)" item names two genuinely separable sub-parts in its own parenthetical. This session builds ONLY the first: a new `nightwing::tuner` module (`src/tuner/selfplay.h`/`selfplay.cpp`, new `src/tuner/` directory) that plays engine-vs-itself games via `search::search_fixed_depth()` for both sides, with a short uniformly-random opening (new use of the existing `support::Xorshift64Star` PRNG, previously only a startup/init-time utility per its own header comment) as each game's only source of diversity (`search_fixed_depth()` is itself fully deterministic, so identical positions always produce identical moves), samples "quiet" positions from each game (not in check, not about to make/receive a capture, and never from the random-opening plies), and labels each with the game's eventual result (1.0/0.5/0.0, White's perspective, matching CPW's "Texel's Tuning Method" convention: https://www.chessprogramming.org/Texel%27s_Tuning_Method). A new `nightwing_selfplay` executable (`src/tuner/selfplay_main.cpp`, mirroring `bench.cpp`'s "thin main() over a real library function" shape) runs a batch and writes the resulting `<fen>;<result>` training-data file to stdout. The gradient-descent Texel-loss optimization loop over eval's named tunable constants (docs/DECISIONS.md, the "All terms as named tunable constants" entry immediately before this one) is explicitly NOT built this session — see "Rationale" below.

**Rationale for splitting the two halves across sessions:** a real gradient-descent tuning loop needs a parameter-VECTOR abstraction over eval that doesn't exist yet — every eval term is currently an `inline constexpr Score kXxxBonus` (compile-time constant, by design, per every eval module's own established convention), and a tuner needs to read and WRITE those values at runtime across many iterations. Building that abstraction correctly (enumerable, runtime-mutable weights that eval's own terms reference instead of referencing the constexpr names directly, without silently reintroducing runtime overhead into the actual search/eval hot path outside of a tuning run) is itself substantial, separable design work deserving its own session-length review and its own DECISIONS.md entry — attempting it in the same message as self-play generation, on top of this session's own build/test verification depth, risked rushing exactly the part of this feature with the most room for a subtle correctness bug (a tuner that reads the wrong weight, or writes one and forgets to reset it, produces confidently wrong tuned values with no obvious symptom). This mirrors an established precedent in this codebase for splitting a single ROADMAP bullet's sub-parts across sessions when they're genuinely separable and one has a real prerequisite the other doesn't (2026-08-21 (1)'s "Pawn structure eval implemented ahead of the Pawn hash table it exists to serve" is the closest earlier example — a sequencing decision made explicit in the docs rather than silently reflected only in commit history).

**Why self-play generation could safely go first despite that same prerequisite concern:** it has NO dependency on the parameter-vector abstraction at all — it only ever calls `eval::evaluate()` indirectly, through `search_fixed_depth()`'s already-existing, already-tested search, using whatever constants are currently compiled in. Building and thoroughly testing it now, rather than waiting, also means a real training corpus can start being generated (by hand, or via a GitHub Actions `workflow_dispatch` job) in parallel with next session's tuner work, rather than both being blocked on the same single session.

**Why self-play games' only source of diversity is a short random opening, not randomness threaded through the rest of each game:** `search_fixed_depth()`'s own determinism (an established property of this codebase's search — same position, same depth, same result, every time) means the alternative (randomizing move selection throughout) would mean deliberately fighting the search's own correctness property instead of working with it. A random opening of `SelfPlayConfig::random_opening_plies` (default 8) plies, then real search-selected play for the rest of the game, is standard self-play/tuning-corpus practice and keeps the reproducibility property this file's own tests rely on (same seed -> byte-identical game, useful for debugging a specific game later) intact for everything after the opening.

**Why sampled positions exclude the random-opening plies and require "quiet" (not in check, not about to capture):** CPW's own Texel's Tuning Method page recommends training against static-eval-representative positions, not ones where an imminent tactic (about to be resolved by quiescence search, not by the raw static eval a tuner is meant to calibrate) or a uniformly-random, never-actually-played opening move would inject noise unrelated to what eval::evaluate() is supposed to be judged on. The filter used (not in check, next move not a capture) is deliberately the simple, standard approximation — not a full "verify quiescence search wouldn't change anything" check, which would cost meaningfully more per candidate position for a marginal precision gain not needed for a first version of this tool.

**Why a hard `max_plies` safety cap exists as a fourth draw condition alongside checkmate/stalemate/50-move/threefold-repetition:** this codebase does not yet have insufficient-material draw detection (ROADMAP.md Phase 6's own "draw detection refinement (insufficient material)" item, explicitly not yet built) — without a cap, a drawn-but-unbalanced endgame (KBK, KNK, etc.) that neither side's own search recognizes as drawn could run indefinitely. Reaching the cap is scored as a draw (0.5), matching CPW's own stated convention for adjudicated/unterminated games in this exact context, and is safe specifically because it only fires on positions this codebase's own shallow-depth self-play search already treats as roughly balanced by then.

**Why the training-data file format is `<fen>;<result>` (semicolon-separated) rather than space-separated:** a FEN string already contains internal spaces (piece placement, side to move, castling rights, en passant target, and the two move-count fields are all space-separated within one FEN), so a space-separated `<fen> <result>` line would be ambiguous to re-split without additional structure; a character that never appears inside a valid FEN (`;`) avoids that ambiguity with the simplest possible format, no delimiter-escaping logic needed. `read_training_data()` was built now (not deferred alongside the tuning loop) since it's a small, natural counterpart to `write_training_data()` that also enables this session's own round-trip test, and the not-yet-built tuner will need it regardless.

**What was built:** `src/tuner/selfplay.h`/`selfplay.cpp` (new) — `SelfPlayPosition`, `SelfPlayGame`, `SelfPlayConfig`, `play_one_game()`, `play_games()`, `write_training_data()`, `read_training_data()`. `src/tuner/selfplay_main.cpp` (new) — `nightwing_selfplay` executable, positional-argument config (`num_games`, `base_seed`, `search_depth`, `random_opening_plies`, `max_plies`), writes training data to stdout, a short run summary to stderr. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `tuner/selfplay.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources, `nightwing_selfplay` executable target added (mirroring `nightwing_bench`'s own). `tests/CMakeLists.txt`/`tests/selfplay_tests.cpp` (new, 9 tests) — game termination/valid-result invariants, same-seed reproducibility, different-seeds-diverge (two empirically hand-verified seeds, same "verified fixture" spirit as `pawn_tt_tests.cpp`'s reused doubled-pawns FEN — checked directly against a real build of this session's own code, not assumed), no positions sampled while every ply is a random-opening ply, every sampled position's side to move confirmed not in check (re-verified independently in the test file, not by trusting `selfplay.cpp`'s own internal helper), `play_games()`'s consecutive-seeding contract, training-data write/read round-trip, the exact `;`-separated line format, and `read_training_data()`'s malformed-line tolerance. Verified against a fresh repository checkout with every new/changed file overlaid: `nightwing_lib`/`nightwing`/`nightwing_bench`/`nightwing_selfplay`/`nightwing_tests` all build clean, zero warnings, `-Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic`, in BOTH Release and Debug (ASan+UBSan) configurations; `nightwing_selfplay` run by hand end to end (5 games, produced a well-formed training-data stream on stdout and a run summary on stderr); full test suite passed clean in both configurations — **Release: all 298 test cases, 52,715 assertions. Debug/ASan/UBSan: identical, all 298 test cases, 52,715 assertions** (up from Session 57's 289/52,380 — the 9 new `[selfplay]` cases and their 335 assertions account for the difference); the 9 `[selfplay]` cases alone run in under 1 second under Release and ~13 seconds under Debug/ASan/UBSan — comfortably within CI's existing per-push budget. Regression bench (Linux Release, depth 6) identical in every field to the established baseline, exactly as expected for a change that adds a new, separate tool without touching search/eval.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Building both halves (self-play generation and the gradient-descent tuner) in one session, accepting a larger, less-thoroughly-verified single change -- rejected; see "Rationale" above.
- Randomizing move selection throughout every self-play game (not just the opening), e.g. by adding noise to search's own move ordering or occasionally picking a non-best move at any ply -- rejected: would trade away `search_fixed_depth()`'s determinism (and this module's own reproducibility, which its tests rely on) for diversity a short random opening already provides just as well across a large enough batch of different seeds.
- A file format encoding the result as `1-0`/`1/2-1/2`/`0-1` (PGN's own result notation) instead of a numeric `1.0`/`0.5`/`0.0` -- rejected: the not-yet-built tuner will want the numeric value directly for its loss computation every single position it reads; storing PGN notation would just mean re-parsing the same three-way mapping back to a double on every read for no benefit, since this format isn't meant to be a general-purpose PGN-adjacent interchange format, just this tool's own training-data shape.
- Sourcing self-play games from a PGN/EPD library file instead of playing them internally via `search_fixed_depth()` -- rejected: generating games from this engine's OWN current search/eval is the entire point of a Texel-style self-tuning loop (CPW's own description) -- training against externally-sourced games (e.g. another engine's or humans') would tune this engine's eval to match a different search's move/position preferences, not its own.
