**Decision:** `src/CMakeLists.txt`'s `add_library(nightwing_lib STATIC ...)` sources list did not include `eval/eval_cache.cpp` -- the file itself, and every other file that declares or calls into `EvalCache` (`eval/eval_cache.h`, `eval.h`/`.cpp`, `search/quiescence.h`/`.cpp`, `search/search.cpp`, `tests/eval_cache_tests.cpp`, `tests/CMakeLists.txt`), were all present and correct in the pushed commit, but this one line in `src/CMakeLists.txt` was not -- meaning `EvalCache`'s member functions were declared and called everywhere but never actually compiled into `nightwing_lib`, producing "undefined reference"/"unresolved external symbol" linker errors on every one of CI's 6 platform/config jobs (GNU ld on Linux, MSVC link.exe on Windows, Apple ld on macOS -- confirmed identical root cause across all three toolchains' differently-worded error output). Fixed by adding `eval/eval_cache.cpp` back to the sources list, immediately after `eval/material_imbalance.cpp` and before `eval/eval.cpp`, matching where it was originally meant to sit (`eval.cpp` -- the file that actually calls into `EvalCache` -- listed last among the eval sources, same convention `eval/pawn_tt.cpp` already follows relative to the other term files that use it).

**Rationale:** A `.cpp` file existing in the repository is not sufficient for it to be built -- this build system (CMake with an explicit, hand-maintained source list rather than a glob) requires every compiled file to be named exactly once in `add_library()`'s argument list, and this one addition was evidently missed when `src/CMakeLists.txt` was delivered/committed for the Eval cache session (2026-08-30 (1)) even though the accompanying `tests/CMakeLists.txt` update (registering `eval_cache_tests.cpp`) DID make it in correctly -- the test file still compiled fine everywhere (its own `.cpp` was listed), it just failed at the final link step once the test binary tried to pull in `nightwing_lib`'s (missing) `EvalCache` symbols.

**Why this surfaced at link time, not compile time, and why it passed locally in the delivering session's own verification:** `eval_cache.h`'s declarations are all any `.cpp` file that merely CALLS `EvalCache`'s methods needs to compile successfully against -- the missing piece is only the DEFINITIONS, which only matters once the linker tries to resolve those calls against actual object code, which only happens building a full executable/test binary, not a syntax-only or single-translation-unit check. The delivering session's own verification (docs/DECISIONS.md, 2026-08-30 (1)'s "What was built" section) DID build the full `nightwing_lib`/`nightwing`/`nightwing_bench`/test binary successfully and run the suite green -- against a sandbox copy of `src/CMakeLists.txt` that WAS correctly edited in that session's own working files; the gap was specifically between that correct sandbox file and what actually got committed/pushed to the live repository afterward, not a gap in the verification method itself.

**What was built:** `src/CMakeLists.txt` -- one line added (`eval/eval_cache.cpp`). No other file touched; every other file from the Eval cache and tunable-constants sessions was already correct in the live repository, confirmed by fetching the live repo fresh and checking (this session's own verification, below). No new tests -- this is a build-configuration fix, not a behavioral change; the 12 `eval_cache_tests.cpp` cases already added in the Eval cache session are what now finally get to run (they compiled successfully before this fix too, in isolation, but the binary that would run them never finished linking). Verified against a genuinely fresh clone of the live repository (not a sandbox copy with other files overlaid) with only this one-line `src/CMakeLists.txt` fix applied: clean configure, clean build of `nightwing_lib`/`nightwing`/`nightwing_bench`/`nightwing_tests` with zero errors, and the full test suite -- **all 289 test cases, 52,380 assertions, passed** -- with the identical regression-bench totals already established as the expected baseline for this exact commit's search behavior.

**Alternatives considered:**
- None -- this was a single missing line in a source list with one unambiguous fix, not a design decision with real alternatives to weigh.

**Decision:** Audited every scoring line in every `src/eval/*.cpp` file (`psqt`, `pawns`, `pawn_tt`, `mobility`, `king_safety`, `piece_bonuses`, `knight_outposts`, `space`, `threats`, `king_tropism`, `trapped_pieces`, `tempo`, `material_imbalance`, `eval` itself) for raw numeric literals used directly in a `Score`-accumulating expression, rather than a named `inline constexpr Score kXxxBonus/kXxxPenalty` constant declared in that module's own header — the pattern every module has followed since it was first written (`tempo.h`'s `kTempoBonus`, `pawns.h`'s `kIsolatedPawnPenalty`, etc.). Found exactly one gap: `eval/psqt.h`'s `material_value()` returned raw literals (`{100, 100}`, `{320, 320}`, `{330, 330}`, `{500, 500}`, `{900, 900}`) directly in its `switch` statement's `return` lines, rather than referencing named constants the way every other term in the codebase already does. Fixed by adding `kPawnValue`/`kKnightValue`/`kBishopValue`/`kRookValue`/`kQueenValue` (all `Score`, all `inline constexpr`, declared immediately above `material_value()`) and having each `case` return the corresponding named constant instead of a literal. `material_value()`'s own signature, behavior, and every caller (`quiescence.cpp`'s delta pruning, `see.cpp`, `ordering.cpp`'s MVV-LVA) are entirely unchanged — this is a pure internal refactor, zero behavioral difference.

**Rationale:** ROADMAP.md's own item wording ("per DECISIONS.md") points at this codebase's established convention rather than specifying new content, so the work here was auditing for adherence to a pattern already in place everywhere else, not inventing a new one. `compute_phase()` (`eval.cpp`) was checked too as a plausible second candidate (game-phase weights are exactly the kind of number that could have been left as a raw literal) but was already fully compliant — `kKnightPhase`/`kBishopPhase`/`kRookPhase`/`kQueenPhase`/`kMaxPhase` are all named constants in `score.h`.

**Why base material values specifically mattered most to catch, of anything an audit like this could have found:** these are typically the single most heavily tuned parameter set in any Texel/SPSA tuner (CPW's own "Texel's Tuning Method" discussion treats material weights, conventionally anchored to pawn=100, as the foundation the rest of a tuning run's other terms are calibrated relative to) -- leaving exactly this one term as an un-named literal would have been the one real obstacle to Phase 5's very next item (the Texel/SPSA tuner module itself) being able to enumerate and adjust every term uniformly.

**Why the multi-piece-table constants (the seven `kPawnTable`/`kKnightTable`/.../`kKingEgTable[64]` PSQT arrays in `psqt.cpp`, and `pawns.h`'s existing `kPassedPawnBonus[8]` per-rank table) were judged to already satisfy "named tunable constant" and needed no further per-cell naming:** an entire table, not each individual cell inside it, is the natural tunable unit for a PSQT/per-rank bonus scheme -- the array itself already has one clear name a tuner (or a human) can address as a unit, exactly the same granularity this codebase already established as sufficient for `kPassedPawnBonus[8]` well before this audit. Naming each of a PSQT's 64 individual cells individually would add substantial verbosity for no real gain in tunability, since a Texel/SPSA tuner adjusts array elements by index either way.

**Why small geometric/structural literals (e.g. `king_safety.cpp`'s shield-zone loop bounds `df` in `[-1,1]`, `dr` in `[1,2]`; `space.cpp`'s analogous zone-shape loop) were judged out of scope and left as-is:** these define WHICH squares a term looks at, not how much a term is worth -- not eval weights, and not what a Texel/SPSA tuner would ever adjust (tuning "which squares count as the shield zone" is a structural/design question, not a gradient-descent-over-scalars one). This distinction was already established for `space.cpp`'s own `kSpaceZoneMinFile`/`kSpaceZoneMaxFile`/`kSpaceZoneMinRelativeRank`/`kSpaceZoneMaxRelativeRank` (those four ARE named, since they're referenced from more than one place and worth a name for readability) versus `king_safety.cpp`'s single-use, single-purpose `shield_zone()` helper (not named, by prior decision, 2026-08-26 (6)) -- this audit didn't revisit or change that prior call, just confirmed it's a geometry/weight distinction, not a "was this missed" gap.

**What was built:** `src/eval/psqt.h` -- five new named constants (`kPawnValue`, `kKnightValue`, `kBishopValue`, `kRookValue`, `kQueenValue`), `material_value()`'s switch statement updated to reference them. No other file touched -- `material_value()`'s callers (`quiescence.cpp`, `see.cpp`, `ordering.cpp`) needed no changes, since its signature and return values are identical. No new tests -- existing `eval_tests.cpp`/`psqt`-adjacent coverage already exercises `material_value()`'s exact return values for every piece type, and a pure constant-extraction refactor with no behavioral change doesn't call for new test cases, only confirmation the existing suite still passes. Verified end to end against a fully fresh repository checkout with the single changed file overlaid: `nightwing_lib`/`nightwing`/`nightwing_bench` all build clean, zero warnings, `-Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic`; full test suite (Catch2) ran clean -- **all 289 test cases, 52,380 assertions, passed**, identical count to the prior session (no tests added or removed, as expected for a same-behavior refactor); regression bench (Linux Release, depth 6) identical in every field to the prior two sessions' baseline, exactly as expected for a change that alters zero returned values.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Treating this ROADMAP item as already fully satisfied without an audit, on the assumption that every session so far had already been disciplined about named constants -- rejected: that assumption turned out to be almost, but not entirely, correct (the one `material_value()` gap), so a real audit rather than a documentation-only pass was the right call, and cheap to do given how few files exist to check.
- Naming each PSQT table's 64 individual cells -- rejected; see "Rationale" above on why the table itself is the right granularity.
- Also naming `king_safety.cpp`'s/`space.cpp`'s small zone-shape geometric literals as individual named constants for uniformity with the rest of this audit -- rejected; see "Rationale" above on why these are a structurally different kind of number (which squares, not how much) that a tuner would never touch, and renaming them here would be scope creep past what this ROADMAP item is actually asking for.

**Decision:** A new `EvalCache` class (new files `src/eval/eval_cache.h`/`src/eval/eval_cache.cpp`) caches `eval::evaluate()`'s complete, final, already-tapered centipawn result -- not any one term -- keyed on a position's FULL Zobrist hash (`board::Position::zobrist_hash`, already incrementally maintained on every make/unmake, unlike `PawnHashTable`'s own `board::compute_pawn_hash()`). Modeled directly on `PawnHashTable`: single-entry-per-slot, unconditional replacement, power-of-2 sized in KB via a new `kDefaultEvalCacheSizeKB = 2048` constant (`search.cpp`). `evaluate()` gained an optional `EvalCache* eval_cache = nullptr` parameter, probed first (short-circuiting every term, including any `pawn_tt` probe, on a hit) and stored into right before returning on a miss. Threaded through `quiescence()`/`quiescence_impl()` and `negamax()`/`search_root()` exactly the way `pawn_tt` already is -- both top-level entry points (`search_fixed_depth()`, `search_iterative_deepening()`) construct one fresh, private instance per call, same lifetime scoping as every other search-local table.

**Rationale (why this is a genuinely different, non-duplicate cache):** the main TT (`search/tt.h`) caches SEARCH results (a score with an alpha-beta bound, valid only relative to the depth/window it was stored at) -- it never stores a position's raw static eval. `PawnHashTable` caches only the pawn-structure term, keyed on pawn placement alone. This cache stores `evaluate()`'s complete output for the exact, fully-placed position the full Zobrist hash identifies -- a hit skips every sub-term's work entirely, not just pawn structure's.

**Why a real hit rate exists despite `eval.cpp`'s own established reasoning against caching individual volatile terms** (piece placement changes on essentially every move, so a coarser-than-full-position key sees a near-100% miss rate on anything but pawn structure): that reasoning is about a SUB-term cache keyed on something less specific than the full position. This cache is keyed on the full position instead, so its hit rate isn't about how often piece placement repeats move-to-move -- it comes from two concrete sources already present in this codebase's own search: (1) transpositions, the same phenomenon the main TT exists to exploit, and (2) a genuinely surprising find while reading `search.cpp` for this task -- razoring and futility pruning each independently call `eval::evaluate()` on the SAME unchanged `pos` when both conditions apply at one `negamax()` node, a guaranteed hit with zero transpositions required. This second case alone was enough to justify the work regardless of how (2)'s transposition hit rate turns out empirically.

**Why `int16_t` for the stored value, not `int`:** matches `search::TTEntry::score`'s own identical reasoning (`search/tt.h`) -- `evaluate()` never returns a mate score (that's exclusively `negamax()`'s/`quiescence()`'s layered-on-top domain), and realistic eval-term magnitudes are comfortably within `[-32768, 32767]`.

**Why `kDefaultEvalCacheSizeKB` (2048) is larger than `kDefaultPawnTTSizeKB` (512) but far smaller than `kDefaultTTSizeMB`:** this cache's useful working set isn't naturally bounded the tight way pawn structure alone is (many fewer distinct pawn structures occur than distinct full positions), but it's still deliberately scoped as an optional supplement rather than the primary cache, matching ROADMAP.md's own "separate from TT" wording for this item.

**Why `eval_cache` is a mandatory reference in `negamax()`/`search_root()` (like `pawn_tt`), not given a default the way `evaluate()`'s/`quiescence()`'s own parameter is:** every call site inside `search.cpp` already threads a real instance through (both top-level entry points construct one) -- there's no in-file caller left needing a "no cache" fallback, whereas `evaluate()`/`quiescence()` are still called directly, without one, from `eval_tests.cpp`/`quiescence_tests.cpp`, which is exactly why those two keep the optional-pointer/default-nullptr shape.

**What was built:** `src/eval/eval_cache.h`/`src/eval/eval_cache.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` -- `evaluate()` gains the `eval_cache` parameter, probed first and stored on a miss; doc comments updated. `src/search/quiescence.h`/`src/search/quiescence.cpp` -- `eval_cache` threaded through `quiescence()`/`quiescence_impl()` and both of quiescence's own `evaluate()` call sites (stand-pat, `kMaxQuiescencePly` safety net) and its own recursive call into itself. `src/search/search.cpp` -- `eval_cache` threaded through `negamax()` (signature, both `evaluate()` call sites at razoring/futility, the depth<=0 quiescence delegation, and all ten of its own recursive `negamax()` calls: null move, ProbCut, singular-extension verification, and the four PVS/LMR move-loop calls) and `search_root()` (signature and its own three recursive `negamax()` calls); `kDefaultEvalCacheSizeKB` constant added; both top-level entry points construct a fresh `EvalCache` and pass it through. `src/CMakeLists.txt`/`tests/CMakeLists.txt` -- `eval/eval_cache.cpp`/`eval_cache_tests.cpp` registered. `tests/eval_cache_tests.cpp` (new, 12 tests): sizing/power-of-2/empty-miss/store-then-probe/zero-key-sentinel/tiny-table-no-false-hit/unconditional-overwrite/`clear()` table tests mirroring `pawn_tt_tests.cpp`'s own structure; `evaluate()` transparency (cached result matches uncached, on both the miss and hit call) for the starting position and for a position with a real material/pawn imbalance; a full-position-keying test confirming two different same-material positions (different Zobrist hashes) never collide onto the same cached value; and a combined `pawn_tt` + `eval_cache` test confirming an eval-cache hit short-circuits correctly and a miss still populates/uses `pawn_tt` normally underneath. Verified end to end against a fully fresh repository checkout with every touched file overlaid: `nightwing_lib`/`nightwing`/`nightwing_bench` all build clean with zero warnings under `-Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic`; a UCI smoke test (`go depth 8` from two different positions) ran the real `negamax()`/`quiescence()`/`eval_cache` path end to end with sane scores and legal PVs; the full test suite (Catch2, fetched live) ran clean -- **all 289 test cases, 52,380 assertions, passed**, including the new `eval_cache_tests.cpp`.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Storing the static eval inside the main TT's own entries instead of a separate table -- rejected: the main TT's replacement scheme (age/depth-preferred) is tuned for search-result value, not eval-cache value; a razoring/futility probe at a node with no existing TT entry (or one about to be evicted) would get no benefit, and conflating the two purposes would make the TT's own replacement reasoning harder to follow. A small, separate, unconditional-replacement table (same reasoning `PawnHashTable` already established) keeps both caches' replacement logic independently simple and correct.
- No eval cache at all, treating razoring+futility's double-`evaluate()` call as an acceptable, isolated inefficiency rather than justification for a whole new table -- rejected once the transposition-driven case (this cache's primary intended payoff, matching the ROADMAP item's own "performance optimization" framing) was also considered; the double-call finding made the concrete, zero-transposition-required benefit easy to state precisely, but it wasn't the sole justification.
- Keying on something coarser than the full Zobrist hash to raise the hit rate (e.g. material + pawn structure only, ignoring exact piece placement) -- rejected: `evaluate()`'s result genuinely depends on exact piece placement (PSQT, mobility, king safety, threats, king tropism, trapped pieces all care where pieces specifically sit, not just what material is present), so a coarser key would produce wrong cached values for positions that differ only in placement -- a correctness bug, not a tuning choice.

---



**Decision:** A new `material_imbalance_value()` (new files `src/eval/material_imbalance.h`/`src/eval/material_imbalance.cpp`) scores exactly the two cases ROADMAP.md's own item wording names -- bishop pair and knight pair -- each scaled by `missing_pawns = kStartingTotalPawns(16) - (total pawns of both colors currently on the board)`. Bishop pair (reusing `eval/piece_bonuses.h`'s own `popcount(bishops) >= 2` definition exactly, for consistency) gets an ADDITIONAL bonus of `kBishopPairPerMissingPawn * missing_pawns` (`{2, 3}` per missing pawn) on top of `piece_bonuses.h`'s existing flat `kBishopPairBonus` -- these are two separate, additive terms, not a replacement. Knight pair (`popcount(knights) >= 2`) gets a PENALTY of the same shape: `kKnightPairPerMissingPawn * missing_pawns`, with `kKnightPairPerMissingPawn = {-2, -2}` (negative). Wired into `eval::evaluate()` as an eleventh uncached term.

**Rationale:** The tenth unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md), whose own parenthetical example ("bishop pair / knight pair value shifts with pawn count, per Stockfish-classic style") is read here as a style reference, not a literal spec for Stockfish-classic's own much larger quadratic imbalance matrix (every own-piece-type-count times every other own- and enemy-piece-type-count) -- this implementation is deliberately scoped to exactly the two cases the item's own wording names, matching this codebase's established "narrowest version the ROADMAP item's own wording actually asks for" precedent (most recently Trapped piece penalties' own knights/bishops-only scope, 2026-08-29 (1)).

**Why a knight-pair PENALTY (not a bonus, unlike bishop pair):** two knights don't complement each other the way two bishops on opposite color complexes do (CPW's own "Material"/"Redundancy" discussion) -- there's no color-complex argument for a second knight adding genuinely new coverage the way there is for a second, opposite-colored bishop.

**Why the knight-pair penalty's magnitude GROWS in the SAME direction as the bishop-pair bonus (both scale up as pawns leave the board), not the opposite:** a knight's characteristic strength is maneuvering around a pawn chain and sitting on outposts a closed, pawn-heavy structure creates -- exactly the terrain that disappears as pawns are traded off. So as pawns thin out, knights lose the specific structure that made a second one still somewhat useful, and the redundancy cost becomes more pronounced, not less -- the mirror image of why bishops (a long-diagonal reach question) go the opposite way, per `piece_bonuses.h`'s own `kBishopPairBonus` doc comment.

**Why `missing_pawns` is a single, both-sides-combined figure rather than computed per side:** "how open has the game become" is a property of the whole board, matching `eval.cpp`'s own `compute_phase()` precedent of summing both sides' material into one shared phase value rather than a per-side one.

**Why `kKnightPairPerMissingPawn` uses equal `mg`/`eg` rather than picking a direction between them (unlike almost every other term added this phase):** this term's entire justification is already "how many pawns are left," a quantity directly in the formula itself -- there's no separate, additional phase-based argument distinct from that pawn-count scaling to justify a further mg/eg split on top of it, and introducing one anyway would be an unjustified extra degree of freedom rather than a defensible asymmetry.

**What was built:** `src/eval/material_imbalance.h`/`src/eval/material_imbalance.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` -- `evaluate()` adds `material_imbalance_value(pos)`; doc comments updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt`/`tests/CMakeLists.txt` -- `eval/material_imbalance.cpp`/`material_imbalance_tests.cpp` registered. `tests/material_imbalance_tests.cpp` (new, 6 tests): starting position exactly zero (full pawn count, zero scaling regardless of either side's pairs); White bishop pair with all pawns off scoring exactly `16 * kBishopPairPerMissingPawn`; White knight pair with all pawns off scoring exactly `16 * kKnightPairPerMissingPawn` (confirmed negative in both `mg` and `eg`); a single bishop (not a pair) scoring exactly zero regardless of missing pawns; linearity (half the pawns off scores exactly half the all-pawns-off value); and Black's bishop pair scoring the exact negation of the equivalent White case. Verified end to end via direct g++ compilation (this sandbox's compiler access, same as Sessions 51/53) against a fully fresh repository checkout with every touched file from this entire phase overlaid, compiling clean with `-Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic`, and a standalone driver confirming all 6 planned test values plus the full pre-existing `eval_tests.cpp`/`tempo_tests.cpp` suites together in one combined run.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Building out Stockfish-classic's full quadratic imbalance matrix (every piece-type pair, own and enemy) -- deferred; see "Rationale" above. A large, mostly-unjustifiable-by-hand table before a real Texel tuner exists would need per-cell hand estimates with no clear basis, the same general objection this codebase has raised against premature large tuned tables everywhere else this phase.
- Scoring rook value shifts with pawn count too (also part of the classic Kaufman's Rule this item's citation touches on) -- deferred; not named in ROADMAP.md's own item wording the way bishop pair and knight pair specifically are. A future, separate item if wanted.
- Replacing `piece_bonuses.h`'s flat `kBishopPairBonus` with this module's scaled version entirely, rather than keeping both as separate additive terms -- rejected: `piece_bonuses.h`'s own bonus is deliberately about the bishop pair's baseline value independent of pawn count (its own `eg`-above-`mg` split already captures a phase-based component); folding that into this module would conflate two genuinely different signals (game phase vs. specifically pawn count, which don't move in lockstep -- e.g. a queen-and-pawns-only endgame has few non-pawn pieces (low phase) but a normal pawn count) into one constant.

## 2026-08-29 (3) — Tempo bonus (Phase 5, ninth item): flat side-to-move bonus, new eval/tempo.cpp/.h module

**Decision:** A new `tempo_value()` (new files `src/eval/tempo.h`/`src/eval/tempo.cpp`) implements the standard CPW "Tempo" concept: `+kTempoBonus` if White is to move, `-kTempoBonus` if Black is to move, `kTempoBonus = {20, 10}`. Wired into `eval::evaluate()` as a tenth uncached term (trivially so -- see below). Unlike every other term added this phase, this one touches nothing but `pos.side_to_move`: no attack table, no board scan, no precondition on `init_masks()`/`init_magic_bitboards()` at all.

**Rationale:** The ninth unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md). Having the move is a real if modest advantage on its own -- an extra opportunity to create or answer a threat before the opponent's next chance to -- and CPW's own article ties this directly to why null-move pruning (search/*, Phase 4) works at all: passing the move away and checking whether the position still holds up is only a meaningful test if having the move was worth something in the first place.

**Why a single flat constant pair rather than anything scaled or conditional:** every other term this phase has a specific structural pattern to detect; "whose turn is it" has no such pattern -- it's already fully captured by `pos.side_to_move`. Matches this codebase's established "simplest defensible version first" preference (most recently Trapped piece penalties' own "exactly zero, not a threshold" choice, 2026-08-29 (1)) taken to its logical floor for a term with no detection problem to solve at all.

**Why `mg` is set above `eg` (same direction as eval/king_safety.h's terms, opposite eval/mobility.h's own mobility bonus):** initiative matters most while many pieces and live threats remain on the board for a first move to exploit. The endgame case is more mixed -- zugzwang (CPW: https://www.chessprogramming.org/Zugzwang) is common enough there that being forced to move can actively hurt the side to move, which is exactly why null-move pruning is conventionally disabled in zugzwang-prone endgame positions. This term does not attempt to detect zugzwang itself (a separate, not-yet-reached search-level item) -- `eg` is kept smaller but still positive, the standard, safe way to reflect that the same advantage is worth less on average once material is scarce, without ever flipping sign.

**What was built:** `src/eval/tempo.h`/`src/eval/tempo.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` -- `compute_phase()` (see 2026-08-29 (2) immediately below) now also declared in `eval.h`; `evaluate()` adds `tempo_value(pos)`. `src/CMakeLists.txt`/`tests/CMakeLists.txt` -- `eval/tempo.cpp`/`tempo_tests.cpp` registered. `tests/tempo_tests.cpp` (new, 5 tests) -- White-to-move exact value, Black-to-move exact negated value, independence from piece placement, the flip-negates-exactly property, and a sanity pin on the constant's own shape (`mg`/`eg` both positive, `mg >= eg`). `tests/eval_tests.cpp`'s own pre-existing "starting position is exactly balanced" test needed updating as a direct, expected consequence of this term existing -- see 2026-08-29 (2) below for that and the larger context it sits inside.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Scaling the bonus by material/phase in some more elaborate way, or by number of pieces attacked -- deferred; see "Why a single flat constant pair" above.
- Detecting zugzwang and suppressing/flipping the bonus in that specific case -- explicitly out of scope for this flat HCE term; belongs to search-level null-move/zugzwang-detection work if ever added (a distinct, not-yet-reached item).

---

## 2026-08-29 (2) — Bug fix: compute_phase() computed the game phase backwards, inverting every tapered eval term's actual mg/eg blend since Tapered Eval was introduced

**What was found, and how:** While verifying Tempo bonus's end-to-end behavior (2026-08-29 (3) above) with the sandbox's g++ access (docs/SESSIONS.md, Session 51's own note that this was the first session with real compiler access), a standalone driver showed `evaluate()` at the starting position changing by only half the expected amount when `pos.side_to_move` was flipped -- `kTempoBonus.eg`-sized (10), not `kTempoBonus.mg`-sized (20), even though the starting position (full material) should by every other term's own documented reasoning be resolved almost entirely by `mg`. Tracing this to `eval.cpp`'s `compute_phase()` (previously anonymous-namespace-local, not directly testable) found it initialized `phase` to `kMaxPhase` and SUBTRACTED present non-pawn material -- meaning a full-material starting position (subtracting the entirety of `kMaxPhase`'s own defining sum) computed `phase = 0`, and a bare, no-non-pawn-material endgame computed `phase = kMaxPhase`. `taper()` itself (`score.h`) is tested and correct (`eval_tests.cpp`'s own `"taper: phase kMaxPhase selects the mg term exactly"` / `"...phase 0 selects the eg term exactly"`), and resolves `phase==kMaxPhase` to each term's `mg` value, `phase==0` to `eg`. Combined, this meant every tapered term in the actually-running engine was blended using the OPPOSITE of its own documented mg/eg intent for the entire game: the true middlegame (phase, per this bug, computing near 0) got each term's `eg` weight, and the true endgame (phase, per this bug, computing near `kMaxPhase`) got each term's `mg` weight -- backwards for every single mg/eg design decision made across this whole phase (Mobility eval's `eg`-above-`mg` mobility bonus, King safety's `mg`-above-`eg` exposure terms, King tropism, Trapped piece penalties, and now Tempo bonus itself, all designed and documented assuming the correct, non-inverted direction).

**Why this went undetected across so many sessions:** `compute_phase()` was never exposed for direct testing (anonymous-namespace-local in `eval.cpp`, not declared in `eval.h`) -- every eval-term test file (per this codebase's own established convention, `eval.h`'s own doc comment: "keeps the function trivially testable independent of whose turn it is") deliberately tests each term's `Score{mg,eg}` pair in isolation rather than through the full tapered `evaluate()` pipeline, which is exactly the right general testing discipline but happened to leave nothing checking compute_phase()'s own output direction specifically. `taper()` itself being correctly tested gave a false sense that the whole tapering pipeline was covered.

**Fix:** `compute_phase()` now starts `phase` at 0 and ADDS each side's present non-pawn material (`phase += ...` for knight/bishop/rook/queen counts, matching the loop structure exactly but with the opposite accumulation direction), with the defensive clamp flipped to an upper bound (`phase > kMaxPhase ? kMaxPhase : phase`, since a promotion-heavy position can now only ever push phase UP past `kMaxPhase`, never negative the way the old subtract-based version could). Moved out of `eval.cpp`'s anonymous namespace and declared in `eval.h` specifically so its exact direction is now pinned directly by three new dedicated `eval_tests.cpp` cases (`compute_phase(start_position()) == kMaxPhase`; a bare-kings position `== 0`; removing one queen from the starting position decreases phase by exactly `kQueenPhase`), plus a fourth end-to-end regression case confirming a centralized White king now scores worse than a back-rank White king specifically at the full-material starting phase (previously-established `psqt_value()`-level `mg`-penalized/`eg`-rewarded centralization direction, now confirmed to actually apply in the right direction once blended through the real `evaluate()` pipeline).

**Why this is correct:** matches `eval.cpp`'s own pre-existing doc comment on `compute_phase()` verbatim ("kMaxPhase ... means 'fully middlegame,' 0 means 'fully endgame'" -- the comment was always right, only the code disagreed with it) and matches `taper()`'s own already-tested, unchanged semantics (`phase==kMaxPhase` selects `mg`, `phase==0` selects `eg`) -- the fix makes `compute_phase()`'s actual behavior agree with both of those already-correct, already-documented references rather than changing either of them.

**Test-suite impact:** `tests/eval_tests.cpp`'s pre-existing `"evaluate: starting position is exactly balanced"` test (`REQUIRE(evaluate(start_position()) == 0)`) needed updating -- not because of this phase fix directly (material/PSQT/pawn-structure/mobility/etc. all still cancel symmetrically at the starting position regardless of which mg/eg weight taper() picks, since White's and Black's own terms are mirror images of each other under EITHER weighting), but because Tempo bonus (2026-08-29 (3), landing in the same session) is the one term that does NOT cancel at the start -- White, to move, now scores exactly `kTempoBonus` there. Renamed/updated to assert `evaluate(start_position()) == taper(kTempoBonus, compute_phase(start_position()))` instead of a bare `0`, computed rather than hardcoded so it stays correct automatically if `kTempoBonus` or `compute_phase()` itself changes again. Every other file calling `evaluate()` directly (`pawn_tt_tests.cpp`, `quiescence_tests.cpp`) only asserts relative/consistency properties (with-cache equals without-cache; quiescence's leaf score matches a freshly-computed `evaluate()` call), never a hardcoded absolute value, and needed no changes -- confirmed by grepping every `tests/*.cpp` file for `evaluate(` calls before concluding the impact was fully scoped to `eval_tests.cpp` alone.

**Regression-bench impact -- flagged prominently, not treated as a normal incremental delta:** every prior session's `BENCH ... nodes=` figure in docs/SESSIONS.md (going back through this entire phase, at minimum since Tapered Eval's own introduction) was measured against the INVERTED tapering direction and is not a valid baseline for comparison going forward -- this is a correctness fix that changes the engine's actual move choices and search-tree shape at essentially every node with more than one legal reply to compare, not a bounded, narrow-trigger term addition the way Trapped piece penalties or Knight outposts were. The next real (CMake/Catch2/CI) `ctest -R bench -V` run's `TOTAL nodes=` figure should be treated as a fresh baseline, not compared against Session 50's `93629` or Session 52's `90967` in the usual "did this term's addition move the needle the expected amount" way.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Leaving `compute_phase()` anonymous-namespace-local and adding only an end-to-end `evaluate()`-level regression test (the "back-rank vs. centralized king" case) without also exposing/directly testing `compute_phase()` itself -- rejected: an indirect test alone leaves the exact function and direction responsible for a future regression less immediately locatable than a direct, dedicated test does, and `compute_phase()` is a small enough, genuinely reusable-elsewhere utility (a "what phase is this position in" query plausibly useful to future search-side phase-dependent logic, e.g. reduction/extension scaling) that exposing it costs nothing.
- Silently fixing this without a dedicated DECISIONS.md entry, on the theory that it's "just a bug fix" -- rejected given its scope: this affects every tapered term added across this entire phase's history simultaneously, not a single term's own behavior, and the regression-bench baseline discontinuity it causes needs to be discoverable by a future session reading docs/SESSIONS.md/DECISIONS.md, not silently absorbed into an unrelated feature's own entry.

---

## 2026-08-29 (1) — Trapped piece penalties (Phase 5, eighth item): zero-safe-mobility knight/bishop flat penalty, new eval/trapped_pieces.cpp/.h module

**Decision:** A new `trapped_piece_value()` (new files `src/eval/trapped_pieces.h`/`src/eval/trapped_pieces.cpp`) evaluates the standard CPW "Trapped Piece" concept for both sides and returns a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as a ninth uncached term. For each own knight/bishop, its "safe mobility" is computed as its pseudo-mobility bitboard (`attacks(sq) & ~own_occupied` — the same building block eval/mobility.h's `mobility_value()` already computes) with every square attacked by an enemy pawn additionally excluded (the reverse-pawn-attack trick eval/pawns.cpp/eval/space.cpp/eval/threats.cpp already establish, here computed once per side as the union of every enemy pawn's own attack set and reused for every own knight/bishop check). A piece with exactly zero safe squares takes a flat penalty: `kKnightTrappedPenalty = {-40, -55}`, `kBishopTrappedPenalty = {-50, -65}`. Only knights and bishops are scored — not rooks/queens.

**Rationale:** The eighth unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md) — CPW's own "Trapped Pieces" material (https://www.chessprogramming.org/Trapped_Pieces) centers on a minor piece that has lost all safe mobility to a closed pawn structure (the classic trapped-bishop-in-the-corner and stranded-rim-knight patterns), a real and often decisive structural weakness distinct from every other term added this phase.

**Why only knights and bishops, not also rooks/queens:** matches Knight outposts' own precedent (2026-08-27 (2)) of scoping a term to exactly the piece types its own name/source pattern describes rather than silently broadening it. CPW's own leading examples are specifically minor-piece patterns; a rook's or queen's much longer reach makes a genuine zero-safe-squares state vanishingly rare in practice and not a distinct pattern worth its own hand-estimated constant at this stage.

**Why only enemy PAWN attacks are excluded from safe mobility, not a full enemy attacks-by-side union (which eval/threats.cpp's own `attacks_by_side()` already computes for a related term):** a piece with every reachable square covered by some other enemy PIECE (not a pawn) is an ordinary, unremarkable state for an active piece in a contested middlegame — folding that into "trapped" would flag large numbers of normal, merely-contested pieces rather than the specific, much rarer pawn-hemmed-with-nowhere-to-go pattern CPW's own examples describe. Restricting to pawn attacks only also keeps this term self-contained (no dependency on threats.cpp's own precompute) and only needs one pass over each side's own pawns, not every enemy piece type.

**Why "exactly zero safe squares" rather than a low-but-nonzero threshold (e.g. "at most one safe square"):** a piece with truly zero safe squares is categorically different from one that merely has few options — it cannot move away from a future attack at all without either losing the piece outright or accepting a capture, a real structural weakness rather than a matter of degree. A softer threshold would also need its own separately hand-tuned cutoff with no obvious right value before a real tuner exists; the zero-mobility case needs no such tuning to justify — it's the plain, unambiguous CPW definition, and the simpler of the two reasonable options at this stage (same preference already established across every term this phase — see eval/mobility.h's own "flat bonus over a diminishing-returns table" reasoning, and eval/space.h's own "fixed zone over a dynamic one" reasoning).

**Why `eg` is set below `mg` for both constants (the same tapering direction as eval/mobility.h's own mobility bonus, the opposite direction from eval/king_safety.h's mg-heavy king-safety terms):** this term is the direct deficit-side mirror of mobility eval's own bonus — a piece with zero mobility is a proportionally larger fraction of a side's remaining force sitting completely idle the fewer pieces are left on the board, and the opponent has more time and a technically simpler task to eventually win it once fewer other threats are competing for attention. Bishop is penalized more than knight: CPW's own leading "Trapped Piece" example is specifically a cornered bishop, and a bishop's fixed-color diagonal gives it fewer tactical resources to eventually escape or be rescued than a knight typically has.

**What was built:** `src/eval/trapped_pieces.h`/`src/eval/trapped_pieces.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now also adds `trapped_piece_value(pos)`; `eval.h`'s doc comment updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/trapped_pieces.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/trapped_pieces_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests: starting-position zero; an exactly-zero-safe-squares trapped knight (corner knight with both its only two squares pawn-covered) checked via exact equality against `kKnightTrappedPenalty`; an exactly-zero-safe-squares trapped bishop (corner bishop walled in by an OWN pawn rather than an enemy one, deliberately confirming the term treats own-occupied and enemy-pawn-attacked squares identically as "unavailable," per the safe-mobility definition itself) checked against `kBishopTrappedPenalty`, plus confirming the two penalty constants are distinct; the exactly-one-safe-square boundary case scoring exactly zero, isolating the "exactly zero" rule from a softer threshold; and a Black-side test confirming the negated (White-relative) sign. `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `trapped_pieces_tests.cpp` added. Every `board::` function this file calls was individually cross-checked against the header that declares it (`board/attacks.h` for `bishop_attacks()`, `board/masks.h` for `knight_attacks()`/`pawn_attacks()`, `board/bitboard.h` for `kEmptyBitboard`/`pop_lsb()`, `board/board.h` for `opposite()`) before delivery, continuing the standing discipline reaffirmed in every eval-term entry since Mobility eval's own missing-include build failure (2026-08-26 (3)/Session 36) — this session additionally had real g++ access in-sandbox (see docs/SESSIONS.md, Session 51) and compiled every touched file plus a standalone driver exercising all five planned test cases against the real implementation, the first time this phase a term's expected values were confirmed by an actual compiled run rather than solely by hand-derivation.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Scoring rooks/queens too, for full CPW "Trapped Pieces" coverage — deferred; see "Why only knights and bishops" above; revisit if a future session finds a rook/queen pattern worth its own constant.
- A low-but-nonzero safe-mobility threshold (e.g. "at most one safe square is still trapped") — deferred; see "Why exactly zero safe squares" above.
- Excluding squares attacked by ANY enemy piece, not just pawns (a full `attacks_by_side()`-style union) — deferred; see "Why only enemy pawn attacks" above.
- A rank-restricted version (only counting a trapped piece if it's also deep in enemy territory, mirroring Knight outposts' own rank window) — deferred as unneeded first-cut complexity; a piece with zero safe squares anywhere on the board is already a real problem regardless of which rank it's stranded on, and CPW's own examples aren't themselves rank-restricted the way its "Outposts" article is.

---

## 2026-08-28 (3) — King tropism (Phase 5, seventh item): linear Chebyshev-distance falloff, reuses king_safety.h's own attack-unit weight scheme, new eval/king_tropism.cpp/.h module

**Decision:** A new `king_tropism_value()` (new files `src/eval/king_tropism.h`/`src/eval/king_tropism.cpp`) evaluates the standard CPW "King Tropism" concept for both sides and returns a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as an eighth uncached term. For each own knight/bishop/rook/queen, its Chebyshev (king-move) distance to the ENEMY king is computed; `proximity = kTropismMaxDistance(7) - distance` (0 at the maximum possible distance, up to 6 for a piece one square away); `units = piece_type_weight * proximity`; the piece's own contribution is `kTropismUnitBonus * units`. Piece-type weights are `Knight=1, Bishop=1, Rook=2, Queen=4` — **reused verbatim from eval/king_safety.h's own `kKnightAttackUnits`/`kBishopAttackUnits`/`kRookAttackUnits`/`kQueenAttackUnits`** (2026-08-26 (6)), not independently re-derived.

**Rationale:** The seventh unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md) — CPW's own "King Tropism" material (https://www.chessprogramming.org/King_Safety#Tropism) rewards a side's pieces for simply standing close to the enemy king, as a proxy for how easily they could be redeployed into an actual attack, distinct from (and not a duplicate of) eval/king_safety.h's existing attacker-weighting component.

**Why this doesn't duplicate eval/king_safety.h's own `attack_units_on()`/`kAttackUnitPenalty` component:** that component counts pieces that ALREADY attack at least one square of the immediate king zone RIGHT NOW — a piece two ranks away with another piece blocking its line scores nothing there, however close it stands. Tropism is deliberately the opposite kind of signal: a purely geometric distance measure that ignores blockers and lines of attack entirely. The two terms measure genuinely different things (current direct threat vs. general positional proximity/redeployment ease) and are meant to coexist, not replace one another — this is why `king_tropism_value()` doesn't need `board::init_magic_bitboards()` at all (no attack-pattern generation involved, just file/rank arithmetic on two squares), unlike `king_safety_value()`'s own attacker-weighting component, which does.

**Why the per-piece-type weights are reused verbatim from king_safety.h rather than independently chosen:** both terms are measuring closely related aspects of the same underlying "how dangerous are this side's pieces to the enemy king" question — a queen's outsized attacking potential relative to a knight's applies identically whether being measured by "does it currently attack the king zone" or "how close is it to the king zone" — reusing the same 1/1/2/4 ratio keeps the two terms' relative piece-type scaling consistent with each other rather than introducing an unexplained second, differently-tuned weighting scheme for what is conceptually the same value judgment.

**Why a linear distance-to-proximity formula rather than a per-distance lookup table:** matches this codebase's established "formula over a large tuned table" preference (mobility.h's/knight_outposts.h's own parallel reasoning) — a linear falloff (`kTropismMaxDistance - distance`) is the simplest reasonable first cut; a genuinely non-linear falloff curve (some published engines' own tropism tables aren't linear) is exactly the kind of refinement left for the eventual Texel tuner to help discover, not hand-guessed upfront.

**Why `chebyshev_distance()` is a local helper in king_tropism.cpp rather than a new board/bitboard.h function:** no other eval/*.cpp file in this codebase currently needs a general square-to-square distance function, so promoting it to a shared header for a single caller isn't warranted yet — the same "not worth promoting to a shared table/function for one caller" reasoning eval/king_safety.cpp's own shield_zone() and eval/space.cpp's own space_zone() already establish for their own single-purpose helpers.

**What was built:** `src/eval/king_tropism.h`/`src/eval/king_tropism.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now also adds `king_tropism_value(pos)`; `eval.h`'s doc comment updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/king_tropism.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/king_tropism_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests: starting-position symmetry (cancels regardless of the exact constants, via full mirror symmetry); a queen closer to the enemy king scoring more than the same queen at the maximum possible distance (which scores exactly zero); a queen scoring exactly 4x what a knight scores at the identical distance, confirming the reused weight ratio; a piece at the maximum possible board distance (opposite corners) contributing exactly zero, the formula's own boundary case; and an additive-stacking test confirming two different pieces' contributions sum rather than interact. `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `king_tropism_tests.cpp` added. Every `board::` function this file calls was individually cross-checked against the header that declares it (`board/bitboard.h` for `pop_lsb()`/`bitscan_forward()`/`file_of()`/`rank_of()`, `board/board.h` for `opposite()`) before delivery, continuing the standing discipline reaffirmed in every eval-term entry since Mobility eval's own missing-include build failure (2026-08-26 (3)/Session 36).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Independently re-deriving a separate piece-type weighting scheme rather than reusing king_safety.h's — deferred; see "Why the per-piece-type weights are reused verbatim" above.
- A non-linear (e.g. quadratic, or explicit per-distance lookup table) falloff curve — deferred; see "Why a linear distance-to-proximity formula" above, revisit once the Texel tuner exists.
- Promoting `chebyshev_distance()` to a shared board/bitboard.h function — deferred; see "Why chebyshev_distance() is a local helper" above; revisit if a second caller ever needs the same computation.
- Scoping tropism to only squares within some fixed radius (rather than a smooth falloff to zero at the maximum distance) — deferred as unnecessary added complexity; the linear formula already naturally reaches zero at `kTropismMaxDistance`, achieving the same practical "far-away pieces don't count" effect without a separate cutoff parameter.

---

## 2026-08-28 (2) — Threats evaluation (Phase 5, sixth item): pawn-attack penalty + boolean hanging-piece penalty, new eval/threats.cpp/.h module, restores the magic-bitboards precondition

**Decision:** A new `threats_value()` (new files `src/eval/threats.h`/`src/eval/threats.cpp`) evaluates two independent, stacking penalties for a side's own knights/bishops/rooks/queens (pawns and the king out of scope), for both sides, returning a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as a seventh uncached term: (1) `{kKnightAttackedByPawnPenalty, kBishopAttackedByPawnPenalty, kRookAttackedByPawnPenalty, kQueenAttackedByPawnPenalty}` applied unconditionally whenever a piece is currently attacked by an enemy pawn (regardless of defense); (2) `{kKnightHangingPenalty, kBishopHangingPenalty, kRookHangingPenalty, kQueenHangingPenalty}` applied whenever a piece is attacked by ANY enemy piece (pawn, minor, major, or king) and has no own defender at all — a deliberately simplified boolean "any attacker vs. any defender" test, not a Static-Exchange-Evaluation-accurate exchange simulation (see "Why a boolean check instead of reusing search/see.h" below). The two penalties stack additively when both conditions hold for the same piece.

**Rationale:** The sixth unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md) — CPW's general "Threats" material (https://www.chessprogramming.org/Threats) covers both patterns as among the most common and highest-value threat signals in practice: a pawn attacking a minor/major piece is a serious problem regardless of defense (the material trade already favors the attacker), and an outright undefended attacked piece is a straightforward hanging-piece detector.

**Why pawns and the king are excluded from being scored as the ATTACKED piece:** ROADMAP.md's own item wording ("hanging/attacked pieces, pieces attacked by pawns") is read here as referring to minor/major pieces specifically, matching how CPW's own material and most engines' Threats-style terms scope this concept — a hanging pawn is already substantially covered by eval/pawns.cpp's own isolated/backward/doubled-pawn terms and by material_value() itself the moment it's actually captured; a "hanging king" isn't a coherent eval-time concept at all (an attacked king is check, which search handles directly).

**Why a boolean attacked/defended check instead of reusing search/see.h's static_exchange_evaluation():** SEE already exists and is more precise (a full capture/recapture simulation rather than a flat any-attacker-vs-any-defender count), but it operates on a specific `board::Move` (a concrete hypothetical capture), and eval/ has no existing dependency on search/ anywhere in this codebase — search calls eval, not the reverse (ARCHITECTURE.md's own module layering). Adding an eval→search dependency here, to judge a piece that isn't actually being captured this turn, would be a new and backwards coupling for a refinement this first cut doesn't need; a simple union-of-attacks boolean check needs nothing beyond board/, matching every other eval/*.h term added this phase.

**Why the two penalties are independent and allowed to stack (rather than the pawn-attack penalty suppressing/replacing the hanging penalty, or vice versa, when both apply to the same piece):** the same "independent signals add" philosophy already established this phase — eval/piece_bonuses.h's own open-file + 7th-rank stacking (2026-08-27 (1)) and eval/space.h's own occupancy + attack stacking (2026-08-28 (1)) — a piece that's both pawn-attacked AND completely undefended is genuinely in worse trouble than either condition alone, and scoring it that way reflects that rather than flattening the two distinct signals into one.

**Why `attacks_by_side()` is computed once per side per call rather than per piece:** every one of the (up to 30) non-king, non-pawn... actually every piece on the board's attacked/defended status is checked against the SAME two bitboards (white's full attack set, black's full attack set) — computing each side's attack union exactly once and reusing it for every piece avoids recomputing the same sliding-piece attack calls repeatedly, the same "compute once, reuse across every relevant check" principle eval/king_safety.cpp's own king-zone precompute already follows.

**Why this restores the `board::init_magic_bitboards()` precondition (unlike the three most recently added terms):** unlike eval/piece_bonuses.h's piece_bonus_value(), eval/knight_outposts.h's knight_outpost_value(), and eval/space.h's space_value() (2026-08-27 (1)/2026-08-27 (2)/2026-08-28 (1), none of which touch a sliding-piece attack function), threats_value() needs the full attack pattern of every bishop/rook/queen on the board to determine attacked/defended status — the same precondition eval/mobility.h's mobility_value() and eval/king_safety.h's king_safety_value() already carry. This isn't a regression or an inconsistency; each eval term's own precondition is documented individually and simply reflects what that specific term's own logic actually needs.

**What was built:** `src/eval/threats.h`/`src/eval/threats.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now also adds `threats_value(pos)`; `eval.h`'s doc comment updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/threats.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/threats_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests: starting-position zero; a pawn-attacked-but-rook-defended knight isolating the pawn-attack penalty alone (checked via exact equality against the named constant, `kKnightAttackedByPawnPenalty`, not just an ordering comparison); an undefended-knight-attacked-by-a-rook test isolating the hanging penalty alone (`kKnightHangingPenalty`); a combined test confirming the two stack additively (`kKnightAttackedByPawnPenalty + kKnightHangingPenalty`); and a test confirming a defended piece is never flagged as hanging even while visibly under attack. `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `threats_tests.cpp` added. Every `board::` function this file calls was individually cross-checked against the header that declares it (`board/attacks.h` for `rook_attacks()`/`bishop_attacks()`/`queen_attacks()`, `board/masks.h` for `knight_attacks()`/`king_attacks()`/`pawn_attacks()`, `board/bitboard.h` for `kEmptyBitboard`/`pop_lsb()`/`bitscan_forward()`/`test_bit()`, `board/board.h` for `opposite()`) before delivery, continuing the standing discipline reaffirmed in every eval-term entry since Mobility eval's own missing-include build failure (2026-08-26 (3)/Session 36).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Reusing `search::static_exchange_evaluation()` from `search/see.h` for a more accurate hanging-piece judgment — deferred; see "Why a boolean check instead of reusing search/see.h" above.
- Scoring hanging pawns too, not just minor/major pieces — deferred; see "Why pawns and the king are excluded" above.
- Weighting the hanging penalty by the VALUE of the specific attacker (e.g. a queen attacked by a pawn with no defender is worse than a queen attacked by another queen with no defender, since the former is an even more one-sided trade) — deferred as an additional refinement beyond this first cut; the current boolean "any attacker, any defender" test doesn't distinguish attacker/defender value at all beyond the attacked PIECE's own type (which the penalty already scales by), left for the eventual Texel tuner or a later refinement to revisit.
- Having the pawn-attack penalty suppress the hanging penalty when both apply (to avoid what might look like double-counting the same pawn's threat) — deferred; see "Why the two penalties are independent and allowed to stack" above.

---

## 2026-08-28 (1) — Space evaluation (Phase 5, fifth item): flat per-safe-square bonus over a fixed c-f/relative-rank-2-4 zone, new eval/space.cpp/.h module, mg-only taper

**Decision:** A new `space_value()` (new files `src/eval/space.h`/`src/eval/space.cpp`) evaluates the standard CPW "Space" concept for both sides and returns a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as a sixth uncached term. For each side, a fixed 12-square "space zone" (files c through f, relative ranks 2–4 — the three ranks just ahead of that side's own back rank) is scanned; a zone square counts if it is NOT occupied by that side's own pawn and NOT attacked by an enemy pawn (using the same reverse-pawn-attack trick established in eval/pawns.cpp and reused in eval/knight_outposts.cpp). Each qualifying square scores a flat `kSpaceSquareBonus = {2, 0}` — **`eg` is exactly zero**, the first term this phase where one taper component is fully absent rather than merely smaller; every other tapered constant added this phase (mobility, king safety, piece bonuses, knight outposts) keeps both components nonzero.

**Rationale:** The fifth unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md) — CPW "Space" (https://www.chessprogramming.org/Space) rewards controlling more central squares as a proxy for how much room a side's pieces have to maneuver, a real positional factor distinct from mobility eval's own per-piece square-count (mobility measures what pieces CAN currently reach; space measures which central squares are structurally safe/controlled regardless of whether any piece is actually using them right now).

**Why `eg` is exactly zero, not just smaller (unlike every other tapered term this phase):** CPW's own "Space" article frames the concept specifically as a middlegame consideration — room to maneuver only matters when there are pieces left to maneuver and central squares still worth contesting; by the endgame, with most pieces traded off, controlling an extra central square doesn't meaningfully cramp an opponent who has little left to cramp. Some engines instead scale a space bonus continuously by the current non-pawn piece count (a smoother version of the same underlying idea); `eval::taper()`'s existing mg/eg blend (already computed from `compute_phase()`) was judged the simpler equivalent for a first cut — an eg value of exactly zero already produces the same practical "fades out as material thins" behavior taper() would apply to a smaller-but-nonzero eg value anyway, without needing a second, separate piece-count-based scaling factor layered on top of it.

**Why the zone is fixed at files c-f / relative ranks 2-4 rather than computed dynamically (e.g. only counting squares actually behind a friendly pawn, a real Stockfish-style refinement):** matches this codebase's established preference for the simpler of two reasonable options at this stage (see eval/mobility.h's own "Why a flat per-square bonus" entry, and eval/knight_outposts.h's parallel reasoning for its own rank window) — every zone square that's safe and pawn-free counts equally toward this first cut, rather than weighting squares differently by whether a friendly pawn specifically shields them; whether that refinement is worth its added complexity is left for the eventual Texel tuner to help decide.

**Why occupancy by a friendly PAWN disqualifies a square but occupancy by any other own piece doesn't:** a pawn standing on a zone square is already fully scored via material/PSQT/pawn structure — counting it again as "controlled space" on top of that would double-count the same pawn's own presence; a knight or bishop parked on a zone square, by contrast, isn't itself what "space" is measuring (the square being safe and available is the thing being rewarded, independent of whether some other piece happens to be sitting there right now).

**Why `space_zone()` is computed on the fly per call rather than precomputed into a masks.h table:** the zone only ever depends on `Color c`, not on a specific square the way passed_pawn_mask()/backward_support_mask() do (masks.h's own genuinely per-square, reused-across-many-callers tables) — same "not worth promoting to a shared table for a single-purpose, color-only-keyed zone" reasoning eval/king_safety.cpp's own shield_zone() already established (2026-08-26 (6)).

**What was built:** `src/eval/space.h`/`src/eval/space.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now also adds `space_value(pos)`; `eval.h`'s doc comment updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/space.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/space_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests: a bare board (kings only) exactly balanced (12 safe squares each side, cancels); the starting position exactly balanced (8 safe squares each side, own back-rank-adjacent pawns removing 4 apiece, cancels via a different route than the bare-board case); an own-pawn-occupied zone square removing exactly one square from that side's own count (`Score{-2,0}` against the bare-board baseline); an enemy pawn attacking an otherwise-empty zone square from OUTSIDE either side's own zone (isolating the attack mechanism from any own-occupancy side effect) producing the identical `Score{-2,0}`; and a combined test confirming the two independent mechanisms (own-pawn occupancy, enemy-pawn attack) stack additively to `Score{-4,0}` rather than double-counting or interacting. `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `space_tests.cpp` added. Every `board::` function this file calls was individually cross-checked against the header that declares it (`board/bitboard.h` for `kEmptyBitboard`/`set_bit`/`test_bit`/`make_square`/`pop_lsb`, `board/masks.h` for `pawn_attacks()`, `board/board.h` for `opposite()`) before delivery, including explicitly confirming `pop_lsb()`'s `int` return type assigns cleanly to a `Square` (itself a `using Square = int` alias, per board/bitboard.h) — continuing the standing discipline reaffirmed in every eval-term entry since Mobility eval's own missing-include build failure (2026-08-26 (3)/Session 36).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Scaling the space bonus by current non-pawn piece count directly, rather than relying on `eg=0` and the existing mg/eg taper — deferred; see "Why `eg` is exactly zero" above.
- Restricting counted squares to those specifically shielded by a friendly pawn one or two ranks back (a real Stockfish-style refinement) — deferred; see "Why the zone is fixed" above.
- A dynamically-sized zone (e.g. widening toward the flanks based on where the enemy king castled) — deferred as unnecessary complexity for a first cut; CPW's own standard c-f/ranks-2-4 zone is the well-established baseline version of this concept.
- Precomputing `space_zone()` into a masks.h-style lookup table rather than computing it per call — deferred; see "Why `space_zone()` is computed on the fly" above; only 2 possible values (one per color) exist, so a table would save negligible work over the direct computation.

---

## 2026-08-27 (2) — Knight outposts (Phase 5, fourth item): pawn-defended, pawn-unreachable, rank-windowed bonus, new eval/knight_outposts.cpp/.h module

**Decision:** A new `knight_outpost_value()` (new files `src/eval/knight_outposts.h`/`src/eval/knight_outposts.cpp`) evaluates a flat bonus (`kKnightOutpostBonus = {18,10}`) for each own knight standing on a qualifying "outpost" square, for both sides, returning a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as a fifth uncached term. A square qualifies if all three hold: (1) it's defended by a friendly pawn (the reverse-pawn-attack trick eval/pawns.cpp's own "Connected" check already established: `pawn_attacks(them, sq) & own_pawns`); (2) no enemy pawn currently on, or still able to advance onto, either of the square's two adjacent files at or ahead of its own rank could ever capture there (computed by reusing `board::passed_pawn_mask(c, sq) & board::adjacent_files_mask(file_of(sq))` intersected with enemy pawns, rather than adding a new masks.h function — passed_pawn_mask's own existing "strictly ahead of sq" span already covers exactly the ranks a threatening pawn could occupy, on any of the three same/adjacent files; ANDing with adjacent_files_mask narrows that to the two files a pawn CAPTURE (not block) actually depends on); (3) the square's relative rank (from the knight's own color's perspective) falls in [3,5] (ranks 4–6), the standard "in or near enemy territory" restriction. Only knights are scored — not bishops, matching ROADMAP.md's own item name exactly rather than silently broadening scope.

**Rationale:** The fourth unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md) — CPW "Outposts" (https://www.chessprogramming.org/Outposts) is a well-established hand-crafted-eval term rewarding a knight that can't be dislodged by a pawn and persistently cramps the opponent's position, distinct from (and additive to) the generic square-count mobility eval/mobility.h already scores — mobility measures raw reach, outposts measure a specific strategic pattern mobility's own flat per-square bonus doesn't capture on its own.

**Why the enemy-pawn-threat check reuses `passed_pawn_mask()`/`adjacent_files_mask()` rather than adding a new masks.h function:** both are already exactly the right shape — passed_pawn_mask(c, sq) already returns "every square on sq's file and both neighbors, strictly ahead of sq from c's own perspective," which is precisely the span a threatening enemy pawn would have to occupy; the only adjustment needed is excluding sq's own file (since a pawn can only ever CAPTURE diagonally, never straight ahead, so a pawn on the knight's own file — however far along it — can never take the knight), which adjacent_files_mask() already provides directly. No new precomputed table needed, unlike king_safety.cpp's own ad-hoc shield_zone() (2026-08-26 (6)), which had no existing masks.h function to compose from.

**Why the outpost rank window is [3,5] (relative ranks 4–6) rather than every rank, or a wider/narrower window:** CPW's own "Outposts" article restricts the concept to squares in or near enemy territory — a knight technically meeting the pawn-defended/pawn-unreachable test on its own back rank isn't exerting the forward pressure an outpost bonus is meant to reward. [3,5] is a first-draft hand estimate of that window's width, not yet Texel-tuned, same caveat every other eval term added this phase already carries; a narrower or wider window is exactly the kind of thing the eventual tuner (a later Phase 5 item) can help settle empirically.

**Why `kKnightOutpostBonus` is `mg`-heavier, unlike eval/mobility.h's own `eg`-at-or-above-`mg` constants:** an outpost knight's value is strategic leverage — a persistent, hard-to-remove piece that cramps the opponent and anchors attacks involving OTHER pieces — which has more to work with while more material remains on the board, closer in spirit to eval/king_safety.h's own mg-heavy reasoning (2026-08-26 (6)) than to mobility's eg-heavy one, though for a distinct underlying cause (battlefield leverage over other pieces, not exposure/danger fading as the king becomes safer late).

**Why only knights, not also bishops (some engines score both):** matches ROADMAP.md's own item wording ("Knight outposts") exactly; a bishop-outpost bonus, if ever wanted, is scoped as a separate future roadmap addition rather than silently folded into this item.

**Why this gets its own standalone file rather than joining eval/piece_bonuses.h's grouping:** unlike piece_bonuses.h's three terms (each individually too small — a handful of lines — to justify a dedicated translation unit, 2026-08-27 (1)), knight-outpost detection's own logic (a defended-square check plus a two-mask enemy-threat-span check plus a rank-window check) is comparable in size to Mobility's or King safety's own single term, so it gets the same standalone-file treatment those two already have, not folded in with piece_bonuses.h's smaller, more numerous group.

**What was built:** `src/eval/knight_outposts.h`/`src/eval/knight_outposts.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now also adds `knight_outpost_value(pos)`; `eval.h`'s doc comment updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/knight_outposts.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/knight_outposts_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests: starting-position zero (both sides' knights outside the rank window); a pawn-defended, unreachable e5 knight scoring exactly `{18,10}` against the identical undefended knight scoring `{0,0}`; an enemy pawn one rank ahead on an adjacent file disqualifying an otherwise-defended outpost; a defended, unreachable knight one rank short of the window scoring `{0,0}`; and a qualifying Black knight producing the expected negative White-relative `{-18,-10}`, confirming the sign convention. `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `knight_outposts_tests.cpp` added. Every `board::` function this file calls was individually cross-checked against the header that declares it (`board/masks.h` for `pawn_attacks()`/`passed_pawn_mask()`/`adjacent_files_mask()`, `board/bitboard.h` for `file_of()`/`rank_of()`/`pop_lsb()`, `board/board.h` for `opposite()`) before delivery, continuing the standing discipline reaffirmed in every eval-term entry since Mobility eval's own missing-include build failure (2026-08-26 (3)/Session 36).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Also crediting bishops with a smaller outpost bonus — deferred; see "Why only knights" above.
- A wider or narrower outpost rank window than [3,5] — deferred; see "Why the outpost rank window is [3,5]" above, revisit once the Texel tuner exists.
- A "potential outpost" bonus for empty qualifying squares a knight could reach in a move or two (some engines score this as a smaller separate incentive to maneuver a knight toward one) — deferred as an additional refinement beyond this first cut, not implemented; only an ALREADY-occupied outpost square is scored, matching this codebase's established preference for the simpler first cut before a real tuner exists.
- A new masks.h function dedicated to the enemy-pawn-threat span — deferred; see "Why the enemy-pawn-threat check reuses" above, since existing functions already compose into exactly the needed shape.

---

## 2026-08-27 (1) — Bishop pair / rook-on-open-or-semi-open-file / rook-on-7th-rank (Phase 5, third item): grouped into new eval/piece_bonuses.cpp/.h module

**Decision:** A new `piece_bonus_value()` (new files `src/eval/piece_bonuses.h`/`src/eval/piece_bonuses.cpp`) evaluates three classic positional bonuses for both sides and returns a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as a fourth uncached term alongside material/PSQT/pawn structure/mobility/king safety: (1) a flat bishop-pair bonus (`kBishopPairBonus = {20,40}`) whenever a side still has both bishops on the board; (2) a per-rook bonus for sitting on a file with no own pawn — `kRookOpenFileBonus = {24,12}` if the enemy has no pawn there either (fully open), `kRookSemiOpenFileBonus = {12,6}` if the enemy still does (semi-open); (3) a per-rook bonus (`kRookOnSeventhBonus = {10,20}`) for sitting on the relative 7th rank, applied as a flat condition (no additional check for enemy king placement or enemy pawns actually present to attack). All three constants are hand-estimated, not yet Texel-tuned, same caveat every other eval term added this phase already carries. The two rook components are independent per-rook additive terms — a rook that is both on an open file and on the 7th rank at once collects both bonuses, not just the larger of the two.

**Rationale:** The third unchecked Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md), grouped into one module per Session 39's own next-start-point guidance (2026-08-26 (6) entry below): all three are simple, well-established CPW positional bonuses (Bishop Pair, Rook on Open File, Rook on Seventh Rank) that don't individually warrant their own dedicated translation unit the way mobility/king safety/pawn structure each do (those three are each meaningfully larger — multiple sub-components, in king safety's and pawn structure's case, or a nontrivial per-piece-type weight table, in mobility's) — three short, independent, same-shaped positional checks are a natural structural fit for one shared file, the same "handful of simple additive constants" philosophy this codebase already applies within each individual term.

**Why bishop pair is `eg`-heavier, mirroring mobility.h's own taper direction:** a bishop's value is fundamentally about reach/mobility, and two bishops together on both square colors gain more from an opening board (fewer pieces blocking long diagonals) than they lose — same underlying "fewer pieces, more room to matter" logic mobility.h's own header comment already documents for the opposite-signed reason king_safety.h's constants go the other way.

**Why the open/semi-open-file bonuses are `mg`-heavier, mirroring (in reverse) king_safety.h's own file-penalty taper direction:** a rook's own open file and the exact same open file penalized from the opposing king's side (king_safety.h's `kOpenFileNearKingPenalty`/`kSemiOpenFileNearKingPenalty`) are literally the same board feature seen from two directions — the attacking rook gets more immediate value from it in the middlegame, while more enemy pieces are still on the board to be attacked along that file, for the identical underlying reason the defending king finds that same file more dangerous then too.

**Why the 7th-rank bonus doesn't require the enemy king on its own back rank or enemy pawns actually present on the rank (both real refinements several engines make):** matches this codebase's established preference for the simpler of two reasonable options at this stage before a real tuner exists (see mobility.h's own "Why a flat per-square bonus" entry, 2026-08-26 (3), for the parallel reasoning) — a rook loose on the 7th rank is a real asset even before either extra condition is checked; whether the added conditions are worth their complexity is left for the eventual Texel tuner to help decide.

**Why piece_bonus_value() doesn't need `board::init_magic_bitboards()`** (unlike mobility_value()/king_safety_value(), both of which do): every check in this function is static file/rank membership (`board::file_mask()`, `board::file_of()`, `board::rank_of()`) — none of it calls a sliding-piece attack function, so it has a strictly weaker precondition than its two sibling terms; documented explicitly in piece_bonuses.h so a future caller doesn't assume it needs the same setup as every other eval/*.h term.

**What was built:** `src/eval/piece_bonuses.h`/`src/eval/piece_bonuses.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now also adds `piece_bonus_value(pos)`; `eval.h`'s doc comment updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/piece_bonuses.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/piece_bonuses_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests: starting-position symmetry (bishop pair cancels between sides, no rook is on an open/semi-open file or its own 7th rank); bishop pair outscoring a single bishop; a strict open > semi-open > closed file ordering for a rook; a 7th-rank rook outscoring the same rook off the 7th rank; and an additive-stacking test confirming a rook that's both on an open file and the 7th rank collects both bonuses rather than just the larger one. `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `piece_bonuses_tests.cpp` added. Every `board::` function this file calls was individually cross-checked against the header that declares it (`board/masks.h` for `file_mask()`, `board/bitboard.h` for `file_of()`/`rank_of()`/`pop_lsb()`/`popcount()`, `board/board.h` for `opposite()`) before delivery, continuing the standing discipline established after Mobility eval's own missing-include build failure (2026-08-26 (3)/Session 36's "Bugs fixed" item 1, reaffirmed again in King safety's own 2026-08-26 (6) entry below).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Three separate files (`bishop_pair.h`/`.cpp`, `rook_files.h`/`.cpp`, `rook_seventh.h`/`.cpp`) instead of one shared module — deferred; each term individually is too small (a handful of lines) to justify its own translation unit and CMakeLists entry, unlike mobility/king safety/pawn structure's own genuinely larger scope.
- Requiring the enemy king on its own back rank (or enemy pawns present on the 7th rank) before applying `kRookOnSeventhBonus` — deferred; see "Why the 7th-rank bonus doesn't require" above.
- A `Score[max_rook_count]` diminishing-returns table for connected/doubled rooks on the same open file (some engines add an extra bonus specifically for two rooks sharing an open file) — deferred as an additional refinement beyond this first cut, not implemented; each rook's own open-file bonus already stacks additively if two rooks happen to share a file, so doubled rooks on an open file are NOT left unrewarded, just not given an EXTRA specific bonus beyond the sum of their two individual ones.

---

## 2026-08-26 (6) — King safety (Phase 5, second item): pawn shield + open/semi-open files + attacker weighting, new eval/king_safety.cpp/.h module

**Decision:** A new `king_safety_value()` (new files `src/eval/king_safety.h`/`src/eval/king_safety.cpp`) evaluates king safety for both sides and returns a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as a third uncached term alongside material/PSQT/pawn structure/mobility.
