Three additive components per side: (1) a flat bonus (`kShieldPawnBonus = {6,1}`) per own pawn found in a 3-file-wide (king's file ± 1), 2-rank-deep zone directly in front of the king; (2) a penalty per file, among the king's own file and its two neighbors, that has no own pawn on it anywhere — `kOpenFileNearKingPenalty = {-24,-4}` if the enemy has no pawn there either (fully open), `kSemiOpenFileNearKingPenalty = {-12,-2}` if the enemy still does (semi-open); (3) a penalty (`kAttackUnitPenalty = {-6,-1}`) per weighted "attack unit," summed over every enemy knight/bishop/rook/queen that attacks at least one square of the king's immediate zone (its own square plus every square `board::king_attacks()` returns), weighted by piece type (knight/bishop 1, rook 2, queen 4 — first-draft hand estimates loosely following relative material value, not copied from any specific engine's own published attack-unit table). All three components are hand-estimated, not yet Texel-tuned, same caveat every other eval term added this phase already carries.

**Rationale:** The second Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md) — king safety (CPW "King Safety": https://www.chessprogramming.org/King_Safety) is, alongside mobility, one of the most standard hand-crafted-eval terms, and the natural next step immediately after Mobility eval (2026-08-26 (3)) both structurally (same file-per-term convention, same "flat hand-estimated constants" philosophy) and because king exposure and piece mobility are the two classic sides of the same "how alive is this position" evaluation coin.

**Why every constant is MG-heavy/EG-light — the OPPOSITE tapering direction from Mobility eval's own constants:** king safety and mobility are the same kind of term (both scale with how active/exposed pieces are) but move in opposite directions as material comes off the board. Mobility.h's own header comment already documents "`eg` at or above `mg` throughout" for exactly the same reason phrased the other way: fewer pieces means more open lines and more room for the pieces that remain to matter more, which is good for mobility but bad for king safety specifically because those same open lines are what a king needs pawn cover and piece proximity to defend against. A king in the endgame, with queens and rooks traded off, typically has far less to fear from the same "open file" or "exposed zone" that would be dangerous in the middlegame — and per eval/psqt.h's own already-established king centralization terms (mg-penalized, eg-rewarded), an active king late in the game is often an asset, not a liability. `eval::taper()` (already used by every other term) handles this fade-out automatically once each constant's `eg` component is set low relative to `mg`, with no special-cased "only in the middlegame" branch needed anywhere in king_safety.cpp itself.

**Why the shield zone is computed ad hoc in king_safety.cpp rather than added as a new board/masks.h function:** unlike `passed_pawn_mask()`/`backward_support_mask()` (masks.h) — genuinely reused across many (color, square) combinations during pawn structure evaluation, and precomputed once at `init_masks()` time as 64-entry tables for exactly that reason — the king's own shield zone is only ever needed here, for exactly one square per side per `king_safety_value()` call. Promoting it to a shared, precomputed table would add startup-time population cost and a permanent 128-entry (64 squares × 2 colors) table for a computation this function already does in a handful of cheap integer comparisons per call, with no other caller to amortize that cost against.

**Why attacker weighting is binary per piece (does it reach the zone at all) rather than counting how many zone squares each piece attacks:** matches Mobility eval's own established preference for the simpler of two reasonable options at this stage (see 2026-08-26 (3)'s own "Why a flat per-square bonus" entry for the parallel reasoning) — a piece either meaningfully threatens the king's surroundings or it doesn't; how many of the (at most 9) zone squares it happens to also cover is a secondary, more marginal signal not worth the added complexity before a real tuner can show whether it's worth tracking.

**Why pawns don't participate in attacker weighting (only knight/bishop/rook/queen do):** same exclusion this codebase already applies consistently — Mobility eval's own knight/bishop/rook/queen-only scope (2026-08-26 (3)) — kept for consistency rather than re-litigated per term; an enemy pawn's own threat to a king's position is already captured indirectly through the open/semi-open-file component (a pawn storm opening a file is exactly what that component penalizes) rather than needing its own separate attacker-weighting entry.

**What was built:** `src/eval/king_safety.h`/`src/eval/king_safety.cpp` (new). `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now also adds `king_safety_value(pos)`; `eval.h`'s doc comment updated. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/king_safety.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/king_safety_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests: starting-position symmetry; an intact pawn shield outscoring a bare king; a strict closed > semi-open > fully-open file ordering; an enemy queen bearing down on the zone being penalized relative to one that isn't; a defensive test confirming a hand-built position missing a king entirely doesn't crash (`bitscan_forward()`'s undefined-behavior-on-empty precondition, guarded against explicitly in `king_safety.cpp`). `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `king_safety_tests.cpp` added. Every function this file calls was individually cross-checked against the specific header that declares it (`board/masks.h` for `king_attacks()`/`knight_attacks()`/`file_mask()`, `board/attacks.h` for the sliding-piece functions, `board/bitboard.h` for the rest) before delivery — the direct, deliberate lesson from Mobility eval's own missing-include build failure (2026-08-26 (3)/Session 36's "Bugs fixed" item 1), not assumed safe by similarity to mobility.cpp's already-correct pattern alone.

**Alternatives considered:**
- A per-count attack-unit lookup table (e.g. a nonlinear penalty curve keyed on total weighted attack units, the way several well-known engines' own king-safety formulas work) instead of a flat linear penalty — deferred for the same reason Mobility eval deferred its own per-count table: more constants to hand-guess correctly before a real tuner exists, for expressiveness this codebase can't yet validate is worth it.
- Including pawn attacks/pawn storms as their own dedicated attacker-weighting entry rather than relying on the open-file component alone to capture that threat indirectly — deferred; see "Why pawns don't participate" above. Revisit if the Texel tuner (a later Phase 5 item) suggests the indirect coverage is insufficient.
- A precomputed, masks.h-resident shield-zone table — deferred; see "Why the shield zone is computed ad hoc" above.
- Scaling the attacker-weighting penalty by how many DIFFERENT king-zone squares each piece attacks, rather than a flat per-attacking-piece amount — deferred; see "Why attacker weighting is binary" above.

---

## 2026-08-26 (3) — Mobility eval (Phase 5, first item): flat per-square-per-piece-type bonus, new eval/mobility.cpp/.h module

**Decision:** A new `mobility_value()` (new files `src/eval/mobility.h`/`src/eval/mobility.cpp`) evaluates knight/bishop/rook/queen mobility for both sides and returns a single White-relative `Score`, wired into `eval::evaluate()` (`eval.cpp`) as an uncached term added alongside material/PSQT/pawn structure. For each qualifying piece, it computes that piece's attacked-square bitboard (`board::knight_attacks()`/`bishop_attacks()`/`rook_attacks()`/`queen_attacks()`), masks out squares occupied by a piece of the SAME color, and multiplies the resulting popcount by a flat, piece-type-specific `Score` bonus (`kKnightMobilityBonus = {4,4}`, `kBishopMobilityBonus = {5,5}`, `kRookMobilityBonus = {2,4}`, `kQueenMobilityBonus = {1,2}` — first-draft hand estimates, explicitly not yet Texel-tuned, same caveat eval/pawns.h's own header comment already carries for its own constants). Pawns and the king are excluded — pawns are already scored via material + Pawn structure (eval/pawns.h); king activity/safety is Phase 5's own separate, not-yet-implemented item, deliberately not folded into this generic term.

**Rationale:** The first Phase 5 item (docs/ROADMAP.md) — mobility (CPW "Mobility": https://www.chessprogramming.org/Mobility) is one of the most standard, well-established hand-crafted-eval terms across engines, rewarding pieces with more available squares (more options, more potential to create threats) over cramped ones, independent of material or static piece placement (PSQT) alone.

**Why a flat per-square bonus, not a diminishing-returns table indexed by mobility count:** the more elaborate approach (a `Score[max_count]` lookup table per piece type, so e.g. a rook's 2nd attacked square is worth more than its 12th) is what several well-known engines use, and is more expressive — but it's also a meaningfully larger set of tunable constants for a codebase whose own stated Phase 5 plan is "hand estimates now, Texel-tune everything once there are enough terms to be worth tuning." A flat bonus keeps this first cut consistent with eval/pawns.h's own established preference (a handful of simple additive constants, not a large table) for exactly the same reason: fewer constants to hand-guess correctly before a real tuner exists, and a smaller, easier-to-reason-about diff for this specific PR. Revisit once the tuner (a later Phase 5 item) is in place and can show whether the added expressiveness of a full table is worth its complexity.

**Why "pseudo-mobility" (counts enemy-occupied attacked squares too), not "safe mobility" excluding squares an enemy pawn attacks:** same reasoning — Stockfish-style "safe mobility" is a real, standard refinement, but it requires computing every enemy pawn's own attack set first (extra work, extra logic) for a benefit that's speculative without tuning data to confirm it matters here. The basic CPW pseudo-mobility definition (any square the piece attacks, minus squares blocked by its own side) was judged the appropriately-scoped first cut.

**Why the king is excluded even though it technically has "mobility" too:** folding king mobility into this generic term would conflate two very different concepts a real engine wants to reason about separately — a king with MORE attacked squares in the middlegame is often a king in more DANGER (exposed), the opposite of what "more mobility is good" means for every other piece type; king safety needs its own dedicated, differently-signed evaluation (ROADMAP.md's own next Phase 5 item), not a shared generic mobility bonus that would reward exactly the wrong thing in the phase it matters most.

**A real, load-bearing gap this surfaced in existing tests, fixed the same session:** `mobility_value()` is the first eval term in this codebase to call a sliding-piece attack function (`board::bishop_attacks()`/`rook_attacks()`/`queen_attacks()`) from inside `evaluate()` itself — material/PSQT/pawn structure never needed magic-bitboard lookups. `tests/eval_tests.cpp` and `tests/pawn_tt_tests.cpp` had several `TEST_CASE`s calling `evaluate()` on positions with bishops/rooks/queens on the board WITHOUT ever calling `board::init_magic_bitboards()` (some called no init function at all; one called only `init_masks()`) — this was silently safe before this session only because no earlier eval term actually read the magic-bitboard tables, so the missing initialization never mattered in practice, despite `evaluate()`'s own documented precondition (this session's own update to eval.h's doc comment makes this explicit going forward) already implying it should have been called anyway. Every affected `TEST_CASE` was updated to call `init_magic_bitboards()` (via each file's own `init_all()` helper, matching search_tests.cpp's/perft_tests.cpp's established convention, or an inline call where a file-level helper didn't already exist) before its `evaluate()` call. `ordering_tests.cpp` and `bench_tests.cpp` were checked and already did this correctly (they exercise search functionality, which already needed magic bitboards for move generation regardless); `quiescence_tests.cpp` likewise already correct.

**What was built:** `src/eval/mobility.h`/`src/eval/mobility.cpp` (new). `src/eval/score.h` — new `Score::operator*(int)` (scales both `mg`/`eg` by a scalar), used to turn "N attacked squares" into "N × this piece type's per-square bonus" without hand-expanding the multiplication at every call site. `src/eval/eval.h`/`src/eval/eval.cpp` — `evaluate()` now adds `mobility_value(pos)` into its returned tapered score, uncached (unlike pawn structure — see mobility.cpp's own header comment on why no analogous hash table exists for this term); `eval.h`'s doc comment gained an explicit precondition note (`init_magic_bitboards()` now genuinely required, not just incidentally true via other subsystems). `src/CMakeLists.txt` — `eval/mobility.cpp` added to `nightwing_lib`'s sources. `tests/mobility_tests.cpp` (new) — dedicated, isolated tests for the term itself (starting-position symmetry, king-only-position zero contribution, center-vs-corner knight, open-vs-boxed-in rook, pseudo-mobility correctly counting an enemy-occupied edge square). `tests/eval_tests.cpp` — gained a proper `init_all()` (`init_masks()`, `init_magic_bitboards()`, `init_zobrist_keys()`) called by every existing `TEST_CASE`, fixing the latent gap above; file header comment updated to mention mobility.h. `tests/pawn_tt_tests.cpp` — both `evaluate()`-touching `TEST_CASE`s gained `init_magic_bitboards()` calls, with a comment on each explaining why (one genuinely needs it for this session's reason; the other doesn't for its specific FEN, but gained it anyway for consistency/future-proofing). `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `mobility_tests.cpp` added. `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` — Module Layout section gained `eval/mobility.cpp/.h` (and, noticed missing while there, `eval/pawn_tt.cpp/.h`, which already existed in the repo but wasn't listed).

**Alternatives considered:**
- The diminishing-returns per-count lookup table — deferred; see "Why a flat per-square bonus" above.
- Stockfish-style "safe mobility" (excluding enemy-pawn-attacked squares) — deferred; see "Why pseudo-mobility" above.
- A pawn-hash-table-style cache for mobility, keyed on some cheap-to-compute hash of non-pawn piece placement — rejected: unlike pawn structure, which changes on a small minority of moves (only pawn moves and captures-of-pawns touch it) and has a natural, already-existing incremental Zobrist-style key (`board::compute_pawn_hash()`), non-pawn piece placement changes on the large majority of moves, so a cache here would have a near-100% miss rate — all cost, no benefit — and there's no existing incremental key for "non-pawn piece placement" to reuse the way pawn structure already had one lying around.
- Including the king with a small, separately-signed mobility term right now, ahead of the dedicated King safety item — rejected: king "mobility" isn't a coherent standalone concept the way it is for every other piece type (see "Why the king is excluded" above); it belongs entirely inside King safety's own dedicated logic, not bolted onto this generic term as a special case.

---

## 2026-08-26 (1) — Mid-search time checks (Priority Fix, High): shared SearchLimits threaded through negamax()/quiescence()/search_root(), interrupted iterations discarded wholesale

**Decision:** A new `SearchLimits` struct (search.h: `deadline`, `has_deadline`, `stopped`) is constructed once per `search_iterative_deepening()` depth iteration, from depth 2 onward, and threaded by pointer (defaulting to `nullptr` everywhere it isn't needed) through `negamax()`, `quiescence()`/`quiescence_impl()`, and `search_root()`. Every negamax()/quiescence() call checks `stopped` at the very top, before any real per-node work, and returns immediately if it's already set; a periodic check (every 2048 nodes, a power of two so the check is a single bitwise AND) reads the clock and sets `stopped` once the deadline has passed. A node whose own move loop observes `stopped` after a recursive call returns breaks immediately rather than trusting that (possibly truncated-subtree) score to update its own `alpha`/`best_move`/`best`, and skips its own TT store. The entire `SearchResult` of an iteration that ends up `stopped` is discarded wholesale by `search_iterative_deepening()` in favor of the previous, fully-completed iteration's result — never partially trusted, even for the moves that happened to complete before the interruption. Depth 1 is unaffected: it is still called with no `SearchLimits` at all (not even one with `has_deadline = false`), preserving the pre-existing "always returns a legal `best_move`, even under an absurdly tight budget" guarantee documented in search.h.

**Rationale:** This is the documented revisit trigger firing (see the 2026-08-25 (8) entry below and the original iterative-deepening entry further down this file) — real `wtime`/`btime`/`winc`/`binc`/`movetime` parsing has been in place for some time, so the previously-accepted "between-iteration checks only" gap (a search could overrun its budget by an entire additional depth iteration) was no longer an acceptable placeholder. The "poisoned but bounded, discarded wholesale" design was chosen over trying to make every internal probe (null-move, ProbCut, singular-extension verification) individually, precisely aware of the interruption: since the ENTIRE iteration's result is thrown away the moment `stopped` is observed at the top level, it doesn't matter that a null-move/ProbCut/singular probe interrupted mid-flight can briefly cause a false cutoff to propagate up one or two frames before the next negamax() call's own top-of-function check catches it — that false cutoff never reaches a TT store (guarded separately) and never survives past the discarded iteration. This keeps the change bounded to a handful of call sites gaining a trailing defaulted parameter plus a small number of guard checks, rather than a much larger rewrite threading precise cancellation semantics through every pruning technique in the file.

**Why quiescence() participates too, not just negamax():** a tactically loaded position can spend a meaningful fraction of total search time inside quiescence search alone; leaving it unaware of the deadline would have left a real blind spot where a search could still overrun its budget from deep inside a single quiescence call, even with negamax() itself fully interruptible.

**Why 2048 nodes, not every node or a much larger interval:** `std::chrono::steady_clock::now()` is not free (ARCHITECTURE.md's "Hot-Path Code Practices" — measure, don't guess, but a syscall-backed clock read on every single node is an obviously bad trade against a node's own, much cheaper, usual cost). 2048 is small enough that even a very tight `movetime` is still checked well within a UCI GUI's expected responsiveness window, while large enough that the clock call's own overhead stays negligible relative to typical per-node work.

**What was built:** `src/search/search.h` — new `SearchLimits` struct; `search_iterative_deepening()`'s doc comment updated (no longer describes mid-search interruption as deferred). `src/search/search.cpp` — `kTimeCheckNodeInterval`/`kTimeCheckNodeMask` constants (replacing the stale comment block that used to explain why this was out of scope); `negamax()` and `search_root()` both gained a trailing `SearchLimits* limits = nullptr` parameter, threaded through every recursive call each makes (NMP probe, razoring's quiescence call, ProbCut probe, singular-extension verification loop, the three-call PVS move loop); both functions' TT stores are now guarded against storing a truncated result; `search_iterative_deepening()`'s depth-2-onward loop now constructs one `SearchLimits` per iteration (shared across aspiration-window fail-high/fail-low retries) and discards the iteration wholesale if it ends up `stopped`. `src/search/quiescence.h`/`src/search/quiescence.cpp` — `quiescence()`/`quiescence_impl()` gained the same trailing `SearchLimits* limits = nullptr` parameter, with their own top-of-function fast bail and periodic deadline check, threaded through quiescence's own self-recursive call. `tests/search_tests.cpp` — new regression test asserting a 1ms budget at `max_depth = 10` from the starting position returns within a generous 2000ms wall-clock margin, specifically exercising the case where depth 1 completes with budget to spare (so the between-iteration check alone would have let depth 2 start) but depth 2 alone would run far longer than 1ms without mid-search interruption.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Making every internal probe (NMP/ProbCut/singular verification) precisely, individually aware of the interruption, refusing to let a truncated score influence even a transient local decision — rejected as unnecessary complexity for this codebase's needs: the wholesale-discard safety net at the top level already guarantees the final answer is never built from a poisoned iteration, so precise per-probe cancellation would only improve unwind speed marginally (bounded already to a small, roughly depth-sized number of extra node visits) at a much larger diff/maintenance cost.
- Threading the deadline through `search_fixed_depth()`'s own public signature instead of leaving it untouched — rejected: `search_fixed_depth()`'s documented contract has never included a time notion (that's exactly what `search_iterative_deepening()` is for), and `search_root()`'s new trailing defaulted parameter means `search_fixed_depth()` needed zero changes at all to keep working exactly as before.
- Applying a deadline to the mandatory depth-1 iteration too (just with a very generous budget) instead of omitting `SearchLimits` for it entirely — rejected: omitting it entirely is a simpler, more obviously-correct guarantee ("this call cannot be interrupted, full stop") than "this call has a deadline that should in practice never be reached."
- Resetting the TT/killers/history between the discarded iteration and the next real search — not needed and not done: the discarded iteration's TT stores were already skipped via the guard, so there's nothing to clean up; killers/history entries written by moves that DID complete before the interruption are still reasonable ordering bets, same as any other iteration's leftover ordering data.

---

## 2026-08-26 (2) — UCI `info` output during search (Priority Fix, Medium): TT-walk PV reconstruction + IterationCallback

**Decision:** `SearchResult` (search.h) gains a new `pv` field (`std::vector<board::Move>`), populated by a new private helper `extract_pv()` (search.cpp) that walks the transposition table from the root position, starting at `search_root()`'s own just-computed `best_move`, following each subsequent position's own TT-stored move as long as it's legal, the TT entry is a genuine `Bound::Exact` hit (not a mere bound), and the walk hasn't already visited that exact position before (cycle guard). `search_root()` calls it once per completed call, right before returning, skipped (like its TT store) when interrupted mid-search. A new `IterationCallback` type (`std::function<void(const SearchResult&)>`) is threaded through `search_iterative_deepening()` as a new, trailing, defaulted (`nullptr`) parameter; it fires once per genuinely completed iteration — including the mandatory depth-1 one, excluding any iteration discarded due to mid-search interruption (2026-08-26 (1), above) — with that iteration's own `best_move`/`score`/`depth_completed`/`pv`, but a `nodes` field rewritten to the *cumulative* total across every iteration so far (matching the conventional meaning of a UCI `info nodes` line). `uci.cpp` gains a new `emit_info()` that formats this into `info depth <d> score cp <s>|mate <n> nodes <n> pv <m1> <m2> ...` and passes it as `handle_go()`'s `on_iteration` argument, so a line is written to the UCI output stream live, once per completed iteration, ahead of the final `bestmove`.

**Rationale:** The second of the two 2026-08-25 external-code-review Priority Fixes (docs/ROADMAP.md's Priority Fixes section) — `uci.cpp` previously only ever emitted a single `bestmove` line per `go`, which most GUIs tolerate but which some tournament managers or strict UCI validators flag, and which gives a human watching the engine no visibility into its own search progress.

**Why a TT walk rather than a triangular PV array:** the triangular PV table is the other standard technique (CPW "Triangular PV Table") — a fixed 2D array threaded through `negamax()`'s own recursion, updated at every node that improves alpha. It's more precise (immune to TT eviction cutting a PV short) but requires new per-node bookkeeping threaded through every one of `negamax()`'s existing recursive call sites (the same ones the 2026-08-26 (1) mid-search-interruption entry, above, just finished threading `SearchLimits` through) — a materially larger, more invasive change to a function that already has a lot of moving parts (NMP/razoring/ProbCut/singular extensions/PVS). A TT walk, by contrast, needed zero changes to `negamax()` itself: the TT is already fully populated with real search data by the time `search_root()` finishes, so reading it back afterward is enough. The accepted trade-off — a PV that can come back shorter than `depth_completed` if a needed TT entry was evicted by the replacement scheme (tt.h) before the walk reads it, or if a node was only ever proven as a bound (Lower/Upper) rather than Exact — was judged acceptable for a Medium-priority UI/tooling feature, not a correctness-critical one; `best_move`/`score` themselves are entirely unaffected either way, since those still come directly from `search_root()`'s own move loop, not from the PV walk.

**Why `nodes` in the callback is cumulative, not per-iteration:** `search_root()`'s own `SearchResult::nodes` is fresh per call (starts at 0, incremented only by that one call's own `negamax()` recursion) — reporting that raw value to the UCI `info` line would show the node count resetting to a small number every iteration instead of growing, which doesn't match what `info nodes` conventionally means to a GUI (total work for the whole `go` command so far) or what the final returned `SearchResult::nodes` already reports (also cumulative, a pre-existing, unchanged contract).

**Why `score mate N` gets its own branch instead of always reporting `cp`:** the UCI spec itself distinguishes the two, and reporting a mate score (which sits in a completely different numeric regime, close to ±32000) as if it were an ordinary centipawn evaluation would be actively misleading to whatever's consuming the `info` line — search.h's own `kMateThreshold` doc comment already flagged this as the constant's intended use before this session.

**What was built:** `src/search/search.h` — `SearchResult::pv`; new `IterationCallback` type; `search_iterative_deepening()` gained the `on_iteration` parameter. `src/search/search.cpp` — new `extract_pv()` (placed immediately before `search_root()`, its only caller, consistent with this file's existing top-to-bottom "used-by" ordering); `search_root()` populates `result.pv`; `search_iterative_deepening()` fires `on_iteration` after depth 1 and after every genuinely completed depth-2-onward iteration, with `nodes` rewritten to the cumulative total first. `src/uci/uci.cpp` — new `emit_info()`; `handle_go()` passes it as `on_iteration`; file header comment's async-`stop`/pondering scope note updated to reference `SearchLimits` now existing (2026-08-26 (1)) without claiming it solves the (still-absent) background-thread half of async `stop`. `tests/search_tests.cpp` — new tests: `pv` non-empty and starts with `best_move` for a genuine mate-in-1; `pv`'s first move is always legal; `pv` is empty for an already-terminal position; `on_iteration` fires once per completed depth in order with non-decreasing cumulative node counts, matching the final result's own total; `on_iteration` is never called for an already-terminal position. `tests/uci_tests.cpp` — new tests: an `info depth` line appears before `bestmove` and carries `score`/`nodes`/`pv` fields; `go depth 3` produces exactly one `info depth <d>` line per depth 1–3 in increasing order; an already-checkmated position's `go` emits no `info depth` line at all, only `bestmove 0000`; a forced mate-in-1 position's `info` line reports `score mate 1`, not `score cp`.

**Alternatives considered:**
- The triangular PV array — rejected for this pass; see "Why a TT walk" above. Revisit if the TT-eviction-truncation trade-off turns out to matter in practice (e.g. once a much larger TT default size or much deeper searches make evictions during a single search's own PV path more common) — tracked here rather than re-litigated in ROADMAP.md, since this entry already explains why it isn't attempted upfront.
- Reporting per-iteration (not cumulative) `nodes` in the `info` line, matching what `search_root()` itself already returns with no rewrite needed — rejected as actively misleading against the UCI convention (see "Why cumulative" above); the one-line rewrite (`result.nodes = total_nodes` ahead of the callback) was a small enough cost not to be worth trading correctness for.
- Passing the callback through as `std::function` versus a template/generic callable parameter (avoiding `std::function`'s small type-erasure overhead) — rejected: `on_iteration` fires at most `max_depth` times per `go` call (a handful), so the overhead is immaterial, and `std::function`'s ordinary trailing-defaulted-parameter ergonomics (a plain lambda at the call site, no template instantiation concerns) fit this codebase's existing style better than a template parameter would for a function with this many other parameters already.
- Emitting `nps` (nodes per second) and `time` fields alongside `depth`/`score`/`nodes`/`pv`, matching what more full-featured engines report — deferred: ROADMAP.md's own wording for this item names exactly `depth`/`score`/`nodes`/`pv`, and `nps`/`time` need wall-clock timing threaded into `SearchResult`/`IterationCallback` that isn't there yet; revisit only if a real GUI/tournament manager is found to require them, rather than speculatively adding fields nothing currently asks for.

---

## 2026-08-25 (9) — Regression bench (Phase 4, final item): fixed positions, fixed depth, node counts printed for docs/SESSIONS.md tracking — Phase 4 now complete

**Decision:** New `tests/bench_tests.cpp`, wired into the existing `nightwing_tests` Catch2 executable (`tests/CMakeLists.txt`), matching the scope agreed in discussion before implementation: node-count only (no tactical/strength suite — deferred until Phase 5's eval work gives the engine something worth measuring beyond forced-mate correctness), a small fixed set of positions, run via `ctest` so it's free on every CI push rather than a separate manual step. Four positions searched via `search_fixed_depth()` at a fixed depth (`kBenchDepth = 6`): `startpos`, `kiwipete` (reusing the project's own existing perft reference FEN, so no new position needs independent verification), a quiet QGD-Exchange-shaped middlegame, and the same externally-verified mate-in-3 endgame reused across every other Phase 4 regression test in `search_tests.cpp`. Each position's node count, score, and best move are printed to stdout in a plain, greppable `BENCH <name> depth=... nodes=... score=... best_move=...` format, plus a `BENCH TOTAL` line.

**Why this test does NOT assert exact node counts:** unlike perft (a mathematically fixed ground truth — the same position at the same depth always has exactly one correct node count), a search's node count is EXPECTED to change whenever a pruning/ordering/extension technique changes, often on purpose (fewer nodes for the same correct answer is usually the whole point of adding a pruning technique in the first place). Asserting a fixed value here would make the test fail on every intentional improvement — the opposite of what a regression bench is for. Instead, the test asserts only genuine invariants: the search completes, returns a non-null best move (`REQUIRE_FALSE(result.best_move.is_null())`), reports real search effort (`nodes > 0`), and actually reached the requested depth (`depth_completed == kBenchDepth`) — a null best move or a `nodes == 0` from a legal, non-terminal position would be an actual correctness bug, not just an uninteresting bench result, so those stay hard assertions while the interesting, expected-to-change number (the node count itself) is only ever printed, never asserted on.

**Why fixed depth, not a time budget:** fixed depth means fixed, reproducible node counts across different machines and CI runners, which is the entire point of a diffable bench — a time-limited search would be dominated by hardware/scheduling noise between runs, not by engine changes, making session-to-session comparison meaningless. `kBenchDepth = 6` was chosen to be deep enough that Phase 4's own depth-gated techniques are genuinely exercised (several only apply from around depth 3 up — `kFutilityMaxDepth`/`kRazorMaxDepth`/`kProbCutMinDepth`/`kSingularMinDepth`, all in `search.cpp`), while staying fast enough to run on every CI push across all three platforms' Debug/ASan+UBSan builds too, not just Release.

**Why these four positions specifically, not more:** kept small and deliberately varied in CHARACTER rather than just count, so a change that helps one phase of the game at another's expense shows up as an uneven shift across the printed rows instead of being hidden inside a single aggregate number — an opening, a tactically-loaded middlegame, a quieter positional middlegame, and a forced-mate endgame is a reasonably representative spread without turning this into a large, slow suite that discourages running it every session.

**How this is meant to be used going forward (the actual "tracked in SESSIONS.md per change" convention this ROADMAP.md item asked for):** after any session that changes search-affecting code, the `BENCH` lines from that session's CI run should be read from the log and a short note added to that session's own SESSIONS.md entry — node counts up or down, roughly by how much, for which positions — as a concrete signal on whether the change actually helped, distinct from (and a check against) the prose rationale already written in DECISIONS.md for that change. This session's own CI run (once pushed) will be the first real baseline numbers to record.

**Phase 4 status:** with this item checked off, Phase 4 (Pruning & Extensions) is now fully complete. Also folded into this same docs push: the "Priority Fixes" ROADMAP.md section from the external-code-review discussion (previously drafted but not yet committed — see the entry immediately below, renumbered from its original draft) is applied now alongside this item, since both target the same three docs files and the review's own findings hadn't reached the repo yet.

**What was built:** `tests/bench_tests.cpp` (new file); `tests/CMakeLists.txt` — `bench_tests.cpp` added to `nightwing_tests`'s source list.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Asserting exact node counts with a documented "update this number when you intend to change search behavior" comment (matching how some engines pin bench node counts in CI specifically to catch UNINTENTIONAL changes) — rejected for this first draft; that pattern works well once a project has a stable, tuned search and wants to catch accidental regressions, but Phase 4 has been landing a new search technique almost every session, so a pinned assertion would need updating on nearly every push, adding friction without adding real protection at this stage. Worth revisiting once Phase 4/5 churn settles down.
- A separate standalone `bench` binary/CLI command instead of a `ctest` test case — deliberately NOT done here; ROADMAP.md's own Phase 8 already lists a dedicated "`bench` command — fixed-position node/time benchmark for fishtest/OpenBench-style regression testing" as separate, later, more infrastructure-heavy work (CLI argument parsing, NPS timing, PGO integration). This session's item is the lighter-weight "make node counts visible somewhere per change" need Phase 4 actually asked for; building the two as one item now would have either scope-crept this session or under-built Phase 8's eventual real needs.
- More than four positions, or a deeper bench depth — rejected for now per the "keep it fast enough to run every push" reasoning above; revisit if four positions prove too narrow to catch something a fifth would have.

## 2026-08-25 (8) — External code review: findings logged, two gaps promoted to a new pre-Phase-5 Priority Fixes section

**Context:** An independent code review was conducted against a checkout of the repository (`nightwing-main.zip`), covering a from-scratch build in both Release and Debug (ASan/UBSan) configurations, a full run of the test suite in each, and a UCI smoke test. This is the first time this project's own self-reported state (docs/ROADMAP.md's checkboxes, docs/SESSIONS.md's per-session notes) has been checked against an actual build/test/run cycle by a party other than whoever wrote the session that made a given claim — every prior session's own "no compiler available in this environment" verification caveat has been exactly that, a caveat, until now.

**Findings, and what was done with each:**

1. **Build/test verification: both configurations clean, 52,236 assertions across 211 test cases passing in each, including the full perft suite under AddressSanitizer/UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer.** This is the strongest possible confirmation available that the repeated "no compiler available, hand-verified only" caveat on every prior session's own work has not been masking an accumulating defect — no action needed beyond recording it here as real, external validation rather than another self-reported claim.

2. **No UCI `info` output during search (reviewer: Medium priority).** `SearchResult` already collects score/nodes/depth per iteration; `uci.cpp` never emits it mid-search, only the final `bestmove`. Added to ROADMAP.md as a new Priority Fix, positioned before Phase 5 rather than left for its natural Phase 8 slot ("Full UCI option set") — see the ordering rationale below.

3. **Time management doesn't check the clock mid-search (reviewer: High priority).** This is not a new discovery — it's the exact, previously-logged Phase 2 scope cut (this file, the iterative-deepening entry: checking the clock only *between* `search_fixed_depth()` calls, not inside `negamax()`, with an explicit note that the natural revisit point is "alongside real time-control parsing in the UCI loop"). What the review adds is confirmation that the stated revisit TRIGGER has already fired — `wtime`/`btime`/`winc`/`binc`/`movetime` are all implemented in the current UCI layer — without the revisit itself happening. Added to ROADMAP.md as a Priority Fix, explicitly distinguished from Phase 8's own broader "Time management" item (that item is the full allocation-strategy feature — increment handling, best-move-stability extension, etc.; this fix is narrower: does the search loop actually respect an already-computed budget once it's mid-iteration).

4. **TT/pawn hash tables reallocated per `go` call (reviewer: Low priority, "no action needed ahead of schedule").** Already an intentional, explicitly documented placeholder (this file, 2026-08-15, "Transposition table lifetime scoped per top-level search call, not a persistent global") pending Phase 8's `Hash` UCI option. No new ROADMAP.md item needed — cross-referenced from Phase 8's existing "Full UCI option set" line instead, so the finding isn't lost even though it doesn't need its own checklist entry.

**Why findings 2 and 3 were promoted to a new pre-Phase-5 section instead of left in their originally-scheduled phases (8 and 8/Phase-2-revisit respectively):** both are real gaps in something the project already claims to have ("basic UCI loop" since Phase 2, real time controls since some later session) rather than missing FUTURE scope — Phase 8 is "Polish & Tournament Readiness," phrased as things to add once the engine is otherwise feature-complete, but a search that can blow through its time budget isn't a polish gap, it's a correctness gap in a feature this project already asserts is done. Ordered by the reviewer's own severity assessment (time checks, High, before UCI info, Medium), and both ordered before Phase 5 (eval expansion) rather than after, since eval work will only make search slower per node, compounding the time-overrun risk finding 3 describes rather than leaving it alone until Phase 8 arrives on schedule.

**Why the regression-bench item (Phase 4's own last remaining item) went FIRST, ahead of these two fixes, and is now done (see the entry immediately above):** it was small, already scoped in discussion before this review arrived, and finished Phase 4 cleanly rather than leaving it dangling while a new section jumped the queue — the Priority Fixes section is real and urgent, but not so urgent that an already-agreed-scope checklist item should be abandoned mid-discussion to make room for it.

**What was built:** `docs/ROADMAP.md` — new "Priority Fixes (external code review, 2026-08-25)" section between Phase 4 and Phase 5, with the two promoted items; Phase 8's "Full UCI option set" and "Time management" lines both annotated to cross-reference this section and this entry, so the relationship between the fast-tracked fix and its eventual fuller feature is documented in both directions; the "Current phase" footer updated to spell out the new sequencing. No source code changed as part of THIS entry's own work (see the entry above for the regression-bench implementation that was bundled into the same docs push once both were ready).

**Alternatives considered:**
- Silently reordering ROADMAP.md's existing phase items (e.g. moving Phase 8's "Time management" item up) instead of adding a new section — rejected; that would conflate the narrow "respect an already-computed budget mid-search" fix with the broader "compute a good budget in the first place" feature Phase 8's item actually describes, losing the distinction the review itself draws between "gap" and "not yet scheduled."
- Treating finding 3 as urgent enough to interrupt the in-progress regression-bench work immediately — rejected per the ordering rationale above; nothing about finding 3 is more urgent TODAY than it was in prior sessions (fixed-depth testing, the only kind this project's own test suite and every prior session's manual/CI verification has exercised, was never affected by it), so finishing the small, already-scoped bench item first was a reasonable, bounded delay.

## 2026-08-25 (7) — Singular extensions (Phase 4): TT-move-specific verification search, second depth-adding technique in this file

**Decision:** Implemented as CPW's "Singular Extensions": evaluated ONLY for the TT move at a node (order_moves() always places it at index 0 when present and legal, so this is scoped to `i == 0`), not for every move the way check extensions are. When the TT's own previously-stored entry for this position indicates the TT move caused a beta cutoff (`Bound::Lower`) at a search deep enough to trust, a cheap reduced-depth verification search is run against every OTHER legal move at the node, under a narrow window built just below that stored score (`singular_beta = probe.score - kSingularMarginPerPly * depth`). If NONE of the alternatives can reach `singular_beta`, the TT move is judged "singular" (the only genuinely good option here) and its own child search is granted one extra ply — combined via `std::max()` with any check-extension bonus the same move might separately qualify for (never summed, so a checking TT move judged singular still only extends once).

**Why the verification loop has to run BEFORE the TT move's own `make_move()`:** it needs to try every OTHER legal move from the SAME, still-undisturbed position `pos`. This required restructuring `negamax()`'s move loop: `moved_piece` (needed regardless of which move) is still computed first, but the singular-extension check itself — including its own internal make_move/unmake_move pairs for each alternative — now runs before this iteration's own `board::make_move(pos, move, undo)` line, whereas `move_gives_check` (needed for check extensions) still has to run AFTER, since it depends on the CURRENT move already being applied. The two extension techniques therefore straddle the move's own `make_move()` call from opposite sides, for reasons specific to what each one needs to observe.

**Guards, and why each exists:** `depth >= kSingularMinDepth` (8) — the technique pays for an entire extra search per eligible node, so shallow nodes can't afford it. `probe.depth >= depth - kSingularTTDepthMargin` (3) — the TT entry must be from a search deep enough to trust as a baseline; a much-shallower stored entry's score isn't reliable enough to build a verification window from. `probe.score` within `(-kMateThreshold, kMateThreshold)` — the same mate-range guard every other Phase 4 margin-based technique in this file already has, for the identical reason (a margin computed against a mate score is meaningless). `probe.bound == Bound::Lower` specifically (not `Exact` or `Upper`) — only a Lower bound means the stored score reflects "this move caused a cutoff," the actual signal singular extension is checking for; an `Exact` score is a fully-resolved PV value with no cutoff implication, and an `Upper` bound means the position failed LOW, the opposite of what's being tested here.

**Why a formula (`kSingularMarginPerPly * depth`) instead of a fixed lookup table for the margin:** unlike `kFutilityMargins`/`kRazorMargins` (capped at a small, fixed max depth where a literal-per-depth table is easy to hand-verify), singular extensions can trigger at any depth from `kSingularMinDepth` up through however deep iterative deepening goes — too wide a range for a table to stay hand-verifiable. A simple linear formula is the more honest first-draft choice here, same "not yet tuned" caveat as every other constant in this file.

**Why `us_in_check` is NOT part of this technique's own guards (unlike several other Phase 4 checks):** a deliberate difference from LMP/futility/history-pruning's own in-check exclusions. Those techniques skip searching a move entirely on a heuristic; doing that at a forced check-evasion node (few, critical replies) would risk missing the only real escape. Singular extension does the opposite — it runs an EXTRA search to gain more confidence before granting a bonus, never skips anything outright — so applying it at an in-check node is safe and, if anything, more valuable there (a check-evasion node with exactly one non-losing reply is a natural fit for what "singular" means).

**What was built:** `src/search/search.cpp` — `#include <algorithm>` added (for `std::max`); `kSingularMinDepth`/`kSingularTTDepthMargin`/`kSingularMarginPerPly`/`kSingularDepthDivisor`/`kSingularExtensionPly` constants; the singular-extension verification block itself, inserted into the move loop before each move's own `make_move()`; `check_extension`/`extension` variables reworked so `extension` is now `std::max(check_extension, singular_extension)`, threaded through every recursive call exactly as `extension` alone was before this session. `negamax()`'s header comment extended with a singular-extensions section, and check extensions' own section updated to note it's the file's first (not "only") depth-adding technique. `tests/search_tests.cpp` — one new regression test, using `search_iterative_deepening()` specifically (not `search_fixed_depth()`) so the TT is realistically populated by the time the final iteration's singular check could plausibly trigger.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Excluding `us_in_check` nodes from singular extensions, matching several other Phase 4 techniques' own convention — rejected per the guards section above; the underlying soundness concern (skipping a move that might be the only escape) doesn't apply here, since this technique only ever adds a verification search, never skips one.
- A "negative extension" / reduction for the TT move when an alternative's verification score exceeds `beta` itself (not just `singular_beta`) — a related idea sometimes called a "multi-cut" signal, explicitly out of scope for this session, consistent with the same scope decision already logged for ProbCut (2026-08-25 (4))'s own multi-cut note; a plausible follow-up once both techniques' interaction can be reasoned about together.
- Applying the singular check to every move, not just the TT move — rejected; CPW's own formulation and every real engine's implementation of this technique specifically target the TT move, since it's the one move whose prior-cutoff evidence already exists in the TT to build a verification window from at zero extra cost.

## 2026-08-25 (6) — Check extensions (Phase 4): the first depth-ADDING technique in this file, bundled with an LMR eligibility fix

**Decision:** Implemented as CPW's "Check Extensions": a move that gives check gets its own child search granted one extra ply (`depth - 1 + kCheckExtensionPly` instead of the usual `depth - 1`) in both `negamax()`'s own move loop and `search_root()`'s. Every previous Phase 4 addition (LMP, futility, razoring, history pruning, continuation history, ProbCut, delta pruning, and LMR itself) removed search depth or skipped moves outright; this is the first that adds depth back.

**Why `move_gives_check` needed to be hoisted out of the `else` (i != 0) branch:** check extensions apply to ANY checking move, including the very first one tried at a node (`i == 0`), which previously never computed `move_gives_check` at all (futility/LMP/history-pruning's own uses of it were all inside the `i != 0` branch only, since none of those three techniques ever apply to the first move). Moved the `in_check(pos)` call (and the `extension` calculation derived from it) to right after `make_move()`, before the `i == 0`/`else` split, so both branches can use it.

**The `ply + 1 < kMaxPly` guard is a correctness requirement, not an optimization:** `path` and the killer/PV bookkeeping this function relies on are fixed-size arrays sized by `kMaxPly` (search/ordering.h). An unbounded chain of check extensions could in principle push `ply` past that bound in a long forced-check sequence; without the guard, that would be an out-of-bounds array write, not merely a wasted search. This mirrors the same category of guard already established for other Phase 4 techniques' own mate-range/depth-range checks, just protecting a different invariant (array bounds instead of margin-comparison meaningfulness).

**Bundled fix — LMR's own eligibility never excluded checking moves:** while wiring `move_gives_check` into the extension decision, a latent gap in LMR's own `eligible_for_lmr` condition became visible: it already excluded captures and promotions (tactical moves needing full-depth verification) but never excluded checking moves, even though a checking move is exactly as forcing/tactical as either of those, and CPW's own LMR guidance excludes them for the identical reason. Fixed by adding `&& !move_gives_check` to `eligible_for_lmr`. This is treated as a confirmed correctness/quality issue directly entangled with this session's own work (not an unrelated rewrite of otherwise-green code, per this project's standing "never rewrite completed/green code unless a bug is confirmed" rule) — check extensions and LMR's own eligibility both now consult the same `move_gives_check` value at the same point in the loop, and leaving LMR's gap unfixed while adding extensions right next to it would have left a checking move theoretically eligible for BOTH a reduction and an extension simultaneously (nonsensical — a move can't be both more forcing than usual and less worth searching fully than usual). With the fix, extension and reduction are naturally mutually exclusive per move: a move is either forcing enough to extend, or ordinary enough to consider reducing, never plausibly both.

**Why `search_root()` also got the extension (not just `negamax()`):** for the same symmetry reasoning every previous session's `search_root()` update has followed (continuation history's `prev_piece`/`prev_to` threading, 2026-08-25 (1)) — a root move that gives check is exactly as forcing as one found deeper in the tree, and treating the root move loop as a special case that doesn't extend would be an arbitrary, undocumented inconsistency rather than a deliberate scope decision.

**What was built:** `src/search/search.cpp` — `kCheckExtensionPly` constant; `move_gives_check` and `extension` hoisted to compute once per move (shared by both the `i == 0` and `else` branches); every recursive `negamax()` call in the move loop (both `negamax()`'s own and `search_root()`'s) now passes `depth - 1 + extension` (or `depth - 1 + extension - reduction` where LMR's own reduction also applies) instead of a bare `depth - 1`; `eligible_for_lmr` gained `&& !move_gives_check`. `negamax()`'s header comment extended with a check-extensions section, and the existing LMR section's text updated to note the new exclusion. `tests/search_tests.cpp` — one new end-to-end regression test on a mating line built entirely from checking moves, with an explicit note that it confirms the mate is still found correctly with extensions active, not that the extension is NECESSARY to find it at this specific depth (constructing a "extension is the deciding factor" position with confidence would need external engine verification, deferred rather than guessed at by hand).

**Alternatives considered:**
- A cap on consecutive check extensions along one line (some engines limit how many times in a row a single search path can extend, independent of the `kMaxPly` array-bounds guard) — deliberately deferred for this first draft; `kMaxPly` already provides a hard backstop, and this codebase's existing 50-move-rule/repetition detection (`is_draw_by_rule()`) should catch any position that would otherwise loop on checks forever well before that bound is threatened in practice. A plausible future refinement once real game data suggests search-time cost, not correctness, is the concern.
- Leaving the LMR eligibility gap unfixed and only documenting it as a known issue for a future session — rejected; the fix is a one-token change (`&& !move_gives_check`) directly adjacent to code this session was already rewriting, and leaving it in place would have made the newly-introduced extension/reduction interaction reasoning (this entry's own "naturally mutually exclusive" claim) false the moment it was written.
- Not extending `search_root()`'s own move loop, leaving check extensions as a `negamax()`-only technique — rejected per the symmetry reasoning above.

---

## 2026-08-25 (5) — Delta pruning in quiescence search (Phase 4): the first Phase 4 item to touch quiescence.cpp rather than search.cpp

**Decision:** Implemented as CPW's "Delta Pruning": inside `quiescence_impl()`'s candidate loop (search/quiescence.cpp), a capture is skipped outright — before even paying for the existing SEE-pruning check right after it — when the captured piece's raw material value plus a generous safety margin (`kDeltaMargin`, for positional factors this material-only estimate can't see) still can't plausibly bring the stand-pat baseline up to `alpha`. Checked first among the two capture-skip checks specifically because it's cheaper than SEE (no swap-algorithm simulation, just one piece's value lookup), so a capture delta pruning already rules out never needs SEE run on it at all.

**Why this is the first Phase 4 item to touch quiescence.cpp instead of search.cpp:** every prior Phase 4 technique (LMP, futility, razoring, history pruning, continuation history, ProbCut) lived entirely in `negamax()`'s own move loop or its immediate node-level preamble. Delta pruning is inherently a quiescence-search technique (CPW itself describes it as quiescence-specific, unlike futility/razoring's full-search applicability) — `quiescence_impl()` doesn't thread `alpha`/`beta` bounds through the same machinery `negamax()` does, and reusing SEE's own captured-piece-value extraction pattern (rather than negamax()'s own killer/history-oriented move classification) made more sense done locally in the file that already owns that logic.

**Guards:** not in check (mirrors SEE pruning's own guard immediately below it — every evasion must be tried regardless of material cost when in check); `alpha` not already in mate-score range, checked ONCE per node (`delta_pruning_may_apply`, computed right before the candidate loop, not re-derived per move) rather than folded directly into the per-move condition — without this guard, delta pruning's own per-move check would wrongly conclude EVERY ordinary capture fails to close a mate-sized alpha and skip all of them, a correctness bug (silently reporting no good options exist when a real capture does), not just a missed optimization; excludes capture-promotions (a promotion's value swing is large and volatile enough that the plain captured-piece-value estimate isn't reliable for it, the same reasoning futility/LMP/history-pruning in search.cpp already exclude promotions from their own quiet-move-only checks, just applied here to a capture-specific technique instead).

**Captured-piece value extraction matches SEE's own convention exactly:** `move.is_en_passant() ? PieceType::Pawn : board::piece_type_of(pos.piece_at(move.to()))` — copied in spirit (not literally, a fresh one-line expression) from `search/see.cpp`'s own identical need to know what's actually being captured, since en passant's captured pawn sits one rank behind `move.to()`, not on it, and naively reading `piece_at(move.to())` for an en passant move would silently return an empty square rather than the pawn actually being captured.

**Why `kDeltaMargin` (200cp) sits at the conservative end of search.cpp's own futility-margin range (100-300cp) rather than razoring's wider one (300-500cp):** a wrongly-pruned capture inside quiescence search can't be recovered by a later iterative-deepening pass the way a slightly-too-aggressive full-search margin sometimes gets papered over by search.cpp's own later, deeper iterations — quiescence is the leaf of the whole search tree, so there's no "next iteration" to catch a mistake made here. Not yet tuned for Nightwing specifically, same caveat as every margin constant in this codebase.

**What was built:** `src/search/quiescence.cpp` — `kDeltaMargin` constant; `#include "eval/psqt.h"` added (for `eval::material_value()`, not previously needed in this file); the `delta_pruning_may_apply` node-level guard and the per-move delta-pruning check itself, both inserted into `quiescence_impl()`'s existing candidate loop right before the pre-existing SEE-pruning check. `tests/quiescence_tests.cpp` — two new regression tests: one isolating delta pruning specifically (a genuinely good, SEE-positive capture skipped anyway because alpha is set far higher than the captured piece's value could plausibly reach, confirming the skip is delta pruning's doing and not SEE's), and one confirming the mate-range-alpha guard actually works (the same good capture, with alpha pushed into mate-score territory, still gets searched rather than wrongly skipped) — following this file's own existing pattern (the SEE-pruning tests just above) of asserting on `nodes` count rather than just the final score, since alpha-beta alone would already guarantee a losing move is never played even without any pruning at all.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Skipping the mate-range-alpha guard for simplicity, reasoning that a capture failing delta pruning near a mate-score alpha would be rare in practice — rejected; this is a genuine correctness risk (every capture at such a node would be silently mispruned), not a rare edge case worth accepting, and the guard costs one cheap comparison per node, not per move.
- Checking delta pruning after SEE instead of before — rejected; delta pruning's whole value proposition is being cheaper than SEE, so checking it first avoids paying for SEE's swap-algorithm simulation on captures that delta pruning already rules out on its own.
- Applying delta pruning to capture-promotions too (using the captured piece's value, ignoring the promoted piece's own value entirely) — rejected; that would systematically undervalue a capture-promotion's true material swing and risk pruning genuinely strong moves, the same reasoning search.cpp's own quiet-move techniques already exclude promotions for.

---

## 2026-08-25 (4) — ProbCut (Phase 4): node-level fail-high shortcut at moderate-to-high depth, reuses order_moves()'s output

**Decision:** Implemented as CPW's "ProbCut": at moderate-to-high remaining depth (`kProbCutMinDepth` and up — the opposite end of the depth spectrum from futility/razoring, which apply near the leaves), a reduced-depth verification search against a raised, null window (`beta + kProbCutMargin`) is tried for each capture/promotion in the node's own already-ordered move list; if any one of them alone proves the position is winning by at least that inflated margin, the whole node returns immediately (fail-soft, the verification score itself) rather than paying for a full-depth search that's likely to reach the same fail-high conclusion anyway. Placed right after `order_moves()` and the `us_in_check` computation, before the futility-pruning block — the earliest point in `negamax()`'s existing top-to-bottom cascade where the ordered `moves` list is available to reuse.

**Why this reuses `order_moves()`'s output instead of being self-contained (like razoring):** razoring runs BEFORE movegen even happens, so it has nothing to reuse and is necessarily self-contained (its own `in_check()`/`eval::evaluate()` calls — 2026-08-24 (3)). ProbCut runs AFTER movegen and ordering, specifically so its own verification loop can walk captures/promotions in their already-best-first MVV-LVA order rather than pay for a second movegen or a second `order_moves()` call. Quiet moves in the list are skipped with `continue`, not `break`, because the TT move — always tried first regardless of type — can itself be a quiet move without that implying every move after it is quiet too.

**Guards:** not in check; remaining depth at or above `kProbCutMinDepth` (5); `beta` not already in mate-score range (a raised window built from a mate score would be meaningless, the same reasoning as NMP's own beta guard). No separate "not a PV node" guard — this codebase doesn't currently thread a PV/non-PV distinction into `negamax()` at all (no prior Phase 4 technique has needed one), so adding one just for ProbCut would be new machinery beyond this session's scope; noted as a plausible future refinement once PV-node tracking exists for some other reason.

**Why `kProbCutReduction` (4) is deliberately larger than LMR's own R (1-2):** ProbCut's verification search only needs to be roughly right about "is this position winning by at least this large, specific margin" — not compute an exact score — so a much shallower, cheaper probe is an acceptable trade specifically for this technique, mirroring how NMP's own reduction is chosen independently of LMR's rather than sharing a single "the" reduction constant across every pruning technique in this file.

**Why fail-soft (the verification score) rather than just returning `beta`:** matches this file's own existing convention (NMP's null-move probe, 2026-08-21 (5)) of returning the real bound achieved rather than clamping down to the window edge — more informative to the caller, same soundness argument (`probcut_score >= probcut_beta > beta`, so it's already a valid bound on the true score) — with the same mate-range clamp NMP's own return already has, for the identical reason: a score from a reduced-depth search shouldn't be trusted as an exact mate distance.

**Scope note on "ProbCut / multi-cut pruning" (ROADMAP.md's combined checklist line):** this session implemented ProbCut specifically — a single-move verification search that returns as soon as ONE move proves the margin. "Multi-cut" (CPW's related but distinct technique: try several moves at reduced depth and require a MINIMUM COUNT of them to fail high, not just one, before cutting) was not implemented as a separate mechanism. Both techniques are checked off together on ROADMAP.md's single combined line, but this is a conscious scope decision, not an oversight — multi-cut's own extra bookkeeping (a per-node fail-high counter, a separate move-count threshold) would be a meaningfully separate piece of work, and CPW itself treats them as related-but-distinct techniques rather than one asking for both.

**What was built:** `src/search/search.cpp` — `kProbCutMinDepth`/`kProbCutMargin`/`kProbCutReduction` constants (alongside the existing `kRazor*` block); the ProbCut check itself, inserted between `order_moves()`/`us_in_check` and the futility-pruning block. `negamax()`'s header comment extended with a ProbCut section, positioned right after razoring's own section to reflect where the check now sits in the code. `tests/search_tests.cpp` — one new regression test, the same externally-verified mate-in-3 position reused for every prior Phase 4 check, requested at depth 6 specifically so the root's own immediate children (remaining depth 5) hit `kProbCutMinDepth` exactly on the test's very first ply, confirming the check is genuinely exercised rather than merely present.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Implementing multi-cut alongside ProbCut in the same session — deliberately deferred, per the scope note above; a plausible follow-up once this is confirmed correct in isolation.
- Making ProbCut self-contained with its own movegen/ordering (matching razoring's own pattern) instead of reusing `order_moves()`'s existing output — rejected; ProbCut's natural placement (after movegen/ordering already happened) makes reuse strictly cheaper with no downside, unlike razoring which genuinely has no ordered list yet available at its own, earlier point in the function.
- A PV-node exclusion guard — deliberately out of scope, per the guards section above; this codebase has no existing PV/non-PV distinction to hook into yet.

## 2026-08-25 (3) — Second CI build failure: tests/ordering_tests.cpp not updated for order_moves()'s new signature, plus a repeat of the same TEST_CASE-corruption pattern

**Cause 1 (the real bug):** Session 26's continuation-history change extended `order_moves()`'s signature (search/ordering.h) with three new parameters (`cont_history`, `prev_piece`, `prev_to`), and updated every call site in `search.cpp` — but `tests/ordering_tests.cpp`, a file that unit-tests `order_moves()` directly and was never fetched or scanned during that session, still called the old 6-argument form at all 11 of its own call sites. This is a real gap in Session 26's own process: the session's file-discovery relied on `grep -n "order_moves("` against `search.cpp` only, not a repo-wide search, so a second, independent caller of the function it was changing went unnoticed until CI caught it.

**Cause 2 (a repeat mistake):** `tests/search_tests.cpp` had, for a second time, an orphaned test fragment — this time from Session 26's own `str_replace` that inserted the continuation-history test: the `old_str` match ended mid-line, on the partial text `TEST_CASE("search_fixed_depth: back-rank mate in 1 is still found exactly when searched well "` (a line the tool matched successfully since it's a real substring), but the corresponding `new_str` didn't repeat that partial line's continuation, silently dropping the rest of that `TEST_CASE`'s own declaration (its second string-literal line and the `TEST_CASE(` call itself) the same way Session 25's edit did. This is the exact failure mode the previous bugfix's own process note (2026-08-25 (2)) warned about, and it recurred in the same session that wrote that warning — the note called for re-viewing a retry after a *rejected* (non-unique) match, but this edit succeeded on its first attempt with a *technically-correct-but-too-short* anchor, a related but distinct failure the previous note didn't fully cover.

**Fix 1:** `tests/ordering_tests.cpp` — added a `ContinuationHistoryTable cont_history;` declaration alongside each `HistoryTable history;` declaration that precedes an `order_moves()` call (10 of the file's 14 `HistoryTable` declarations; the other 4 are pure `HistoryTable`-unit tests with no `order_moves()` call and were left untouched), and appended `, cont_history, PieceType::None, 0` to all 11 `order_moves()` call sites. Also added 5 new unit tests for `ContinuationHistoryTable` itself, matching the file's existing pattern of testing `HistoryTable` in isolation (unrecorded-score-is-0, depth-squared bonus, the `PieceType::None` no-op sentinel specifically, distinct-context independence, and clamping) — `ContinuationHistoryTable` had no dedicated unit tests at all before this fix, only indirect coverage via `search_tests.cpp`'s end-to-end mate-in-3 check.

**Fix 2:** `tests/search_tests.cpp` — restored the missing `TEST_CASE("search_fixed_depth: back-rank mate in 1 is still found exactly when searched well "` line and its declaration. Re-swept the entire file programmatically this time (a script checking every `"[search][...]") {` line has a `TEST_CASE(` within the preceding few lines) rather than a manual read-through, specifically to catch this class of error with certainty rather than by eye.

**Process fix, going forward:** two changes to how a function-signature change gets verified in future sessions. First, before changing any function's signature, search the ENTIRE `tests/` directory for its name (not just the source files being edited) — `grep -rn "function_name(" tests/` — since a function can have callers in a dedicated unit-test file that never comes up while reading the production code that also calls it. Second, any `str_replace` whose `old_str` ends mid-line (not at a natural statement/declaration boundary) is treated as higher-risk regardless of whether the tool accepts it on the first try: the file is re-viewed around the edit site immediately afterward to confirm the surrounding text reads correctly, not just that the tool reported success.

## 2026-08-25 (2) — CI build failure: corrupted TEST_CASE in tests/search_tests.cpp (Session 25's edit)

**Cause:** A `str_replace` performed during Session 25 (history pruning) hit a non-unique match on its first attempt, was corrected, but the corrected edit's anchor text ended up deleting the `TEST_CASE("search_fixed_depth: back-rank mate in 1 is still found exactly when searched well beyond the mating depth (mate distance pruning)",` line and its continuation, leaving an orphaned `"[search][mdp]") {` fragment with no macro invocation around it — a syntax error, not a logic bug, so it was caught by every platform's compiler (`error: expected unqualified-id before string constant` on GCC/Clang, `error C2059: syntax error: 'string'` on MSVC) rather than by any test actually running.

**Fix:** Restored the missing `TEST_CASE(...)` line and its two string-literal arguments ahead of the orphaned `"[search][mdp]") {` fragment, reconstructing the exact original test declaration from the last known-good fetch of this file. Swept the rest of `tests/search_tests.cpp` for the same failure pattern (an orphaned `"[search][...]") {` line with no `TEST_CASE(` in the preceding few lines) — none found; only this one instance existed.

**Why correct:** The restored line matches the test body that follows it exactly (mate distance pruning, `"[search][mdp]"` tag, `search_fixed_depth` call at depth 6 on the same back-rank-mate-in-1 position the test's own body already references) — this is a reconstruction of a real, previously-working test that a bad edit accidentally decapitated, not a new test being invented to paper over the error.

**Process note (why this happened):** `str_replace`'s uniqueness requirement caught the FIRST unsafe edit attempt (correctly refused a non-unique match), but the retry used a shorter, still-technically-unique anchor string that happened to span a boundary between two adjacent tests, and the retry wasn't re-viewed against the file before being trusted. Going forward, any `str_replace` retry after a "not found"/"not unique" error gets a fresh `view` of the exact target region immediately before retrying, rather than assuming a slightly-adjusted anchor is safe — the same "don't skip a guard on unverified hand-written code without a compiler available" lesson already on record (2026-08-21 (3)) applies equally to edits, not just to new code.
