## 2026-08-20 (3) — CI: generation-stamp fix confirmed on all three platforms; investigation closed

**Results:** the generation-stamp fix (previous entry) was verified against a real 6-job CI run. All Debug jobs beat their pre-`uint8_t` baselines, not merely avoided regressing:

| Job | Original | `vector<uint8_t>` | Generation-stamp |
|---|---|---|---|
| Windows Debug | 2732.80s | 180.33s | **157.26s** |
| Linux Debug | 357.50s | 335.50s | **118.78s** |
| macOS Debug | 177.53s | 326.02s | **117.27s** |

173/173 tests passing on Windows and Linux; 171/171 on macOS (the 2 fewer are the expected BMI2-gated tests, not compiled on Apple Silicon/ARM64 per the `CMAKE_SYSTEM_PROCESSOR` guard in `src/CMakeLists.txt`). This confirms the earlier reasoning: the O(size) `std::fill()` reset, not the collision-array's element type, was the true cost, and removing it outright was a strict win everywhere rather than a platform trade-off.

**Investigation closed.** The full arc, for future reference: an initial Windows-only slowdown was chased through a `/RTC1`-vs-`-O2` flag-precedence fix, then a suspected BMI2 runtime-detection gap (ruled out with real data), then an `attacks.cpp` `-O2` override that was confirmed reaching the compiler but not helping much, then a `std::vector<bool>` MSVC-codegen fix that overcorrected and regressed macOS, and finally the generation-stamp fix that addressed the actual algorithmic cost. Each step was driven by real CI log data rather than a next guess, per this project's standing debugging discipline — worth keeping that pattern for any future cross-platform performance investigation.

---

## 2026-08-20 (2) — CI: `find_magic_for_square`'s O(size) reset was the actual cost; generation-stamp fix replaces the element-type fix, which regressed macOS

**Results of the `std::vector<uint8_t>` fix (previous entry), from a real 6-job CI run:** it worked exactly as intended on Windows Debug (2732.80s → 180.33s total test time — the main goal). Linux Debug was flat-to-slightly-better (357.50s → 335.50s). But macOS Debug got roughly twice as slow (177.53s → 326.02s), confirmed by pushing the person's own observation into the actual per-test numbers rather than taking it on faith: every `init_all()`-calling test on macOS Debug shows a uniform ~2x jump (e.g. test #12 `init_magic_bitboards is idempotent`: 1.71s → 3.50s; test #52: 1.73s → 3.30s; test #63: 1.62s → 3.64s) — spread evenly across all ~89 such tests, not concentrated in any one, matching the ~149s aggregate difference almost exactly (89 × ~1.7s ≈ 149s).

**Root cause of the regression:** the previous fix's own reasoning was half right — `std::vector<bool>`'s bit-packing genuinely was the problem for MSVC — but assumed the same fix (swap to `std::vector<uint8_t>`) would be neutral-or-better everywhere. On Apple Silicon/Clang, `std::vector<bool>` was apparently already well-optimized (no proxy-object penalty observed there), so the swap only added cost: `uint8_t` is 8x larger per element than a packed bit, and the `used` array (up to 4096 entries for the widest rook masks) gets both `std::fill`-reset and randomly probed on every one of potentially thousands of candidate attempts per square — worse cache locality with no offsetting win on that platform.

**Decision: fix the actual algorithmic cost instead of picking a per-platform element type.** The real dominant cost was never the element type — it was the O(size) `std::fill()` reset itself, executed on every failed candidate. `find_magic_for_square()`'s collision-tracking `used`/`stamp_at` array is replaced with a generation-stamp scheme (`std::vector<std::uint32_t> stamp_at`, incrementing a `stamp` counter per attempt instead of resetting the array): an index counts as "used this attempt" only when `stamp_at[index] == stamp`, so a fresh attempt costs one increment instead of an O(size) fill. This removes the actual bottleneck outright rather than trading which platform it's cheap on, and needs no MSVC-vs-Clang special-casing. `board/attacks.cpp` — **modified** (second change to this function today).

**Verification:** Confirmed by inspection that the stamp scheme is correct at the boundary case: `stamp` starts at 0, `stamp_at` is zero-initialized, and the first candidate attempt increments `stamp` to 1 before any comparison, so `stamp_at[index] == 0 != 1` correctly reads as "not used yet" on the very first attempt — no off-by-one risk from the shared initial value. `stamp` is `uint32_t`; wraparound would require ~4.3 billion attempts on a single square, several orders of magnitude beyond what this search converges in, so not a practical concern. Collision-detection control flow and correctness are otherwise unchanged (same comparisons, same early-exit on collision). No compiler toolchain available in this environment for any of the three platforms — real confirmation is the next CI run's timings across all three Debug jobs, expected to hold Windows Debug's win and *improve* on both the pre-uint8_t Linux/macOS baselines (357.50s and 177.53s respectively) rather than just avoid regressing them, since removing an O(size) fill from a thousands-of-attempts loop should be a strict win everywhere the fill itself was costing anything.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Reverting to `std::vector<bool>` for non-MSVC and keeping `std::vector<uint8_t>` for MSVC via `#if defined(_MSC_VER)` — rejected: adds a permanent per-compiler special case to maintain, and doesn't address the actual cost (the repeated reset), just re-relocates which platform absorbs it.
- Leaving macOS Debug's regression unaddressed since the primary goal (Windows) was achieved — rejected: a session isn't done when the loudest job is fixed if another job measurably regressed as a direct result of the same change; the person's own observation caught this and it's a real, fixable cost.

---

## 2026-08-20 — CI: Windows Debug speed — real root cause found (`std::vector<bool>` in `find_magic_for_square`), BMI2 lead closed

**Diagnostic results, from real Windows Debug CI logs:** the Windows Debug diagnostic step's `WARN()` output confirms the runner detects `"BMI2 POPCNT"` — the BMI2 hypothesis from the two prior entries is ruled out. The Build log confirms `board/attacks.cpp`'s `/O2` override reaches the compiler exactly as expected (`cl : Command line warning D9025 : overriding '/Od' with '/O2'`). Despite both being confirmed working, every `init_all()`-calling test still took a near-constant ~29.7-30.4s, essentially unchanged from before the `-O2` fix and unrelated to test workload — a trivial legality test and a full depth-6 perft test cost almost the same, and total job time (~45.5 min) barely moved from the prior attempt's ~50 min.

**Root cause, found via source inspection rather than further guessing:** `board/attacks.cpp`'s `find_magic_for_square()` — the random magic-number search — runs unconditionally for every square regardless of whether BMI2/PEXT ends up used at runtime (per its own header comment: "This is the portable path, always built"). Its innermost retry loop used a `std::vector<bool> used` collision-tracking array, cleared with `std::fill` on every failed candidate. `std::vector<bool>` is a bit-packed specialization accessed through a proxy reference type rather than a real `bool&`; GCC/Clang's `-O2` optimizes this shape well enough to be invisible (consistent with the measured 33.6s→4.0s win on Linux/macOS, 2026-08-17 entry), but MSVC's `-O2` does not produce comparable codegen for it — which is why the confirmed-applied `/O2` override kept having only a negligible effect on Windows specifically. This makes the BMI2 investigation (the previous three entries) a fair diagnostic step given the evidence available at the time, but ultimately not the actual cause.

**Fix:** `used` changed from `std::vector<bool>` to `std::vector<std::uint8_t>` in `find_magic_for_square()`. Semantically identical — every read/write site already treated it as a boolean flag array — removing only the bit-packing/proxy-reference overhead. This is a genuine portability fix, not an MSVC-only patch, since `std::vector<bool>` is a documented pitfall on any compiler.

**Cleanup:** the two temporary diagnostics from the BMI2 investigation are removed now that it's resolved: `tests/test_smoke.cpp`'s `WARN()` and `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s "Show detected CPU features" step.

**Verification:** Confirmed by direct inspection that the `used[index]` read (`!used[index]`) and write (`used[index] = true`) sites need no further changes — implicit bool conversion behaves identically for `uint8_t`. Collision-detection control flow (same comparisons, same loop structure) is untouched. The actual Windows Debug timing improvement is unverified from this environment (no MSVC toolchain available) — real confirmation is the next CI run's Windows Debug job duration. If the gap doesn't fully close, the Linux/macOS 33.6s→4.0s baseline is available to help isolate any remaining Windows-specific factor.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Continuing to chase the BMI2 lead with Windows-specific code (e.g., forcing the portable path, or OS-conditional logic) — moot now that the real cause is unrelated to BMI2 entirely.
- Rewriting `find_magic_for_square()`'s collision check to avoid the `used` array altogether (e.g., a generation-counter/sentinel scheme avoiding the per-candidate `std::fill`) — a further possible optimization, but out of scope here; the `vector<uint8_t>` swap is the minimal change addressing the measured gap, and a generation-counter version can be revisited if the plain swap doesn't fully close it.

---

## 2026-08-19 (2) — CI: build.ninja diagnostic results — the -O2 override was never the problem; new lead is runtime BMI2 availability

**Diagnostic results, from a real CI run:** the previous entry's `build.ninja`-dumping diagnostic step returned hard evidence, and it overturns the leading hypothesis from that entry. MSVC's own build output shows `cl : Command line warning D9025 : overriding '/Od' with '/O2'` for `board/attacks.cpp` — confirming this specific conflict resolves via the expected rightmost-wins behavior (distinct from the `D8016` class that blocked the first two attempts) now that `/RTC1` is gone from the Debug baseline. The dumped `build.ninja` content directly confirms `attacks.cpp`'s recorded `FLAGS` end in `/O2`, while the otherwise-identical flags for a neighboring, untouched file (`attacks_tests.cpp`) don't have it. The override reaches the compiler and is applied correctly. The previous entry's hypothesis — that it wasn't reaching the compiler — was wrong.

**New lead, with real supporting evidence already in the test suite's own naming:** several existing test names reference behavior conditional on "when the host CPU supports BMI2" (`tests/attacks_tests.cpp`), reflecting that this codebase already has a runtime BMI2/PEXT-vs-portable-magic-bitboard dispatch (`src/support/cpu_features.h`), independent of whether BMI2 support was compiled in. If this specific Windows runner's CPU doesn't support BMI2 at runtime, `init_magic_bitboards()` would take a genuinely different, slower algorithmic path regardless of compiler optimization level — which would explain why `-O2` (confirmed applied) only produced a modest improvement instead of the dramatic one measured on Linux/macOS.

**Decision:** rather than act on this lead as another guess, gather direct evidence for it too. `tests/test_smoke.cpp`'s existing "CPU feature detection runs without crashing" test only asserted `cpu_feature_summary()` is non-null, never actually surfacing what it detected. Added a temporary `WARN()` call reporting the detected summary string. `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s diagnostic step (Windows Debug only) was updated to run this specific test directly against the compiled binary with Catch2's `-s` flag (`ctest --output-on-failure` only shows output for failing tests, and this one passes) — this surfaces the `WARN()` output regardless of pass/fail, giving direct visibility into whether this runner's CPU actually reports BMI2/POPCNT support. The now-answered `build.ninja`-dumping step was removed, replaced by this one.

**Verification:** the modified `tests/test_smoke.cpp` was built and run directly in this environment (confirming the `WARN()` correctly prints `"Detected CPU features: BMI2 POPCNT"` on this sandbox's Linux CPU when run with `-s`, and prints nothing extra under plain `ctest`, preserving normal pass/fail behavior for every other CI job). YAML re-validated for the workflow change. What this specific Windows runner's CPU actually reports is, as with every step of this investigation, only knowable from the next real CI run.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Acting on the BMI2 hypothesis directly (e.g., forcing the portable fallback path everywhere, or adding a Windows-specific code path) without first confirming it's actually what's happening — rejected, for the same reason the previous two guesses were costly: confirming the mechanism before proposing a fix is cheaper than another wrong attempt.

## 2026-08-19 — CI: attempt 3 partially worked (build break fixed); Node 20 warning fixed for real; speed gap now diagnosed with evidence, not guessed at again

**Results of attempt 3 (previous entry), from a real CI run:** the `D8016` build break is genuinely fixed — Windows Debug reached the Test step for the first time since this investigation started. However, the speedup was far smaller than expected: per-test times for the ~89 tests calling `init_all()` dropped only from ~41-54s to ~32-39s (roughly 20-25%), nowhere near the ~8x reduction measured and confirmed on Linux/macOS for the identical source change. Total job time: ~50m, barely better than the original ~1h10m.

**Ruled out first, with real data, before speculating further:** checked whether Windows process-launch overhead itself (a real, documented phenomenon — Windows `CreateProcess` is inherently heavier than POSIX `fork`+`exec`, and Windows Defender's real-time scanning of newly-spawned executables is a well-known source of CI slowness) could be the dominant cost instead of `attacks.cpp`'s optimization level. Checked trivial tests that never call `init_magic_bitboards()` at all (`CPU feature detection`, `TranspositionTable:`/`KillerTable:`/`HistoryTable:` unit tests) — these completed in 0.01-0.03s, identical to Linux. This rules out process/OS-level overhead as the explanation: the cost is still specifically inside `init_magic_bitboards()`, meaning the `-O2` override most likely isn't actually reaching the compiler for that file under Ninja+MSVC the way it does under GCC/Clang, despite the build succeeding without error.

**Decision: gather evidence before attempting a fourth fix, not guess again.** Ninja doesn't echo full compiler invocations by default the way `make VERBOSE=1` does, so there's no way to directly inspect what flags `attacks.cpp` actually received from these CI logs. Rather than propose another change based on inference, `.github/workflows/ci.yml` gained a temporary diagnostic step (Windows Debug only, after Build) that prints the relevant section of the generated `build.ninja` file, giving direct, ground-truth visibility into the actual recorded flags before the next real fix attempt. This is purely additive (no behavior change, no risk to the working build) and is meant to be removed once its purpose is served.

**Also fixed for real this time: the Node 20 deprecation warning's return.** Traced to `ilammy/msvc-dev-cmd@v1`, which still declares itself as a Node 20 action (confirmed against several other real, currently-active projects hitting and reporting the identical warning for the identical action, as recently as within the past few months) — an entirely different action than the one fixed in the 2026-08-16 entry (`actions/checkout`, unrelated). Switched to `egor-tensin/vs-shell@v2`, a real, actively-maintained alternative that performs the same job (setting up the MSVC dev environment/`cl.exe` on `PATH`) but is implemented in PowerShell rather than Node.js — confirmed via a real, documented case of another project (`coin-or/COIN-OR-OptimizationSuite`) making this exact substitution for this exact reason. This resolves the warning at its root rather than suppressing it via the `ACTIONS_ALLOW_USE_UNSECURE_NODE_VERSION`/`FORCE_JAVASCRIPT_ACTIONS_TO_NODE24` environment variables, which don't fix anything and stop working once Node 20 is fully removed from runners (per GitHub's own changelog, fall 2026).

**Verification:** YAML re-validated and parsed structure confirmed for both changes (correct `if:` scoping on the new diagnostic step; `vs-shell` step correctly replaces `msvc-dev-cmd` with matching `arch: x64` input). Neither change can be verified beyond that from this environment — the diagnostic step's actual output, and confirmation the Node 20 warning is gone, both require the next real CI run.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Guessing at a fourth CMake/compiler-flag fix based on the D8016 investigation's reasoning alone — rejected; that reasoning already produced two wrong guesses in this same investigation, and a third without direct evidence would repeat the same mistake.
- Suppressing the Node 20 warning via `ACTIONS_ALLOW_USE_UNSECURE_NODE_VERSION` instead of switching actions — rejected; doesn't fix anything, just defers the eventual hard failure when Node 20 is actually removed.

## 2026-08-18 (2) — CI: Windows Debug speed fix, attempt 3 — Ninja generator + removing /RTC1 from MSVC's Debug baseline

**Context:** after the second failed attempt (previous entry), a pure revert (making the `board/attacks.cpp` optimization override GCC/Clang-only, leaving Windows Debug slow but working) was drafted and explained, but not pushed — a request came back that Windows Debug's speed actually be fixed, not left as a known gap. This entry supersedes that drafted revert with an actual fix, developed with more care after the two prior misses.

**What was actually wrong, on reflection:** the second attempt's `/RTC-` fix assumed MSVC resolves conflicting flags the way GCC/Clang do — rightmost-on-the-command-line wins. Checking Microsoft's own documentation on the specific error code showed this was the wrong model: `D8016` (the error hit both times) is described as an absolute "these options cannot be specified together" check, distinct from the more common `D9025` class of conflicts, which genuinely is rightmost-wins. Appending a counter-flag can't resolve a `D8016`-class conflict regardless of position. Separately, real evidence (a `ninja-build` GitHub issue showing an actual generated MSVC Ninja command line) confirmed that CMake's Visual Studio generator represents Debug's compiler flags as structured per-file MSBuild XML properties, not the plain literal command line that Ninja (and Makefiles, on Linux/macOS) produce — a structural reason the second attempt's override may not even have reached the compiler the way it was assumed to.

**Decision:** two changes, shipped together since neither alone fixes the actual problem:

1. `CMakeLists.txt` (top level) — MSVC's Debug configuration no longer includes `/RTC1` at all. CMake's own MSVC Debug default is `/MDd /Zi /Ob0 /Od /RTC1`; it's now set explicitly to `/MDd /Zi /Ob0 /Od` (identical, minus the one flag causing the conflict). This guarantees no `/O2`-vs-`/RTC1` conflict can occur anywhere in the project for MSVC Debug builds, regardless of generator or flag-ordering semantics — rather than trying to cancel an already-present flag per file, the flag is simply never present to begin with.
2. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` — Windows jobs now configure with `-G Ninja` instead of the default Visual Studio generator (Linux/macOS unchanged). This makes `board/attacks.cpp`'s per-file `-O2` override (`src/CMakeLists.txt`) reach the compiler as a plain trailing flag the same way it already does on GCC/Clang, rather than as a structured MSBuild property. Requires two new Windows-only steps: `ilammy/msvc-dev-cmd@v1` (Ninja doesn't auto-locate `cl.exe` on `PATH` the way the Visual Studio generator does) and `pip install ninja` (Ninja's actual pre-installed availability on GitHub-hosted Windows runners has changed over time and multiple GitHub issues from 2023-2024 show conflicting/unreliable signals about it — pip is the most dependable option, since Python is guaranteed present on every GitHub-hosted runner and PyPI's `ninja` package is a real binary rather than a wrapper).
3. `src/CMakeLists.txt` — the `board/attacks.cpp` override simplified back to a single flag for both toolchains (`-O2` / `/O2`), removing the `/RTC-` companion flag and multi-flag generator-expression-list machinery from the second attempt, since there's nothing left to counter.

**Honest cost, not hidden:** removing `/RTC1` project-wide for MSVC Debug is a real, if narrow, coverage reduction — every MSVC Debug build loses those runtime stack-frame/uninitialized-variable checks, not just `attacks.cpp`'s compilation. Windows Debug already carries zero ASan/UBSan coverage (2026-08-13 revert, logged separately), so `/RTC1` was its only remaining sanitizer-adjacent safety net; this removes it. Linux and macOS Debug builds are completely unaffected and retain full ASan/UBSan coverage exactly as before. This tradeoff was surfaced and discussed before implementation, not decided unilaterally.

**Verification:** real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with all three changed files overlaid in. The GCC/Clang path (Linux, macOS) was fully verified directly: zero warnings, confirmed via verbose Makefile output that `attacks.cpp` compiles with `-g -O0 -fsanitize=address,undefined -fno-omit-frame-pointer ... -O2` (the trailing `-O2` correctly overriding `-O0`, full sanitizer coverage retained) while every other file keeps plain `-O0`/ASan/UBSan unchanged; confirmed the Release build's `attacks.cpp` invocation is still untouched (`-O3`, no stray flags); a sample of tests ran clean. YAML re-validated for the workflow change. The Windows/MSVC/Ninja path — the actual point of this entire fix — could not be directly verified from this environment, as with every MSVC-specific change in this history: no MSVC toolchain or Windows runner available here. Real confirmation is the next CI run's Windows Debug job actually completing and its duration.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Shipping the Ninja migration alone, without the `/RTC1` removal — considered and rejected once it became clear the generator switch alone doesn't change binary runtime speed at all, only build orchestration; the two changes only solve the actual problem together.
- A third attempt at a per-file flag override instead of a project-wide `CMAKE_CXX_FLAGS_DEBUG` change — rejected; per-file overrides are purely additive in CMake, with no portable mechanism to remove an already-applied global flag, which is exactly the class of approach that failed twice already.
- Dropping the Windows Debug job from the matrix entirely rather than continuing to invest in fixing it — not chosen; raised as an option during discussion but a real fix was requested instead.

---

## 2026-08-18 — CI: Windows Debug build break from the attacks.cpp optimization override, fixed

**Decision:** `src/CMakeLists.txt`'s per-file `/O2` override for `board/attacks.cpp` (2026-08-17 entry) now also passes `/RTC-` for MSVC specifically, restructured from a single flag string to a small per-flag generator-expression list so both GCC/Clang (one flag, `-O2`) and MSVC (two flags, `/O2` and `/RTC-`) are handled uniformly.

**What broke and why:** the previous session's fix landed and worked exactly as measured on Linux (1h2m → 6m41s) and macOS (18m29s → 10m6s) in the very next real CI run, but Windows Debug failed outright at the build step with `cl : command line error D8016: '/O2' and '/RTC1' command-line options are incompatible`. CMake's default MSVC Debug configuration enables `/RTC1` (runtime stack-frame and uninitialized-variable checks) as part of `CMAKE_CXX_FLAGS_DEBUG`, and MSVC hard-errors if `/RTC1` and any optimization level above `/Od` both appear on the same compile command — a real, documented MSVC restriction, not something guessed at: this was flagged as an open, unverified risk in the original fix's entry ("Windows Debug's slowdown may also involve MSVC's iterator-debugging checks... but the real confirmation is the next CI run"), and the next CI run supplied the exact, unambiguous answer.

**Fix:** `/RTC-` is MSVC's documented syntax to explicitly disable runtime checks, and later flags override earlier ones on MSVC's command line the same way GCC/Clang take the last `-O` flag as authoritative — appending it after the inherited `/RTC1` resolves the conflict for this one file without touching `/RTC1` (or anything else) anywhere else in the Debug build.

**Verification:** real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with the one-file CMakeLists.txt change applied. Confirmed via verbose Makefile output that GCC's compile line for `attacks.cpp` is completely unchanged by the restructure (still ends in a single trailing `-O2`, same as before); confirmed the Release build's `attacks.cpp` invocation is still untouched (plain `-O3`). Ran a batch of unrelated fast tests to confirm no other regression from the CMakeLists.txt restructure — all passed. The MSVC-specific `/RTC-` addition itself could not be directly verified (no MSVC toolchain available in this environment, same limitation noted in the original entry) — real confirmation is the next CI run's Windows Debug job actually reaching the test step instead of failing at build.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Drop the optimization override for MSVC entirely, accepting Windows Debug staying slow — rejected; the whole point of the original fix was cross-platform, and the actual error message pointed to a precise, standard, well-understood fix rather than an unknown problem requiring a fallback.
- Lower Windows Debug's optimization override to `/O1` instead of `/O2` to reduce risk — rejected; the reported error was specifically about `/RTC1` incompatibility with optimization in general, not about which optimization level, so `/O1` would have failed identically.

---

## 2026-08-17 (2) — Internal Iterative Reduction: implementation, thresholds, and a real investigation into node-count volatility before shipping

**Decision:** `negamax()` (`search.cpp`) now reduces its own `depth` by `kIIRReduction` (1) whenever a node has no transposition-table entry at all (`!probe.hit`) and the unreduced `depth` is already at least `kIIRMinDepth` (4) — CPW's modern replacement for Internal Iterative Deepening: instead of spending extra search effort *at this node* up front to manufacture a move-ordering hint, the node is simply searched slightly shallower, trusting iterative deepening's own outer loop to naturally revisit it with a real TT-move hint on a later, less-reduced iteration. `depth` is reassigned in place so the move loop's recursive calls and the TT store at the end both correctly reflect what was actually searched. Deliberately does not apply at the root (`search_root()`) — the root is the exact position a caller asked to search to a specific depth, and silently searching it shallower would misreport `depth_completed`.

**This is explicitly not an "exact" technique, unlike PVS/TT/aspiration windows.** Those three were provably equivalent to plain full-window alpha-beta regardless of how they were called. IIR is a genuine heuristic approximation — its safety net is iterative deepening's own self-correction across iterations, not per-call exactness. A bare `search_fixed_depth()` call with no shallower iteration to have already populated the TT has no such correction available, so it's a real (if generally small) accuracy/depth trade at any one node, same as every other engine that uses this technique.

**A real investigation, not just a clean-looking benchmark, before shipping:** an initial depth-8 benchmark on the start position showed IIR making node count *worse* by ~88% (832,406 vs. 441,714 nodes) and changing the reported score at that depth (10 vs. 0, though the best move still agreed). That result was not dismissed or hidden — it was investigated properly before anything was presented. Tracing node counts across depths 4-9 on the start position showed a genuinely volatile, non-monotonic pattern: identical at depth 4-5 (IIR not yet triggered), a small win at depth 6 (-3.5%), a larger win at depth 7 (-26.7%, but with the reported score/best-move actually diverging from the non-IIR run at that depth), a large loss at depth 8 (+88%), and another loss at depth 9 (+28.6%, though score/move matched there). Repeating the same sweep on Kiwipete (a standard, sharp, asymmetric test position, not the symmetric start position) told a much cleaner story: small, consistent wins at every depth tested (depth 6: -0.4%, depth 7: -0.12%, depth 8: -5.4%), with the reported score and best move identical to the non-IIR run at every single depth — no divergence anywhere.

**Conclusion drawn from that data:** the start position's volatility is very likely alpha-beta search's own well-documented sensitivity to small move-ordering perturbations, not a bug in this implementation — the start position is an unusually symmetric position with many roughly-equal "book" opening moves, exactly the condition under which tiny ordering differences are known to cascade into large, close-to-arbitrary swings in total node count in either direction, in any correctly-implemented alpha-beta search, IIR or not. Kiwipete's clean, monotonically-positive results across three separate depths, with zero score/move divergence, are the more representative signal for whether this implementation is actually correct and beneficial. The one real correctness-relevant observation (the depth-7 start-position best-move/score divergence) is expected and accounted for by IIR's own documented nature: it's a heuristic, not an exact technique, and a single fixed-max-depth run at that exact depth is precisely the case with the least self-correction opportunity — by depth 9 on the same position, the best move had already converged back to matching the non-IIR run.

**Rationale for shipping despite the imperfect start-position numbers:** hand-tuning `kIIRMinDepth`/`kIIRReduction` based on two positions' worth of data would be overfitting to an arbitrarily small sample — real tuning of search constants like these is explicitly what ROADMAP.md's future Texel/SPSA tuner (Phase 5) exists for, once there's a proper multi-position bench suite to tune against rather than ad hoc spot checks. The chosen values (`kIIRMinDepth = 4`, `kIIRReduction = 1`) are common, modest starting points used elsewhere in the field, not claimed to be already-optimal for Nightwing specifically (same caveat already on record for `kAspirationInitialDelta`).

**Verification:** real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with the two changed files (`search.cpp`, `search.h`) and one updated test file (`tests/search_tests.cpp`, two new tests) overlaid in. Release: zero warnings, **all 173 `ctest` cases passed unchanged** — no existing test needed updating, because every existing fixed-depth test in the suite requests a depth shallow enough (<=3) that IIR's `depth >= 4` gate can never trigger internally, so none of them could have been affected either way. Two new tests specifically exercise IIR: a rigorously-verified (independently brute-force-checked with `python-chess`, not just recalled from memory) forced mate-in-exactly-3 position searched at `max_depth=6`, confirming a genuine mate is still found correctly with IIR active across several iterative-deepening iterations; and a coarse depth-5 regression/safety-net check confirming `search_fixed_depth()` completes normally and returns a legal, bounded result at a depth where IIR can engage. Debug (ASan+UBSan): zero warnings; both new tests ran to completion clean. Node-count investigation (Release, `-O3`, outside `ctest`) covered above.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Restricting IIR to PV nodes only (wide-window searches, excluding PVS's own null-window probes) — considered as a possible fix once the start-position volatility first appeared, before the Kiwipete data clarified it wasn't a bug; not implemented, since the broader investigation showed the current unrestricted condition is already sound and the volatility was a benchmark-position artifact, not a design flaw to work around.
- Raising `kIIRMinDepth` to be more conservative given the start-position numbers — rejected for the reason above (overfitting to too small a sample); revisit once a real tuning framework exists.
- Shipping without investigating the depth-8 regression, since it "still passed all unit tests" — rejected; passing unit tests doesn't establish that a claimed efficiency technique actually delivers efficiency, and shipping a regression under the banner of "reduce depth on nodes with no TT move" without checking whether it actually reduces work would have been a real, avoidable gap between what was claimed and what was verified.

## 2026-08-17 — CI: Debug jobs' 1-hour+ runtime traced to one file, fixed with a per-file optimization override

**Decision:** `src/CMakeLists.txt` now compiles `board/attacks.cpp` (magic bitboard table generation) at `-O2` (`/O2` on MSVC), even inside the overall Debug build's `-O0` + ASan/UBSan configuration, via `set_source_files_properties()` guarded by a `$<CONFIG:Debug>` generator expression so Release builds are unaffected. Every other file keeps the Debug build's normal `-O0`, full sanitizer instrumentation, and debug symbols unchanged.

**Investigation:** Debug CI jobs were taking 1h+ (Linux Debug: 1h2m, Windows Debug: 1h10m per a real run's logs) while Release jobs ran in 1-6 minutes. Downloaded and analyzed the actual CI logs rather than guessing: of 171 tests, 89 each individually cost ~41-54 seconds (Linux/Windows) or ~10-15 seconds (macOS) — accounting for 99.9%+ of total test time — while the other ~82 trivial tests ran in milliseconds. Critically, Windows Debug showed the identical signature despite having zero ASan instrumentation (reverted 2026-08-13 after hanging that runner), ruling out sanitizer overhead as the root cause. The common factor: every one of the 89 slow tests calls `init_magic_bitboards()` via its own `init_all()` helper, and because this codebase deliberately runs each Catch2 `TEST_CASE` as its own process (for isolation — see `tests/*.cpp`'s own comments), that one-time setup cost gets fully redone from scratch, unoptimized, in 89 separate process launches.

Verified directly by timing `init_magic_bitboards()` in isolation at each relevant configuration:

| Config | Time |
|---|---|
| `-O0` (plain Debug) | 5.4s |
| `-O0` + ASan/UBSan (actual Linux Debug CI config) | 33.6s |
| `-O2` (no sanitizers) | 358ms |
| `-O2` + ASan/UBSan (sanitizers still fully active) | 4.0s |

The last row is the fix that was applied: an 8.3x reduction with zero loss of sanitizer coverage on that file.

**Rationale for a narrow per-file fix over restructuring the test suite into fewer processes:** the data argues against a broader architecture change. Trivial tests that never call `init_magic_bitboards()` cost 0.03-0.04s each, confirming plain process-launch overhead was never the actual bottleneck — it only looked that way because one specific piece of code's cost was being multiplied by 89. Fixing that code directly is a two-line CMake change; reworking which tests share a process would be a much larger change to test architecture for a problem the data shows it isn't actually causing, and would give up the crash-isolation property that per-process tests provide under ASan's abort-on-error behavior (one sanitizer violation can't hide the pass/fail status of the other 170 tests in the same run). This fix also scales forward: any future test that calls `init_all()` now pays the small, fixed ~4s cost automatically, rather than CI time growing worse and worse as more tests are added across the remaining ROADMAP.md phases.

**Verification:** real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with the one-line CMakeLists.txt change applied. Confirmed via verbose Makefile output that the actual compiler invocation for `attacks.cpp` ends with `-O2` overriding the preceding `-O0` (compilers take the last `-O` flag on the command line as authoritative), with `-fsanitize=address,undefined` still present; separately confirmed the Release build's `attacks.cpp` invocation is untouched (still plain `-O3`, no stray `-O2`). Ran 23 of the 89 previously-slow tests (the full `perft:`, `quiescence:`, and `SEE:` groups) under the real Debug/ASan/UBSan configuration: all 23 passed, averaging 4.45s each (down from ~41-46s each in the original CI run), consistent with the isolated 4.0s measurement above. Extrapolated across all 89 affected tests, this should bring the Linux Debug job from ~1h2m down to roughly 7-8 minutes. The Windows/macOS-specific magnitude of improvement hasn't been directly verified (no MSVC or Apple Silicon toolchain available in this environment) — Windows Debug's slowdown may also involve MSVC's iterator-debugging checks in addition to plain `-O0`, which this fix's `/O2` override should address the same way, but the real confirmation is the next CI run's actual job durations across all three platforms.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Stop running every `TEST_CASE` as its own process (run all tests in one process per binary, Catch2's default) — rejected; the data shows this isn't where the actual cost is, and it would trade away meaningful crash isolation for a problem a narrower fix already solves.
- Cache/reuse Catch2's build across CI runs — investigated first as the likely culprit before the log analysis, but ruled out: Catch2's own configure+build cost was under a minute total in the same logs, negligible next to the 89 × ~41s test-time cost.
- Apply the optimization override more broadly (e.g. all of `board/`) rather than just `attacks.cpp` — rejected; `init_masks()` and `init_zobrist_keys()` both measured at 0ms even at `-O0` + ASan, so there was no cost to address there, and a narrower change is easier to reason about and lower-risk.

## 2026-08-16 (4) — CI: readable job names via matrix.include, replacing the default cross-product labels

**Decision:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s matrix changed from a plain `os: [...] x build_type: [...]` cross-product to an explicit `matrix.include` list of all 6 combinations, each carrying an added `os_name` field (`Linux`/`macOS`/`Windows`), plus a job-level `name: ${{ matrix.os_name }} ${{ matrix.build_type }}`. Jobs now show as "Linux Release," "macOS Debug," etc. in the Actions UI instead of GitHub's default "build-and-test (ubuntu-latest, Release)" label.

**Rationale:** purely cosmetic/readability — the primary usage context is mobile-only, reading the Actions UI on a narrow screen where the default matrix labels truncate awkwardly (per the screenshot prompting this change). No change to what actually runs: `runs-on` still resolves to the same `ubuntu-latest`/`macos-latest`/`windows-latest` runners, and `os_name` is a new field used only for display, not referenced by `runs-on` or any step.

**Verification:** YAML re-validated (`python3 -c "import yaml; yaml.safe_load(...)"`), and the parsed structure inspected to confirm all 6 combinations are present with the intended `os`/`os_name`/`build_type` values. As with the previous `actions/checkout` version bump, workflow files can't be fully exercised outside GitHub itself — real confirmation is the next push's job list showing the new names.

**Alternatives considered:**
- A reusable/matrix-level `env` or `${{ fromJSON(...) }}` lookup table instead of `matrix.include`'s explicit list — rejected; `include` is more verbose (6 explicit entries vs. 2 short arrays) but is the standard, most-readable way to attach an extra display-only field per combination, and 6 entries is small enough that the verbosity cost is negligible.

---

## 2026-08-16 (3) — Quiescence search + SEE: scope, the depth==1 PVS fix it unlocked, and two test-construction bugs caught and fixed

**Decision:** Two new modules. `src/search/see.h`/`see.cpp` implements Static Exchange Evaluation (the classic CPW swap-off algorithm: iteratively find the least valuable attacker of a square, alternate sides, then resolve the resulting gain array backward with each side choosing to stop or continue optimally). `src/search/quiescence.h`/`quiescence.cpp` implements quiescence search: at negamax()'s depth <= 0 base case (`search.cpp`), instead of returning a raw `eval::evaluate()` call, the search now continues through captures (always) and check-giving quiet moves (only at the very first quiescence ply — see below), pruning captures with negative SEE, until a quiet position is reached. Several notable decisions:

1. **Checks only at the first quiescence ply, not recursively.** Unlike captures, which strictly deplete the board's material each ply (self-bounding to roughly 30 plies max), non-capturing checks don't reduce anything — recursively chasing every check-evade-check-evade chain risks running far deeper than a horizon-effect fix should cost, especially since Nightwing doesn't yet have repetition detection wired into search (a separate, still-unchecked ROADMAP.md item) to recognize and cut off a repeating pattern on its own. `include_checks` is `true` only on negamax()'s initial call into quiescence, `false` on every recursive call quiescence makes into itself.
2. **A defensive `kMaxQuiescencePly` (32) hard cap, independent of the above.** Given the same lack-of-repetition-detection concern, an explicit ply cap on quiescence's own recursion exists as a rare-pathological-case safety net (a contrived repeating-check position could otherwise, in principle, recurse further than intended) — not expected to be hit in normal play, but cheap insurance given what's not yet in place to bound it another way.
3. **No PVS null-windowing, no TT/killer/history threading, inside quiescence itself.** Quiescence's own move loop is plain fail-soft alpha-beta, and it doesn't currently probe/store the transposition table or use `KillerTable`/`HistoryTable` (search/ordering.h) — a deliberate, documented scope-narrowing for this first pass (common in introductory/staged qsearch implementations), not an oversight. Ordering within quiescence is a simple stable MVV-LVA-descending sort (reusing `mvv_lva_score()`, newly exposed publicly from `ordering.h` for exactly this reuse rather than a second, drifting copy), with non-captures scored to sort after every capture.
4. **`negamax()`'s stale `depth == 1` PVS special case removed, unlocking real additional pruning.** That special case existed specifically because a depth-1 move's child used to be a depth-0 leaf resolved by a raw eval call that never consulted alpha/beta at all — a null-window probe there genuinely could never prune anything. Quiescence search DOES meaningfully respond to whatever window it's given (its own stand-pat comparison and cutoff logic), so that premise no longer holds; the special case was folded into the general PVS branch. Root's OWN equivalent special case (`search_root()`) was deliberately left as-is: a depth-1 REQUEST only ever reaches the root branch via `search_fixed_depth(pos, 1)` or iterative deepening's mandatory first iteration, both of which always use the full `(-inf, +inf)` window (no previous score to aspirate around yet), so `beta` is always `kInfinity` there and a null-window probe against it has nothing to gain from skipping to — changing it would add complexity for zero practical effect given how it's actually invoked.
5. **`++nodes` moved to after negamax()'s depth<=0 check, to avoid double-counting with quiescence's own counter.** Previously, `++nodes` happened unconditionally at the top of `negamax()`, including on calls that immediately delegated to a raw eval return. Now that depth<=0 delegates to `quiescence()` (which increments `nodes` itself, once per its own top-level and recursive calls), incrementing `nodes` in `negamax()` BEFORE that delegation would double-count the same conceptual node. `negamax()`'s own `++nodes` was moved to fire only on the depth >= 1 path, so a depth<=0 call's node-count contribution comes entirely and exactly once from quiescence's own counting.

**Two test-construction bugs, caught by the real build+test cycle before anything was presented:** the SEE-pruning and "good capture actually searched" quiescence tests both used a position that also had unrelated quiet checking moves available (`Qh5+`, `Qe2+`, `Qa4+`), initially unnoticed — with `include_checks=true`, those got explored too, inflating the node count these tests were checking and (for the pruning test) causing an outright failure (`nodes == 5`, not `1`), while for the "good capture" test it would have silently passed even if the capture itself had been wrongly pruned, since the checks alone were enough to push `nodes > 1`. Both fixed by setting `include_checks=false`, which correctly isolates pure capture-ordering/pruning behavior from the separately-tested `include_checks` flag itself. Separately, an early draft of the checkmate test used a FEN one move before an actual mate (still had 8 legal replies) rather than an already-checkmated position, and an early draft of the stalemate test's FEN was wrong (never independently verified before being written into the file) — both caught by cross-checking every new test FEN against `python-chess` (a new step this session; see Verification below) before the first build attempt, and again when the first real test run's actual failures pointed at exactly these two files.

**Rationale for reusing eval::material_value() but overriding King in SEE:** `eval::material_value(King)` is correctly `{0, 0}` for evaluation purposes (king "material" isn't real tradeable value), but SEE needs the opposite signal — a king must register as the least desirable attacker and a catastrophic "capture" if a hypothetical exchange ever reaches one. `see_piece_value()` reuses `eval::material_value()` for Pawn..Queen (one source of truth, same values MVV-LVA already uses) but substitutes a large sentinel (20,000 — above a queen, comfortably below `kMateThreshold`/`kMateScore`) for King specifically.

**Verification:** Every new test FEN (7 in `see_tests.cpp`, 8 in `quiescence_tests.cpp`) was independently checked against `python-chess` (validity, check status, legal move list, and specifically which moves give check) BEFORE the first build attempt — a new step this session, adopted specifically because quiescence/SEE test correctness depends on precise tactical properties of hand-built positions that are easy to get subtly wrong by hand-reasoning alone (as the two bugs above demonstrate even with this step in place). Real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with all new/changed files overlaid in. Release: zero warnings; **first real run surfaced exactly the two test bugs above and nothing else** (170 other cases green immediately) — after fixing both, **all 171 `ctest` cases passed**, including the depth-1 exact-node-count test unchanged (confirmed empirically, not just predicted: no White first move creates an immediate capture or check opportunity for Black, so quiescence contributes zero extra nodes for any of the 20 root branches) and the depth-2 mate-in-1 and depth-3 iterative-deepening-vs-direct tests, all passing with no assertion changes needed. Debug (ASan+UBSan): zero warnings; all 8 quiescence tests, all 7 SEE tests (specifically including the en-passant occupancy edge case — the one place SEE's occupancy bookkeeping diverges from every other capture type), and the depth-1/mate-in-1 search tests all ran to completion clean. Separately, a real depth-5 benchmark on the start position, compiled against both this session's code and a fresh pre-quiescence `main` checkout: **15,612 → 10,780 nodes, and the reported score/best-move actually changed (70/e2e4 → 40/b1c3)** — both expected and correct: quiescence search is specifically meant to change what a fixed-depth search reports by resolving tactics past the nominal horizon instead of trusting a possibly-mid-exchange static eval, so a different, more accurate answer at the same requested depth is the technique working as intended, not a regression.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Extending checks to every quiescence ply, not just the first — rejected (see decision 1); the standard, documented mitigation for the exact runaway risk described.
- Threading TT/killers/history through quiescence in this same pass — rejected for now; a legitimate future enhancement, but real added complexity (mate-distance handling, generation/age semantics, etc. all apply equally inside quiescence) better done as its own deliberate, tested step rather than folded into an already-large session.
- Keeping negamax()'s stale depth==1 special case rather than removing it — rejected (see decision 4); leaving demonstrably-outdated reasoning in place once its premise was known to be false would be worse than either fixing the behavior or at minimum correcting the comment, and the fix itself was small, contained, and unlocked genuine additional pruning at zero correctness risk (same PVS mechanism already proven correct at every other depth).

---

## 2026-08-16 (2) — Aspiration windows: root-only, symmetric-widening retry, mate-score bypass

**Decision:** `search_iterative_deepening()`'s depth >= 2 iterations (`search.cpp`) now search through a narrow window centered on the previous iteration's score, per CPW "Aspiration Windows," instead of always using the full `(-inf, +inf)` range. `search_root()` (the shared root-move-loop core behind both public entry points) now takes `aspiration_alpha`/`aspiration_beta` as explicit parameters rather than hardcoding the full window internally — `search_fixed_depth()` and depth-1's always-unconditional first iteration both still pass the full window explicitly, since there's no previous score to aspirate around in either case. Four notable decisions:

1. **Root-only, not touching `negamax()`.** Only `search_root()`'s own window changed; `negamax()` itself is completely untouched by this session's diff. Aspiration windows are specifically a root-level technique (CPW): only the root call has a "previous iteration's score" to estimate from, and narrowing the root's window automatically narrows every internal node's effective window too, since they all derive from it through the existing PVS/negamax recursion.
2. **Symmetric widening, doubling the failing side only.** On a fail-low (`next.score <= window_alpha`), only `window_alpha` widens (doubling the delta from the original center); on a fail-high, only `window_beta` widens. Re-searching wider on the side that didn't fail would waste a retry proving something already known. Widening is unconditionally correct regardless of how many retries it takes: once a window reaches the full `(-inf,+inf)` range on both sides, the search is exact by construction (identical to `search_fixed_depth()`'s own always-full-window search), so the retry loop is guaranteed to terminate (bounded by roughly log2(kInfinity/25) ≈ 16 worst-case doublings, essentially never reached in practice — 0 or 1 retries is the common case).
3. **Mate-score bypass.** When the previous iteration's score is already within `kMateThreshold` of `kMateScore` (search.h), the next iteration skips aspiration entirely and uses the full window directly. Mate scores are a completely different numeric regime from centipawn evaluations — aspirating a ~25cp-wide window around a score near 32000 would guarantee an immediate, pointless fail on the first attempt every time, with zero chance of the real benefit (an already-narrow, already-correct estimate) aspiration windows are for.
4. **`search_root()`'s TT-store bound classification generalized, not just re-labeled.** With a finite window now possible, `search_root()`'s own result can genuinely be a Lower or Upper bound rather than always Exact — its TT store at the end now classifies via the same `alpha <= aspiration_alpha ? Upper : alpha >= aspiration_beta ? Lower : Exact` pattern `negamax()` already used at node level (CPW "Node Types"), rather than always writing `Bound::Exact` as it did before this session. This is a genuine correctness fix made necessary by the aspiration-window change, not a pre-existing bug: with the old, always-full window, `Bound::Exact` was unconditionally correct (fail-high/fail-low were structurally impossible when beta was literally `kInfinity`), so this generalization doesn't change behavior for `search_fixed_depth()` or depth 1's own call — verified by all 155 pre-existing tests passing unchanged (see Verification below).

**Rationale for delta = 25 centipawns:** a commonly-cited real-engine starting value for this technique; not yet tuned for Nightwing specifically. Flagged in a code comment as a plausible future Texel/SPSA tuning target once that tuner exists (ROADMAP.md Phase 5), rather than presented as an already-verified-optimal constant.

**Verification:** Real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with `search/search.h`/`search/search.cpp` overlaid in (the only two files touched — `negamax()`'s untouched-ness meant no other file needed changes). Release: zero warnings, **all 155 `ctest` cases passed unchanged** — no test needed updating this time (unlike the move-ordering session), which is itself a useful confirmation: aspiration windows are provably exact when implemented correctly, and the test suite agreeing with that on the first try is evidence the fail-high/fail-low detection and retry logic are correct, not just plausible. Debug (ASan+UBSan): zero warnings; the three most correctness-sensitive search tests specifically re-verified clean — the exact depth-1 node-count test (confirming depth 1 still bypasses aspiration entirely, unaffected), the depth-2 mate-in-1 test (confirming the mate-score bypass path), and critically the depth-3 iterative-deepening-vs-direct-search test (the one that actually exercises the new retry loop at a real, non-mate depth). Separately, a real (non-`ctest`) depth-5 benchmark on the start position was compiled and run against both this session's code and the pre-aspiration-window version fetched fresh from `main`: **19,051 nodes (before) → 15,612 nodes (after)**, ~18% fewer nodes, with an identical score (70) and best move (e2e4) — the expected result for a correctly-implemented aspiration window: same answer, reached more cheaply.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Widening both sides of the window on any fail, rather than only the side that actually failed — rejected; strictly more re-search work for no correctness benefit, and the asymmetric version isn't meaningfully harder to implement or reason about.
- Applying aspiration windows inside `negamax()` itself (at every PV node, not just the root) — rejected for this pass; CPW documents this as root-specific for good reason (only the root has a stable "previous iteration" to estimate from), and internal nodes already get PVS's own null-window mechanism for a related but distinct purpose.
- A fixed, non-doubling retry window (e.g. always jump straight to the full window on any fail, no intermediate widening) — rejected; loses the benefit of a second, still-narrower-than-full attempt in the common case where the first guess was only slightly off, which is cheap insurance against the log-bounded worst case.

## 2026-08-16 — CI: actions/checkout bumped v4 → v7 (Node 20 deprecation warnings)

**Decision:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s `actions/checkout@v4` bumped to `actions/checkout@v7` (currently latest, v7.0.1). No other change to the workflow.

**Rationale:** all 6 green CI jobs were carrying a "Node.js 20 is deprecated" annotation — `actions/checkout@v4` ships a Node 20 runtime, and GitHub Actions runners are now force-running it on Node 24 instead (with a warning) ahead of Node 20's full removal. `actions/checkout` v5+ was rebuilt to run on Node 24 natively, so this is a straight version bump with no workflow-behavior change: this job uses only the default checkout (no `with:` inputs), and neither v5's, v6's, nor v7's breaking changes (credential-persistence file location; `pull_request_target` checkout-safety defaults) apply here — this workflow only triggers on `push`/`pull_request`, never `pull_request_target`, and never persists/uses git credentials for anything beyond the checkout itself.

**Verification:** YAML re-validated (`python3 -c "import yaml; yaml.safe_load(...)"`) after the edit. GitHub Actions workflow files can't be fully exercised outside GitHub itself, so the real confirmation is the next push's Annotations panel showing 0 warnings.

**Alternatives considered:**
- v5 (the first Node-24 release) instead of v7 — rejected; no reason to pin behind the latest stable major when nothing in this simple workflow is sensitive to the intervening versions' changes, and staying current avoids needing to revisit this again soon.

## 2026-08-15 (4) — Move ordering: scoring bands, root-level TT interaction added, and a node-count test assertion updated

**Decision:** New `src/search/ordering.{h,cpp}` implements ROADMAP.md's Phase 3 "Move ordering" item exactly: TT move, MVV-LVA captures, killer moves, history heuristic (SEE and counter-moves are ARCHITECTURE.md's fuller eventual scheme but not this item). `negamax()` in `search.cpp` now calls `order_moves()` on the generated move list before its own loop, using the earlier TT probe's move as a hint (even one too shallow to trigger a cutoff — still useful for ordering), and records killer/history updates on a beta cutoff caused by a quiet, non-promotion move. `search_root()` does the same for the root's own move list. Three notable decisions:

1. **Score bands, not a single continuous formula.** `TT move (2,000,000) > captures (1,000,000 + MVV-LVA, range ~100-9000) > non-capture promotions (900,000 + promoted piece value) > killer 1 (800,000) > killer 2 (799,000) > history score (capped at 8192)`, each band with enough headroom that no lower band's maximum reaches into the one above. Non-capture promotions getting their own band above killers isn't literally named in CPW's MVV-LVA article, but the same "try the forcing, probably-strong move first" logic clearly applies to promotions — treated as a from-scratch extension of the general idea rather than a separate cited technique.
2. **Root-level TT probe/store added this session, closing last session's explicit deferral.** The 2026-08-15 (3) TT entry's sub-decision 4 deferred root-level TT interaction specifically "without move ordering yet using a TT-move hint" — now that ordering exists, `search_root()` probes the root position for an ordering hint (only that — never for an early return; the root always reviews its full legal move list to report a genuine UCI-quality best move, unlike internal nodes which can trust a sufficiently-deep proven bound) and stores its own result at the end (always `Bound::Exact`, since the root's open `(-inf,+inf)` window with no early cutoff means whatever score it settles on is exact by construction, not merely a bound).
3. **A pre-existing `search_tests.cpp` assertion was updated, not the code.** `search_iterative_deepening()` deliberately shares one `TranspositionTable`/`KillerTable`/`HistoryTable` across its own depth iterations (the whole point of pairing ordering with iterative deepening — see the 2026-08-15 (3) TT entry's sub-decision 1, which already anticipated this: "without move ordering... supplying a TT-move hint" was the reason cross-iteration TT reuse gave minimal benefit *before* this session). With real ordering now in place, that cross-iteration reuse is real: on the start position, `search_iterative_deepening(pos, 3)` now visits **660** total nodes across its three iterations, while a cold `search_fixed_depth(pos, 3)` visits **880** — i.e. accumulated depth-1 + depth-2 + depth-3-with-inherited-ordering-hints is now *cheaper* than a single depth-3 pass starting cold. This is the intended payoff of the feature, not a regression, but it broke a `search_tests.cpp` assertion (`id.nodes > direct.nodes`) whose own comment explicitly assumed "No TT/move-ordering shared between iterations yet (Phase 2)" — a premise this session's work directly and intentionally invalidates. The correctness assertions in that test (`best_move`/`score`/`depth_completed` all matching between `id` and `direct`) are untouched and still pass — PVS, the TT, and move ordering are all exact techniques, none of them are allowed to change the final answer, only how cheaply it's reached. Only the stale inequality (and its outdated comment) was replaced, with a weaker but still-meaningful sanity bound (`id.nodes >= 20`, the fixed depth-1-alone node count) and a comment explaining exactly why, with the real numbers, so a future reader doesn't mistake this for a silently-loosened test.

**Rationale for the score bands' relative order:** TT move first is universal practice (CPW, every serious engine) — a proven-or-recently-good move from a shallower/transposed search is the single best available ordering signal. Captures before quiets, and MVV-LVA within captures, is the direct ROADMAP.md wording. Promotions ranked between captures and killers reflects that a queen promotion is almost always strong, similar in spirit to a good capture, but a plain non-capture promotion is a genuinely different move class from a real material-winning capture so it sits just below. Killers above history reflects that a killer is a *specific, very recent* refutation at this exact ply, a stronger and more targeted signal than history's accumulated-across-many-positions score.

**Verification:** Real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with all changed/new files overlaid in (`search/ordering.h`, `search/ordering.cpp`, `search/search.h`, `search/search.cpp`, both `CMakeLists.txt` files, `tests/ordering_tests.cpp`, and the one updated assertion in `tests/search_tests.cpp`). Release: zero warnings, **all 155 `ctest` cases passed** (136 prior + 19 new ordering tests, after fixing the one stale assertion above — the *first* run, before that fix, showed exactly the one expected failure and no others, confirming the change's blast radius was exactly what this entry describes and nothing more). Debug (ASan+UBSan): zero warnings; all 9 fast `KillerTable`/`HistoryTable` cases and 5 of 10 `order_moves()` cases ran to completion clean (the other 5 not reached before this sandbox's already-documented per-test ASan launch-overhead ceiling — not a new issue); the depth-1 exact-node-count and mate-in-1 `search_fixed_depth` cases specifically re-verified clean under ASan+UBSan too, given they're the two most correctness-sensitive tests in the whole suite for this kind of change.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Not sharing killers/history/TT hints across iterative-deepening's own iterations, to keep the old `id.nodes > direct.nodes` assertion trivially true — rejected; that would have thrown away the actual point of this ROADMAP.md item and left real engine-strength value on the table just to avoid updating one test assertion. Fixing the test's now-invalid premise was the correct move given "Tests must stay green" exists to catch *unintended* breakage, not to freeze a specific node count that a working, deliberate optimization is expected to change.
- Giving non-capture promotions the SAME band as captures (both under one "big/forcing move" bucket) — rejected; keeping them distinct, with captures strictly on top, matches the literal ROADMAP.md wording ("MVV-LVA captures" specifically) more precisely and avoids a queen promotion outranking a hanging-queen capture for no good reason.
- Leaving root-level TT interaction deferred another session — rejected; the exact trigger condition the prior entry named (move ordering existing) was met within this same session, so implementing it now avoided a needless second pass through `search_root()` later.

## 2026-08-15 (3) — Transposition table: layout, replacement scheme, mate-distance handling, and per-call lifetime

**Decision:** New `src/search/tt.{h,cpp}` implements `TranspositionTable` per ARCHITECTURE.md's spec: power-of-2-sized array of 4-entry, 64-byte-aligned buckets (one cache line each), each `TTEntry` exactly 16 bytes (`uint64_t key`, `uint16_t move_raw`, `int16_t score`, `uint8_t depth`, `uint8_t bound_and_age` packing a 2-bit `Bound` + 6-bit age) — both sizes enforced by `static_assert`. `negamax()` in `search.cpp` now prefetches, probes, and stores against a `TranspositionTable&` passed down through the whole recursion; `search_root()` (the extracted, shared root-move-loop core behind both public entry points) does not yet probe/store for the root position itself. Four sub-decisions, each with real alternatives considered:

1. **Per-call lifetime, not a persistent global (despite ARCHITECTURE.md's eventual "single global TT").** Each top-level `search_fixed_depth()` call constructs its own fresh, private table; `search_iterative_deepening()` constructs one and shares it across its own depth-1..max_depth iterations (calling `new_search()` once per iteration), but that instance is never shared with any OTHER top-level call. **Rationale:** a genuinely persistent global would make `tests/search_tests.cpp`'s several same-process, sequential-top-level-call comparisons (e.g. `id.nodes == fixed.nodes` for max_depth=1; `id.nodes > direct.nodes` for max_depth=3) silently order-dependent on whichever call happened to run first and populate a shared table — not a bug in the TT itself, but exactly the kind of test-fragility "Tests must stay green" exists to avoid, and not yet fixable properly since the real fix (a `ucinewgame`-triggered clear, tied to the UCI `Hash` option) doesn't exist until Phase 8. Traced through concretely: because each ID iteration always probes with a DEEPER remaining-depth requirement than the previous iteration provided, a stored shallower entry is essentially never "deep enough" to trigger a cutoff anyway without move ordering (a separate, still-unchecked item) supplying a TT-move hint — so a persistent global wouldn't even have delivered much real benefit yet, making this an easy, low-cost deferral rather than a real capability sacrifice.
2. **Mate-distance ply adjustment on store/probe, per CPW "Score in TT."** `negamax()` already encodes mate scores root-relative-within-one-search-call (`kMateScore - ply`, existing PVS-session convention, unchanged). Storing that value verbatim and reusing it from a DIFFERENT ply (a different top-level call, or the same position reached via a transposition at a different distance-from-root) would misreport mate distance. `store()` converts to a ply-independent "intrinsic" value (`score + ply` for winning mates, `score - ply` for losing ones) before writing; `probe()` converts back (`score - ply` / `score + ply`) relative to whatever ply it's probed from. Covered by two new `tt_tests.cpp` cases that store at one ply and probe at a different one, checking the exact resulting magnitude — this is historically the single most common TT bug (CPW explicitly calls it out), so it got direct, targeted test coverage rather than relying only on `search_tests.cpp`'s indirect mate-in-1 check.
3. **A probe that doesn't immediately resolve the window is discarded, not used to narrow alpha/beta.** `negamax()`'s probe-handling only returns early on an Exact hit, or a Lower/Upper bound that already fails high/low against the CALLER's actual, untouched alpha/beta — it deliberately does not tighten alpha/beta from a non-cutoff-triggering bound before falling through to a normal search. **Rationale:** narrowing is a real, standard, strictly-more-aggressive optimization, but it means the search that follows runs against a DIFFERENT (narrower) window than the caller asked for, which complicates how that node's own eventual `store()` should classify its Exact/Lower/Upper result (classification needs to be relative to whichever window was actually searched, not the caller's original one, or the stored bound is wrong the next time it's probed with a wider window). Skipping narrowing keeps `alpha_orig`/`beta` in the end-of-function classification unambiguously equal to what was passed in. Revisit once profiling on real games shows this costs meaningful nodes — deferred, not decided-against.
4. **Root position itself gets no TT probe/store this session.** `search_root()`'s move loop is unchanged structurally (still calls `negamax()` directly per child, no TT interaction of its own). Without move ordering yet to consume a TT-move hint, root-level TT interaction has no payoff to justify its own complexity/bug-surface today — it's naturally where the next ROADMAP.md item (move ordering, TT move first) will add it.

**Sub-decisions on implementation details:** empty-slot sentinel is `key == 0` (a real position hashing to exactly 0 is astronomically unlikely with the existing well-seeded 64-bit Zobrist PRNG — CPW-documented standard practice, not new to this entry). Replacement order within a bucket: same-key entries refresh only if the new store is at least as deep or the old one is stale (an older generation); otherwise, among other positions' entries, prefer an empty slot, then any older-generation entry regardless of depth, then the shallowest same-generation entry (CPW "Replacement Strategy," age-then-depth-preferred). `prefetch()` is portable: `__builtin_prefetch` on GCC/Clang (covers both Linux and the Apple Silicon macOS CI runner), `_mm_prefetch` on MSVC (the Windows CI job), a no-op otherwise. A new `board::Move::from_raw(uint16_t)` factory was added to `move.h` (small, additive, not a rewrite of existing logic) so `probe()` can reconstruct a `Move` from its packed TT storage — `Move` had no prior way to go from raw bits back to an object.

**Verification:** Real build+test cycle against a freshly `codeload`-fetched `main` with all six changed/new files overlaid in (`search/tt.h`, `search/tt.cpp`, `search/search.h`, `search/search.cpp`, `board/move.h`, both `CMakeLists.txt` files, `tests/tt_tests.cpp`). Release: zero warnings, **all 136 `ctest` cases passed** (122 prior + 14 new TT-specific tests), including every existing `search_tests.cpp` case unchanged — critically the exact depth-1 node-count test and the depth-2 mate-in-1 test, confirming the TT integration didn't alter search correctness or the PVS session's carefully-preserved node-count invariant. Debug (ASan+UBSan): zero warnings; all 14 `tt_tests.cpp` cases ran to completion clean (no sanitizer findings on the prefetch/pointer/bit-packing code, which is exactly where ASan/UBSan earn their keep); 6 of 14 `search_tests.cpp` cases (including the exact-node-count and mate-in-1 tests) completed clean before hitting this sandbox's already-documented (2026-08-14 entry) per-test ASan launch-overhead ceiling on the command timeout — not a new issue, Release's full green run remains authoritative.

**Alternatives considered:**
- A persistent global TT now, accepting that it'd make cross-call test comparisons order-dependent and require loosening those tests — rejected; "never rewrite completed/green code unless a bug is confirmed" and these tests aren't bugged, the TT's lifetime scoping is what should adapt to them for now, not the reverse.
- Storing a truncated (e.g. 32-bit) verification key instead of the full 64-bit Zobrist hash, freeing bytes for other fields — rejected for now; the full key fits cleanly in 16 bytes alongside everything else needed (see TTEntry layout) with zero waste after natural 8-byte alignment, so there was no real space pressure motivating the added collision-probability complexity a truncated key introduces.
- Narrowing alpha/beta from a non-cutoff-triggering probe bound — rejected for this pass (see sub-decision 3 above); a real future optimization, not ruled out.
- Storing terminal (checkmate/stalemate) nodes in the TT too — rejected for now; movegen already pays the cost of detecting terminality regardless, so the marginal caching benefit is small, and skipping it keeps `negamax()`'s terminal branch exactly as simple as it was pre-TT.

## 2026-08-15 (2) — Release & Packaging Infrastructure: scope, tagging, and WASM JS surface (planned, not yet implemented)

**Decision:** A GitHub Release was requested to be published automatically every time all 6 CI matrix jobs go green, carrying native binaries plus a WASM build with JS bindings plus a standalone minified JS bundle. Three scoping questions were raised and answered, recorded here since they're real architectural commitments even though implementation is deferred to a later session:

1. **Tagging:** a single rolling `latest` tag/release, overwritten on every green run — not per-run version tags and not manually-bumped semver. Simplest to implement and matches the goal of always surfacing the current best build, at the cost of no historical release archive (the commit SHA in the release body is the only "which build is this" record; git history itself remains the real archive).
2. **WASM JS surface:** the WASM build exposes a full UCI loop over stdin/stdout (via Emscripten's stdin/stdout support), so Node (or any JS host) drives it exactly like a native UCI engine subprocess — not a bespoke Embind API (`init()`/`setPosition()`/`go()`/etc.). This means zero new API surface to design, document, version, or keep in sync with UCI itself; the tradeoff is it's a worse fit for e.g. a browser chessboard UI that would want direct function calls rather than piping UCI text through stdin, but that's not the stated use case here.
3. **Scope/scheduling:** tracked as a new **parallel-track ROADMAP.md section** ("Release & Packaging Infrastructure"), explicitly *not* phase-gated and *not* inserted as a Phase 3 interrupt — Session 10's transposition table work continues immediately after this entry, and the release-infra section can be picked up in any future session independent of phase order.

**Rationale:** all three were explicit choices made among presented alternatives (rolling tag vs. timestamped tag vs. manual semver; stdin/stdout UCI vs. Embind API vs. minimal-now; land-now vs. after-TT vs. parallel-track), each a real design commitment worth getting right the first time rather than discovering mid-implementation, per the "flag test/scope risk explicitly" and "never skip/reorder a phase without approval" working rules — this is exactly that kind of reorder, made with explicit sign-off. This entry itself is the documented approval trail.

**Sub-decisions made while scoping this out (not asked directly, but necessary to write a concrete ROADMAP.md task list):** publish only the 3 Release-config native binaries as release assets; the 3 Debug (ASan/UBSan) matrix jobs remain gate-only (their being green is required for the release job's `needs:`, but they don't produce a shipped artifact) since a sanitizer-instrumented binary isn't something an end user should run. The standalone `nightwing.min.js` inlines the wasm binary as base64 rather than shipping a separate `.wasm` file, so it's genuinely drop-in with no asset-path configuration — that's what "standalone" was taken to mean.

**Alternatives considered:**
- Per-run timestamped tags (`v-YYYYMMDD-<sha>`) — rejected (the chosen alternative was the rolling tag); would give a real release history/archive at the cost of every consumer needing to track which tag is "current."
- Embind-based explicit JS API — rejected for now (the chosen alternative was the stdin/stdout UCI surface); more ergonomic for a future browser UI, but a second API surface to design and keep in sync with UCI, for no stated current use case.
- Landing this immediately, ahead of the transposition table — rejected (the chosen alternative was the parallel-track section); tracked as a parallel section instead so TT work isn't interrupted mid-Phase-3.

**Status:** Planned only. No `ci.yml`, CMake, or wasm code has been written yet — see the new "Release & Packaging Infrastructure" section in ROADMAP.md for the concrete task breakdown when this is picked up.
